Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Introduction  
	  The text is a translation of Martin Luther's "Bondage of the Will" into English.
	  The translation was done by Edward Vaughan in 1823.
    Translation and Edition  
	  The translation is based on the original Latin text.
	  The edition has five parts with numerous sections in each part.
	  The translator, Vaughan, aimed to simplify the text and make it more readable for a modern audience.
    Vaughan's Approach  
	  Vaughan was a Calvinist and brought a biased perspective to his notes on Luther's work.
	  He was from Cambridge and avoided the Oxford Movement's Romanizing influence.
	  Vaughan's notes are often pedantic and condescending, reflecting his Cambridge background.
    Luther's Style  
	  Luther's writing style was direct and forceful, with many dashes and parentheticals.
	  He wrote as he spoke, with a lot of informal language and syntax.
    Vaughan's Notes  
	  Vaughan's notes are a mix of critique, explanation, and commentary on Luther's work.
	  He is overly critical of Luther at times, slipping into commentary on doctrine.
	  Vaughan's notes identify points where Luther's doctrine differs from Calvinist and later Reformed doctrine.
    Language and Grammar  
	  Luther's responses to Erasmus were often focused on language and grammar.
	  He argued that grammar-masters and street boys knew that imperative verbs express what ought to be done.
    Luther's Biographical Sketch  
	  Vaughan's biographical sketch of Luther is pithy, informative, and inspiring.
	  It highlights Luther's exceptional gifting by God to fight for God's grace and authority.
    Edition's Features  
	  The edition includes a preface and notes by Vaughan.
	  The notes are often lengthy and have been converted to endnotes to avoid distracting the reader.
	  The edition has been reformatted and annotated by William H. Gross.
    Purpose and Audience  
	  The edition aims to provide a worthwhile addition to existing translations of Luther's work.
	  It is intended for readers who want to understand Luther's classic work in a more readable format.
    Availability and Limitations  
	  The edition is based on a 19th-century translation and may not reflect modern scholarship.
	  An English translation of Erasmus' "Diatribe" is not currently available online, which is a limitation of the edition.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  I. Introduction  

  Reasons for the work:
	  To defend Luther's views on free will
	  To critique Erasmus' views on free will
  Postscript on Plato and Augustine:
	  Luther's views on free will are influenced by Plato and Augustine

  II. Erasmus' Preface Reviewed  

  Erasmus' assertions defended:
	  Erasmus argues that free will is a necessary subject for true piety
	  Erasmus claims that God's foreknowledge is not absolute
  Erasmus shown to be a skeptic:
	  Erasmus doubts the importance of the question of free will
	  Erasmus is accused of being inconsistent in his views

  III. Erasmus' Theology Exposed  

  Erasmus' Christianity:
	  Luther accuses Erasmus of having a flawed understanding of Christianity
  Erasmus' theology exposed by similes:
	  Luther uses similes to illustrate the absurdity of Erasmus' views
  Absolute necessity of the subject of free will:
	  Luther argues that free will is essential for true piety

  IV. Objections to Erasmus' Views  

  Objection to the term "necessity" admitted:
	  Luther acknowledges that the term "necessity" can be problematic
  Universal prevalence of this persuasion:
	  Luther argues that many people share his views on free will
  Exceeding temerity and mischievousness of Erasmus' pretended moderation:
	  Luther accuses Erasmus of being hypocritical and misleading

  V. Luther's Response  

  All Scripture truth may be published safely:
	  Luther argues that all Scripture truth can be safely published
  Argument "some truths should not be published" is either inconsistent with Erasmus' act or out of place:
	  Luther accuses Erasmus of being inconsistent in his views

  VI. Erasmus' Examples  

  Erasmus' three examples of truths "not to be published":
	  Luther critiques Erasmus' examples and argues that they are not relevant
  Erasmus neither understands nor feels the vast importance of the question:
	  Luther accuses Erasmus of being ignorant of the importance of the question

  VII. The Church and Scripture  

  Peace of the world disturbed is no argument against a dogma:
	  Luther argues that the disturbance of the peace is not a reason to reject a dogma
  Doubts whether the dogma of free confession is scriptural:
	  Luther questions whether the dogma of free confession is supported by Scripture

  VIII. The Fathers and Scripture  

  The Fathers are not to be set on a level with Christ:
	  Luther argues that the Fathers are not equal to Christ
  Their decisions have no authority but from the word:
	  Luther argues that the decisions of the Fathers are only authoritative if they are supported by Scripture

  IX. Luther's Conclusion  

  Luther concludes his review of Erasmus' Preface by reducing him to a dilemma:
	  Luther argues that Erasmus is trapped by his own contradictions
  Luther claims victory but will proceed:
	  Luther announces that he will continue to critique Erasmus' views

  X. Luther's Confutation of Erasmus' Testimonies  

  Erasmus' definition of free will examined:
	  Luther critiques Erasmus' definition of free will
  Inferences from Erasmus' definition:
	  Luther argues that Erasmus' definition leads to absurd conclusions
  Luther claims that Erasmus' testimonies are not sufficient to support his views:
	  Luther argues that Erasmus' testimonies are not convincing evidence for his views

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Part I: Luther's Response to Erasmus  

    Sect. 1-5: Luther's Definition of Free Will  
	  Luther defines free will as a power to choose between good and evil
	  He argues that Erasmus' definition is inconsistent with his own
    Sect. 6-10: Erasmus' Opinion on Free Will  
	  Luther examines Erasmus' opinion on free will and finds it wanting
	  He argues that Erasmus' opinion is not supported by Scripture
    Sect. 11-15: Luther's Critique of Ecclesiasticus  
	  Luther critiques Erasmus' use of Ecclesiasticus to support free will
	  He argues that Ecclesiasticus does not support free will, but rather God's sovereignty
    Sect. 16-20: Luther's Examination of Scripture  
	  Luther examines various Scripture passages to argue against free will
	  He argues that Scripture teaches God's sovereignty and not human free will
    Sect. 21-25: Luther's Response to Objections  
	  Luther responds to objections from Erasmus and others
	  He argues that Scripture teaches God's sovereignty and not human free will

  Part II: Luther's Defense of Certain Testimonies Against Free Will  

    Sect. 26-30: Luther's Defense of Moses' Testimonies  
	  Luther defends Moses' testimonies against Erasmus' tropes
	  He argues that Moses' testimonies teach God's sovereignty and not human free will
    Sect. 31-35: Luther's Examination of Erasmus' Tropes  
	  Luther examines Erasmus' tropes and finds them wanting
	  He argues that Erasmus' tropes make nonsense of Scripture
    Sect. 36-40: Luther's Response to Diatribe's Objections  
	  Luther responds to objections from Diatribe
	  He argues that Diatribe's objections are based on a misunderstanding of Scripture
    Sect. 41-45: Luther's Examination of Paul's Testimonies  
	  Luther examines Paul's testimonies and finds them to support God's sovereignty
	  He argues that Paul's testimonies teach God's sovereignty and not human free will

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Introduction to the Debate  
	  The author is responding to Luther's views on salvation and free will
	  The author argues that Luther's views are inconsistent and absurd
    Scripture and Interpretation  
	  Luther maintains the perfect consistency of Scripture, but this is not the case
	  The author argues that Scripture must be understood with qualifications
    Luther's Views on God's Help  
	  Luther argues that God's help is not necessary for salvation
	  The author counters that this view is absurd and contradicts Scripture
    Luther's Views on Heathen Virtue  
	  Luther argues that heathen virtue is God's abhorrence
	  The author argues that this view is incorrect and contradicts Scripture
    Luther's Views on Free Will  
	  Luther argues that free will is necessary for salvation
	  The author counters that this view is incorrect and contradicts Scripture
    Paul's Views on Free Will  
	  Paul argues that free will is not necessary for salvation
	  The author argues that Paul's views are consistent and supported by Scripture
    Luther's Defense of His Views  
	  Luther argues that his views are consistent and supported by Scripture
	  The author counters that Luther's views are inconsistent and absurd
    The Consequences of Luther's Views  
	  Luther's views lead to the conclusion that salvation is not necessary
	  The author argues that this view is absurd and contradicts Scripture
    The Authority of the Ancients  
	  Luther argues that the authority of the ancients is abused
	  The author argues that this view is incorrect and contradicts Erasmus
    Conclusion  
	  The author concludes that Luther's views on free will are incorrect and absurd
	  The author argues that Paul's views on free will are consistent and supported by Scripture

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Early Life  
	  Resided in a monastery
	  Met a Latin Bible in the library, discovering that large portions of the Scriptures were withheld from the people
	  Was comforted by an elder brother who directed his attention to the article "I believe in the remission of sins"

•   Influence of Staupitius  
	  Staupitius suggested that Luther did not know how useful and necessary his trial might be
	  Luther believed that God did not exercise him for nothing and that he would one day be employed as a servant for great purposes

•   Luther's Study of Scripture  
	  Accidentally met a Latin Bible in the library
	  Was greatly comforted by an elder brother who directed his attention to the article "I believe in the remission of sins"
	  Began to study the Scriptures and Augustine's works with incredible ardor

•   Luther's Education and Career  
	  Received holy orders in 1507
	  Was called to the Professorship of Divinity at Wittenberg in 1508
	  Gave Luther an opportunity to verify his own forebodings concerning him

•   Luther's Connection with Frederick of Saxony  
	  Was tenderly anxious for the credit and success of his infant seminary
	  Luther more than fulfilled his expectations as a teacher of philosophy and public minister

•   Luther's Experiences in Rome  
	  Was dispatched to Rome on some important business of his order in 1510
	  Performed his duties well and received the distinction of a doctor's degree upon his return
	  Had opportunities to notice the spirit with which religious worship was conducted in Rome

•   Luther's Public Exposition of the Psalms and Romans  
	  Entered upon a public exposition of the Psalms and Paul's Epistle to the Romans
	  Studied Greek and Hebrew with great diligence
	  Improved his taste and enlarged his erudition by availing himself of the philological labors of Erasmus

•   Luther's Rejection of Error  
	  Rejected the corruptive yoke of Aristotle and the Schoolmen
	  Sought and found his peace in erecting a scriptural theology upon the ruins of heathenized Christianity
	  The true light beamed very gradually upon his mind: from suspecting error, he became convinced that it was there

•   Luther's Study of Indulgences  
	  Was employed with great contention of mind in studying, ruminating, teaching, and preaching
	  Had not taken up the subject of indulgences as a speculation; he did not know the real nature, grounds, ingredients, or ramifications of the evil
	  As a confessor, he had to deal with acknowledgments of sin; as a priest, he had to deal with the sale of indulgences

•   Luther's Growing Conviction  
	  Was constrained to reject error, step by step, into truth
	  His attention was in a way compelled to the subject of indulgences
	  He began to suspect that the indulgence system was a corrupt and evil practice

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Indulgence Scandal  
	  The Pope, Leo X, sold indulgences to raise funds for a new cathedral
	  The indulgences were seen as a way to buy forgiveness for sins
	  The practice was widespread and corrupt, with prices varying depending on the buyer

•   Luther's Opposition  
	  Martin Luther, a monk, opposed the indulgence trade
	  He believed it was a corrupt practice that undermined the true meaning of Christianity
	  Luther refused to absolve those who had bought indulgences

•   Tetzel's Response  
	  Johann Tetzel, a Dominican friar, defended the indulgence trade
	  He claimed that he had saved more souls from hell through his indulgences than St. Peter had converted to Christianity through his preaching

•   Luther's Publication  
	  Luther published his 95 theses, a list of objections to the indulgence trade
	  The theses were spread throughout Germany and caused a stir
	  Tetzel responded with 106 theses of his own

•   The Dispute Escalates  
	  Luther's theses were seen as a challenge to the Pope's authority
	  Luther was summoned to Rome to answer for his heresy
	  He was condemned as an incorrigible heretic before he even arrived in Rome

•   The Hearing at Augsburg  
	  Luther was given a hearing at Augsburg, but it was not a fair trial
	  He was forced to admit his crime and recant his views
	  Luther refused to back down, but was ultimately silenced

•   Luther's Appeal  
	  Luther appealed the decision to the Pope, but it was ignored
	  He wrote a letter to the Pope, known as the "Revoco" letter, which was a formal appeal
	  The letter was a last-ditch effort to appeal the decision and restore his reputation

•   The Consequences  
	  Luther's opposition to the indulgence trade had far-reaching consequences
	  It led to a wider critique of the Catholic Church and its practices
	  It ultimately contributed to the Protestant Reformation and the split between Catholics and Protestants

•   Luther's Stand  
	  Luther stood firm in his opposition to the indulgence trade
	  He believed that scripture was the ultimate authority, not the Pope or the Church
	  He was willing to challenge the Church's authority and face the consequences

•   The Legacy  
	  Luther's opposition to the indulgence trade marked a turning point in history
	  It led to a wider critique of the Catholic Church and its practices
	  It ultimately contributed to the Protestant Reformation and the modern era of Christianity.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Language  
	  Luther used the language of the Apostles to argue that obedience to God is more important than obedience to men.
	  He quoted "We ought to obey God rather than men" (Acts 5:29).

•   Cajeta's Failure  
	  Cajeta, a papal counselor, failed to secure a favorable outcome for Luther at the Augsburg negotiations.
	  He was criticized for being too severe and inflexible.

•   Luther's Considerations  
	  Luther considered leaving Germany to retire in France, but the elector forbade him.
	  He made an appeal to the Pope, which was followed by a new bull in favor of indulgences.

•   New Bull  
	  The new bull confirmed all the ancient abuses and did not mention Luther's name.
	  Luther, still clinging to the Pope's authority, saw this as an opportunity to make his retreat impossible.

•   Electoral Support  
	  The death of Emperor Maximilian increased the elector's power to protect Luther.
	  The elector took a more lenient approach to Luther, seeing him as a person of great vigor and popular opinion.

•   Leipzig Disputations  
	  The famous disputations at Leipzig took place in July 1519.
	  Luther was allowed to participate, despite being refused safe conduct as a disputant.

•   Eckius's Role  
	  Eckius, Luther's former friend, came to earn laurels for himself and the Papacy.
	  He defended papal supremacy, but on inferior grounds.

•   Melanchthon's Approval  
	  Melanchthon, who had been acquainted with Luther's lectures, was impressed by his arguments at Leipzig.
	  He became a coadjutor and approved of Luther's doctrine.

•   Papal Supremacy  
	  The question of papal supremacy was first brought into debate at Leipzig.
	  Eckius maintained the Pope's supremacy, but on grounds that Luther disputed.

•   Luther's Mind-Opening  
	  Luther showed a mind open to truth, even if he was not yet fully enlightened.
	  He used different materials and forms of defense, even on important topics like justification and free will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Early Understanding of Christianity  
	  Luther had only recently learned about justification and salvation through faith in Christ
	  He was still learning about the authority of the Pope and the papal system

•   Luther's Debate and Publication of Propositions  
	  Luther calmly reviewed and published his 13 propositions with concise explanations and proofs
	  He based his conclusions on Scripture and ecclesiastical history

•   The Impact of Luther's Teachings  
	  Luther's teachings were the seed-bed of the reviving church
	  The people heard and read his teachings, leading to a growing movement

•   Luther's View of the Pope  
	  Luther believed the Pope was the real Antichrist
	  He saw the Pope's actions and decrees as consistent with the description of Antichrist in the Bible

•   Luther's Second Letter to the Pope  
	  Luther was advised to write a second letter to the Pope in 1520
	  He found it difficult to write the letter with integrity, given his strong views on the Pope

•   Luther's Address to the Pope  
	  Luther's tone in his address to the Pope was altered from his previous letter
	  He declared many of the Pope's abominations and refused to recant

•   Luther's Critique of the Pope's Flatterers  
	  Luther warned the Pope against the big swelling words used by his flatterers
	  He expressed his desire for frankness and less flattery

•   Luther's Doubts and Intentions  
	  Luther had doubts about the Pope's true nature
	  He intended to conciliate the Pope, but his mind was not yet fully made up

•   The Issuance of the Damnatory Bull  
	  The Conclave issued a damnatory bull against Luther on June 15, 1520
	  The bull declared 41 of Luther's propositions to be heretical and commanded his books to be burned

•   Luther's Response to the Bull  
	  Luther was now prepared to receive the judgment of the Lord on the papal usurpation
	  He was willing to suffer martyrdom for the truth, if called to that outcome

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Response to the Bull  
	  Luther published two tracts in response to the bull
	  He treated the bull ironically, questioning its authenticity
	  He called on the emperor and Christian princes to defend the church against the Papists

•   Luther's Defense of Scripture  
	  Luther defended the authority of Scripture in one of the tracts
	  He called on everyone to study Scripture without deferring to human explanations
	  He argued that Scripture should be the ultimate authority in matters of faith

•   Luther's Actions  
	  Luther publicly burned the bull, along with papal decrees and canon law
	  He argued that the Pope was a separatist, not a representative of Christ
	  He claimed that the bull was an illegal instrument and should be rejected

•   Luther's Publication of Articles  
	  Luther published 30 articles from the books he had burned
	  He included a short comment with each article, appealing to the public to judge their validity
	  The two final articles were particularly contentious, arguing for the Pope's authority and the derivation of Scripture's authority from the Pope

•   Luther's Expectations  
	  Luther expected his cause to be just, and his actions to be bold and necessary
	  He believed that he was either a subject to be burned or a vindicator of the oppressed
	  He saw himself as a champion of the cause of Christ, using the "bloodless sword of the Spirit"

•   Luther's Priorities  
	  Luther's main expectation was from the word of God, simply and intelligibly set forth
	  He added practical and experimental treatises to his publications, but the expounded word was his stay
	  He labored in the Epistle to the Galatians, which he first published in 1519 and revised and expanded in 1635

•   Luther's View of Scripture  
	  Luther believed that the doctrine of faith must be taught in its purity
	  He argued that justification comes through faith in Christ, not through works of the law
	  He saw this doctrine as the most important aspect of the Christian faith

•   Luther's Historical Significance  
	  Luther's work was seen as a powerful means of reviving the light of Scripture in the 16th century
	  His treatise on the Epistle to the Galatians was considered a masterpiece of spiritual writing
	  His work was expected to continue to have an impact on future generations, under the blessing of God

•   Luther's Audience  
	  Luther's publications were intended for serious, humble, and contrite spirits
	  He believed that only such individuals would be able to appreciate the importance of the doctrine of justification
	  He saw himself as a teacher of the human heart, and knew its native depravity

•   Luther's Legacy  
	  Luther's work was seen as a means of reviving the light of Scripture in a dark age
	  His doctrine of justification was considered a great evangelical tenet, one that had been obscured for centuries
	  His legacy was expected to continue to inspire future generations, under the blessing of God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Faith  
	  Luther believes that faith unites him with Christ, making them one person.
	  This faith is not just a feeling, but a powerful force that destroys foolish ideas and imagination.

•   Union with Christ  
	  Luther believes that he and Christ are joined together as one flesh and one bone.
	  This union is stronger than the relationship between husband and wife.

•   Luther's Critics  
	  Luther had many critics who attacked his ideas and writings.
	  He responded to them with clear, argumentative, and decisive language.

•   Luther's Language  
	  Luther's language was a reflection of his faith and his purpose.
	  He used strong words to clear the ground, pull up weeds, and smooth the roads.

•   Luther's Personality  
	  Luther was a rough and contentious person who was not afraid to speak his mind.
	  He was more disposed to quarrel with his own vanity than with his opponents' petulance.

•   Charles V's Obligations  
	  Charles V owed Frederick protection for Luther to a certain extent.
	  He did not care for Luther's opinions, but only for the political implications.

•   Luther's Appearance at Worms  
	  Luther was summoned to appear at Worms to defend his views.
	  He was given a safe conduct and several princes countersigned it.

•   Luther's Defense  
	  Luther's defense at Worms has been criticized for being too tame and inexplicit.
	  He later spoke of his boldness and questioned whether he should have been so bold.

•   God's Wisdom  
	  Luther believed that God gave him the wisdom and language he needed for the occasion.
	  He quoted Luke 21:15, "I will give you a mouth and wisdom which all your adversaries shall not be able to challenge or resist."

•   Luther's Reflections  
	  Luther reflected on his experience at Worms and felt that he had not been bold enough.
	  He gave God the glory for his courage and wisdom.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Humility  
	  Luther acknowledges his moderation at the Diet of Worms
	  He expresses weariness of being commended for his moderation
	  He maintains his principle of free interpretation of Scripture

•   Luther's Stance  
	  He refuses to retract his opinions unless convinced by Scripture
	  He believes his conscience is bound to abide by the word of God
	  He is willing to lose his life rather than give up his convictions

•   Secret Persuasions  
	  Many attempts were made to persuade Luther in secret
	  He refused to give up his convictions, even when faced with threats

•   The Edict  
	  Luther received an order to quit Worms after three hours of his last interview with the Elector Archbishop of Treves
	  He was not permitted to preach on his way home
	  A sanginary edict was smuggled through the diet, condemning Luther as a heretic

•   The Edict's Contents  
	  The edict stated that it was the duty of the emperor to protect religion and extinguish heresies
	  It related the pains taken to bring Luther back to repentance
	  It condemned Luther in the strongest terms, declaring him excommunicated and a notorious heretic

•   The Emperor's Decree  
	  The emperor forbade all persons from receiving, maintaining, or protecting Luther
	  He ordered Luther to be proceeded against after the 21-day safe conduct period
	  He directed the same punishment to be inflicted on all Luther's adherents or favourers

•   The Edict's Consequences  
	  The edict was never executed due to the emperor's busy and entangled politics
	  A diet of the empire held at Nuremberg agreed to a conclusion that Luther considered an abrogation of the edict
	  A second diet held at the same place concurred in a similar recess

•   Luther's Reaction  
	  Luther was divided between hope and fear, alternately elated and depressed
	  He answered the divided opinions between hope and fear during some succeeding years

•   The Diet of Spires  
	  The diet of Spires, held in 1526, terminated favourably for Luther
	  The Pope's wrath was only deferred, not eliminated

•   The Emperor's Politics  
	  The emperor was too busy and entangled in politics to take vengeance for the Pope
	  He was too dependent and needy to take action against Luther

•   The Pope's Wrath  
	  The Pope's wrath was only deferred, not eliminated
	  Luther continued to face opposition and persecution from the Catholic Church

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Diet of Spires (1529)  
	  A second diet was held at Spires to establish the Edict of Worms
	  The violence with which it was conducted led to a protest by Lutheran states and princes
	  This protest led to the formation of the Smalcald League

•   The Smalcald League  
	  A defensive league formed by Protestant states and princes
	  The league was formed in response to the Edict of Worms
	  The league was confirmed by the decree of Augsburg in 1530

•   The Failure of Moderate Doctrine  
	  The most moderate expressions of doctrine had no conciliatory effect
	  Force was prepared and must be repelled by military combination

•   The Defeat of the Smalcald League  
	  The league was defeated by the emperor in 1547
	  The emperor took two great Protestant leaders captive and made a spectacle of them
	  The emperor established his Interim and slew Protestant witnesses

•   The Resurrection of the Witnesses  
	  The Protestant witnesses were seen standing on their feet again in Germany
	  This was seen as a miraculous event
	  The treacherous and intriguing Maurice was made the instrument of bringing deliverance to the Protestants

•   The Treaty of Passau  
	  The treaty of Passau legalized Protestantism and secured the rights of the revived witnesses
	  This treaty was seen as a miraculous event

•   The Death of Luther  
	  Luther died of a rapid sickness and premature death in 1546
	  He was 63 years old at the time of his death
	  Fatigue and anxiety had impaired his bodily frame

•   Luther's Hiding Place  
	  Luther was hidden in the castle of Wartburg by Frederick
	  He was fed and nourished at Frederick's expense for 10 months
	  He saw many visions of the Almighty during his time in hiding

•   Luther's Activities in Hiding  
	  Luther wrote many tracts and improved his knowledge of Greek and Hebrew
	  He studied diligently for his projected translation of the Scriptures
	  He actually accomplished his German version of the New Testament

•   Luther's Works  
	  His answer to Latomus' defence of the Louvain divines was his most elaborate work
	  It was a confutation that was replete with solid learning and sound divinity
	  It was considered one of his greatest works, even if he had never published anything else in his life.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Appointment to Presidency  
	  Luther is appointed to the presidency of the College of Wittemberg
	  He receives a congratulatory letter from a friend, expressing divine indignation at the visible church
	  The friend hints at Luther's expected duties as president

•   Luther's Response to Criticism  
	  Luther responds to criticism of his past submission to the Pope
	  He declares his grief and thankfulness to Jesus Christ for insight into Scripture
	  He affirms his conviction that the Pope is Antichrist

•   The Nature of Sin  
	  Luther argues that the word "sin" is used in its true and proper sense in Scripture
	  He rejects the idea that human actions can be sinless and perfect
	  He challenges his critics to produce a man who can say his work is without sin

•   Scriptural Authority  
	  Luther argues that all doctrines must be proved by Scripture
	  He rejects the authority of human tradition and the opinions of the Fathers
	  He cites 1 Thessalonians 5:21 as a scriptural basis for his argument

•   Augustine's Agreement  
	  Luther notes that Augustine is on his side of the question regarding the nature of sin
	  He argues that Augustine's views are in line with Scripture

•   Critique of Latomus  
	  Luther critiques Latomus for his views on the nature of sin
	  He argues that Latomus' views lead to a depreciation of the knowledge of Christ and the spirit of thankfulness to God

•   The Consequences of Latomus' Views  
	  Luther argues that Latomus' views make men presumptuous and secure in their vices
	  He argues that Latomus' views lead to a loss of sight of the mystery of Christ and the conversion of sinners

•   Luther's Defense  
	  Luther defends his views on the nature of sin as being in line with Scripture
	  He argues that his views are not based on human tradition or opinion

•   The Importance of the Controversy  
	  Luther argues that the matter of the controversy is of great importance
	  He affirms that the glory of God, the necessity of Christ's grace, and real humility are at stake

•   Luther's Conclusion  
	  Luther concludes that his views on the nature of sin are based on Scripture and reason
	  He affirms that he has no apology for his arguments and extracts from his treatise.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Defense  
	  Luther defends himself against accusations of treating Thomas Aquinas and others unfairly
	  He claims that their writings have harmed his own mind and advises young students to avoid scholastic theology and philosophy

•   Luther's Doubts about Thomas Aquinas  
	  Luther expresses doubts about Thomas Aquinas' character and morality
	  He believes that Aquinas held many heretical opinions and was a major contributor to the spread of Aristotle's destructive doctrines

•   The Value of Luther's Work  
	  Luther's work is valuable, but it cannot be compared to his truly Herculean labor in translating the Bible
	  He was interrupted from this labor by the task of responding to Latomus' publication

•   The Labor of Translating the Bible  
	  Luther grudgingly spent time reading and answering Latomus' publication, which he considered worthless
	  He was employed in translating the Epistles and Gospels into the vernacular language

•   The Importance of the Bible  
	  Luther notes that people often forget the labor that went into furnishing them with a Bible in their native language
	  He expresses gratitude for the freedom to read and handle the Bible with their own eyes and hands

•   The Circulation of the Bible  
	  Luther observes that the Bible is now circulating in more than 140 languages and dialects
	  He notes that this is a rare thing, and that many people are still without access to the sacred volume

•   Luther's Vision for the Bible  
	  Luther foresaw the important consequences of a fair translation of the Bible in the German language
	  He believed that it would shake the pillars of ecclesiastical despotism and spread knowledge of pure Christian doctrine

•   The Challenge of Translating the Bible  
	  Luther notes that translating the Bible required the silence and seclusion of his Patmos for its origin and commencement
	  He realized that he needed larger resources than he possessed there to complete the task

•   Luther's Request for Help  
	  Luther asks for the help of his friends at Wittemberg to assist him in translating the Bible
	  He proposes that they work together in secret to complete the task

•   The Completion of the Bible  
	  Luther's arduous task was eventually accomplished: the New Testament was published in 1522, and the Old Testament was completed in 1530
	  He was assisted by the labor and advice of several friends, including Jonas and Melancthon

•   The Impact of the Bible  
	  The effects of Luther's labor were soon felt in Germany: immense numbers of people now read the Bible in their own language and saw the just foundations of the Lutheran doctrine
	  The Papists cried out and burned the Bible, but Luther's work had a profound impact on the spread of Christian doctrine.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Luther's Anticipation of the Destruction of the Papacy  
	  Luther believed that the papacy would be destroyed through the knowledge of the Book (Scriptures)
	  He predicted that the Scriptures would be understood by all, and everyone would speak and preach against the papal tyranny
    The Role of the Scriptures in Destroying the Papacy  
	  Luther believed that the true Christian way to destroy the papacy was through the dissemination of the Scriptures
	  He encouraged others to exert every nerve to promote this end
    The Spreading of the Scriptures  
	  In Luther's time, individuals and groups were zealous for the dissemination of the Scriptures in their native languages
	  One such individual had distributed over 300,000 copies of a German version of Luther's translation
    Luther's Translation of the Scriptures  
	  Luther's translation was considered elegant and perspicuous, and was preferred to any other scriptural publication known to the populace
	  It was probable that the translation had many defects, but was generally faithful and sound
    The Papistical Plagiarist Emser  
	  Emser attempted to translate and rival Luther's work, but his "correct" translation was little more than a transcript of Luther's with some alterations
	  Emser's translation was used to promote the Roman tenets, and Luther's work was read under Emser's name
    Luther's Imprisonment and Solitude  
	  Luther was detained in solitude for ten months, during which time he was impatient and longed for action
	  He wrote to Melancthon, expressing his desire to return to his public station in Wittenberg
    The State of Wittenberg  
	  Melancthon lacked spirit and vigor, and the elector lacked boldness and decision
	  Carolstadt had become tumultuous, and the flock was without a shepherd
    Luther's Return to Wittenberg  
	  Luther determined to risk his life and safety by returning to Wittenberg
	  He wrote a noble letter to the elector, expressing his motives and expectations, and delivering the elector from all responsibility for his safety
    Luther's Confidence in Divine Protection  
	  Luther pursued his journey with no other safeguard than divine protection
	  He believed that he was under a more powerful protection than any afforded by the elector of Saxony
    Luther's Decision to Return to Wittenberg  
	  Luther decided to return to Wittenberg, despite the risks, and to act first and then apologize
	  He believed that he was doing God's will, and that the Gospels would not suffer in any degree.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Cause  
	  Is a cause that does not require human aid
	  God will take care of it without human intervention
	  Luther would not return to Wittenberg if he knew the Elector intended to defend him by force

•   Luther's Faith  
	  He had the firmest confidence in God
	  He believed that God would direct the cause and that men should stand still and wait for the event
	  He declared that he would cast his die in God at Wartburg and declared it at Borna

•   Luther's Return to Wittenberg  
	  His return was a healing, confidence, and peace to his scattered and agitated flock
	  He was preserved for the church for 24 years after his return
	  He had to build, protect, extend, uphold, and perpetuate his infant establishment

•   Challenges Faced by Luther  
	  He had to provide against the rapacity of the secular arm without making ecclesiastics rich
	  He had to obtain learned instructors for the people without feeding "hives of drones"
	  He had to make the untaught into teachers and abolish pomp without violating decency

•   Luther's Use of the Press  
	  The press was his great weapon of warfare and culture
	  His publications extended to a vast variety of subjects
	  He had thought and knowledge, matter and weight, for all

•   Luther's Enemies  
	  His enemies were not only the Eckiuses and Aleanders, but also the Elector's caution, his coadjutors' timidity, and the people's fleshly heat
	  His native obstinacy was also an enemy to him

•   Luther's Character  
	  He was magnanimous, capacious, abstinent, studious, disinterested, and intrepid
	  He feared God and feared none else
	  He had been made to drink deep into the knowledge of his own wickedness, accountability, lostness, and impotency

•   Luther's Sensibility of Conscience  
	  He had a deep sense of conscience that prepared him for a trembling reception of the divine word
	  He was terrified by the thought of God's judgment and mercy
	  He was stirred up to blasphemy by the terror of God's word, but the Lord had pity on him and opened his eyes

•   Luther's Understanding of the Righteousness of God  
	  He initially thought that the righteousness of God was God's own essential righteousness, which renders Him the hater and punisher of iniquity
	  He was shown that the righteousness of God is a substance that He has provided to invest sinners with
	  He came to understand that the Lord Jesus Christ is the righteousness of God in his own person, and that to enter into Him and put Him on by faith is to be righteous before God

•   Luther's Faith in God  
	  He believed that God holds him with His right hand, keeps him as the apple of His eye, and spreads a table for him in the midst of his enemies
	  He was confident that God would preserve and protect him despite his enemies and challenges

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Justification by Faith Alone  
	  Luther believed that justification by faith alone was complete and nothing could be added to it.
	  He received this understanding through faith alone.

•   God's Endowments to Luther  
	  God gave Luther three special endowments:
		- Great knowledge of Scripture
		- Great talent for argumentation
		- Singular felicity in addressing the common people

•   Characteristics of Luther's Testimony  
	  Luther's translation of the Bible is a testament to his great knowledge of Scripture.
	  His disputations with Eckius, Latomus, and Erasmus demonstrate his talent for argumentation.
	  His tracts and sermons, particularly his address to the common people, show his felicity in addressing the common people.

•   God's Role in the Reformation  
	  The Reformation was God's act, inferior only to the acts of Calvary and the Red Sea.
	  God accomplished the Reformation by doing all in all that Luther did and all in all that Luther's enemies did.

•   God's Preparation for the Reformation  
	  God prepared for the Reformation by:
		- Capturing Constantinople by the infidel
		- Spreading learning throughout Christendom
		- Facilitating the means of acquiring knowledge through the art of printing

•   God's Activity in the Reformation  
	  God stirred and blended various agents and interests to bring about the Reformation.
	  He used rival princes and vassals, as well as Luther's firmness, to bring about the Reformation.

•   Luther's Controversial Nature  
	  Luther was a rough and contentious person.
	  Controversy was his element and he spent his life in it.

•   The Lord's Choice of Controversy  
	  Controversy was the Lord's choice weapon for the manifestation of his truth.
	  It has been used throughout history, including in the lives of Paul and John.

•   The Fruits of Controversy  
	  Controversy stirs up inquiry and stops the mouths of gainsayers.
	  It roots and grounds the believers.

•   Luther's Regrets  
	  Luther would have been spared three controversies if he could have.
	  These controversies were maintained against former friends.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Banishment of Carolstadt  
	  Luther banished Carolstadt, a professor at Wittenberg, for his tumultuous behavior
	  Carolstadt had removed papal abuses by breaking images and throwing down altars

•   Carolstadt's Behavior  
	  Carolstadt was a turbulent man who despised learning
	  He was a leader of a group of raw and hot-brained recruits who raved against papal abuses

•   Luther's Return  
	  Luther's return to Wittenberg was motivated by the account of Carolstadt's behavior
	  Luther was dismayed by the disorderly spirit and the danger to the Gospel

•   Carolstadt's Flight and Banishment  
	  Carolstadt fled before Luther and became a factious preacher at Orlamund
	  He was banished by the elector, but restored through Luther's intercession

•   Luther's Reconciliation with Carolstadt  
	  Luther and Carolstadt were reconciled, but without much cordiality
	  Carolstadt eventually retired to Switzerland, where he exercised his pastoral office in a more congenial communion

•   Carolstadt's Treatises  
	  Carolstadt wrote a treatise called "Addressto the Celestial Prophets and Carolstadt"
	  Luther thoroughly approved of Carolstadt's banishment, despite some reservations about Luther's own role in it

•   Luther's Approval of Carolstadt's Banishment  
	  Luther took great pain to get Carolstadt restored after his banishment
	  He succeeded in getting Carolstadt restored through the intercession of John, but not through Frederick

•   Luther's Critique of Carolstadt  
	  Luther found Carolstadt to be repulsive, arbitrary, and ungenerously sarcastic
	  He also found Carolstadt to be unwarrantably contemptuous and exclusive in his comments and conflicts with the Munzerites

•   Carolstadt's Doctrine  
	  Carolstadt's doctrine was found to be whimsical, extravagant, false, and unsettled
	  He was a preacher and practitioner of sedition who united himself with Munzer and his associates

•   Luther's Doctrines  
	  Luther's doctrines were found to be clear in marking the line of civil subordination
	  He taught that the peasants should disclaim and express their abhorrence of rebellion, and that his Sacramentarian error was a separate issue.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's views on the Eucharist  
	  Luther believed in the real presence of Christ's body and blood in the bread and wine
	  He believed that the substance of the bread and wine remains, in addition to the new substance of Christ's body and blood

•   Contradiction to sense and reason  
	  The concept of transubstantiation is absurd and contradicts our senses and reason
	  Our senses (sight, touch, taste, smell) assure us that we are eating bread, not Christ's body

•   Exception to the rule  
	  Luther's views on the Eucharist are an exception to the general rule of interpreting Scripture literally
	  He made an exception to his own principle of literal interpretation to accommodate his views on the Eucharist

•   Implications of denying moral attributes  
	  If we deny the existence of moral attributes in God (e.g. justice, faithfulness, purity), we can know nothing about God
	  We would cease to be moral beings if we deny these fundamental principles

•   Contradiction to Scripture  
	  The Bible nowhere calls us to believe a palpable contradiction
	  The concept of transubstantiation is a contradiction to our senses and reason

•   Scriptural evidence against transubstantiation  
	  The Bible teaches that the Lord's body is extended in one place at a time (e.g. Mk 16:19, 1 Pe 3:22)
	  The Lord's body is not ubiquitously present in multiple places at the same time

•   The nature of the Lord's body  
	  The Lord's body is a spiritual body (1 Cor 15:44)
	  It is not a natural body that can be chewed or consumed (1 Cor 15:50)

•   Luther's stress on literal interpretation  
	  Luther's principle of literal interpretation is sound and just
	  However, he made an exception to this principle in his views on the Eucharist

•   The importance of literal interpretation  
	  Literal interpretation is essential for understanding Scripture
	  We should not make exceptions to this principle unless there is an evident context or absurdity that requires it

•   The implications of denying the Lord's body  
	  If we deny the Lord's body, we are left with nothing but bread and wine
	  The concept of transubstantiation is a false and pernicious expedient that solves difficulties by denying fundamental principles.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Controversy with the Cup  
	  Luther believed that the bread and cup in the Eucharist were consubstantiated into Christ's body and blood.
	  He argued that if the bread was consubstantiated, the cup should also be consubstantiated into a testament.

•   Luther's Obstinacy  
	  Luther was known for his obstinacy in defending his views, even when faced with criticism.
	  He was willing to use forceful language and arguments to establish his doctrine.

•   Luther's Honesty  
	  Despite his heat of spirit and language, Luther was honest in his convictions.
	  He never made concessions to his doctrine, even when faced with severe sickness.

•   The Spirit of Controversy  
	  Luther regretted the spirit he had manifested in the controversy.
	  He had maintained his views like a "wild bull" and called names, making devils of his adversaries.

•   Erasmus' Role in the Reformation  
	  Erasmus, a predecessor of Luther, had done some service to the reformers.
	  He facilitated the knowledge of ancient languages and used his talent for ridicule to attack the grosser abominations of Popery.

•   Erasmus' Motivations  
	  Erasmus was not motivated by a hearty concern for the reformers' cause.
	  He was more interested in using his wit and satire to attack the Catholic Church.

•   Erasmus' Legacy  
	  Erasmus prepared the way for a better faith by deriding much of the old faith.
	  He was not a skeptic, but rather a critic of the Catholic Church.

•   The Multitude of Professing Christians  
	  Many professing Christians are not true believers, but rather triflers who would act out what Luther said in his irony.
	  They lack the spirit of martyrdom and are not willing to stand up for their faith.

•   Erasmus' Commitment to the Cause  
	  Erasmus had committed himself to the cause of the reformers by speaking well of them and acquiescing in many of their dogmas.
	  He had written and spoken in favor of Luther and the reformers in 1520.

•   The Last of the Controversies  
	  The controversy between Luther and his opponents continued until Luther's death.
	  The churches that passed under Luther's name still retain his dogma.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Erasmus's relationship with Luther  
	  Erasmus is impressed by Luther's character and writings
	  He believes Luther is a remarkable person with upright morals and gospel purity
	  However, Erasmus is hesitant to join Luther's cause due to his desire for praise and recognition

•   Erasmus's motivations  
	  He wants to stand high with great men, princes, and dignified ecclesiastics
	  He desires to be seen as a moderate and a man of letters
	  He wants to avoid being associated with the reformers, who are despised by the rulers

•   Erasmus's strategy  
	  He decides to distance himself from Luther and the reformers
	  He wants to maintain a good relationship with the princes and the Pope
	  He believes that by doing so, he can achieve his goals and gain recognition

•   Erasmus's relationship with Luther  
	  He confesses to Luther that he has not written against him, but has instead tried to distance himself from him
	  He tells Luther that he has tried to convince the princes that there is no perfect understanding between them
	  He claims that he has only tried to do away with the idea that Luther and he are in agreement

•   Luther's response  
	  Luther is disappointed that Erasmus has not joined his cause
	  He believes that Erasmus has been playing a double game and has not been sincere in his intentions
	  He tries to persuade Erasmus to join him, but Erasmus refuses

•   The underlings in Luther's camp  
	  They lampoon Erasmus and hint at his true intentions
	  They make him look bad in front of his great ones
	  Luther tries to defend Erasmus, but it is too late

•   The Pope's involvement  
	  Henry VII of England has implored the Pope to take action against Luther
	  Duke George has demanded that the Pope take action
	  Pope Clement VII has persuaded the Pope to take action against Luther

•   Luther's last attempt to pacify Erasmus  
	  He tries to be civil and courteous to Erasmus
	  He hints at the real state of the cause, but does so with sufficient civility
	  He tells Erasmus that he has not complained about him, but has instead tried to distance himself from him

•   Erasmus's response  
	  He thanks Luther for his words and claims that he has only tried to do away with the idea that they are in agreement
	  He tells Luther that he has only tried to convince the princes that there is no perfect understanding between them
	  He claims that he has only tried to be fair and moderate in his actions

•   The outcome  
	  The cause is advanced to the point where it is in peril
	  Erasmus's attempts to distance himself from Luther have failed
	  Luther is disappointed that Erasmus has not joined his cause.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Erasmus's Concerns  
	  Fears that Luther's publication of the Diatribe will lead to conflict
	  Asks Luther to remain neutral and not write tracts against him
	  Offers to publish against Luther if he writes against him

•   The Birth of the Diatribe  
	  Luther's publication of the Diatribe is a response to Erasmus's criticism
	  Erasmus had sent a part of the manuscript to Henry VIII for approval
	  Luther expects to be criticized and consoles himself with the example of Henry VIII

•   Erasmus's Motivations  
	  Claims not to have dedicated the Diatribe to anyone to avoid accusations of being hired to please the great
	  Admits to having written the Diatribe with the intention of pleasing the Pope

•   The Consequences of the Diatribe  
	  Erasmus expects the Diatribe to cause "most prodigious commotions"
	  Pamphlets are already circulating against him

•   The Character of the Diatribe  
	  Described as the work of a great scholar but not a deep thinker
	  Scoured the surface of the question but did not penetrate to its substance

•   The Diatribe's Weaknesses  
	  Imposing but not solid
	  Objurgatory and commendative but neither disproving nor establishing what it approves or disapproves

•   The Diatribe's Popularity  
	  Many careless persons and half the tribe of professedly serious gospelers will defend and maintain the Diatribe
	  Some who call themselves Calvinists or Calvinistic are actually Freewillers in heart and understanding

•   Luther's Response  
	  Hesitated to answer the Diatribe but eventually consented to do so
	  Declared that he would answer with all his might, as he has done

•   Erasmus's Reply  
	  Wrote two treatises under the name of Hyperaspistes, "defender as with a shield"
	  The first was written in a few days and was a passionate and hasty effusion
	  The second was a long and highly-labored performance in which he was completely unfettered and in earnest

•   The Character of Erasmus's Reply  
	  Described as a performance that is tedious and confusing
	  The writer is kept alive by his hostility and resentment, but the reader is fatigued and bewildered

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Debate between Erasmus and Luther  
	  Erasmus and Luther debated the state of the human will, with Erasmus arguing it is free and Luther arguing it is in bondage.
	  Luther's response was not to engage in a debate, but to present his final and unrefuted judgment on the matter.

•   Locke's Analysis of the Question  
	  John Locke analyzed the question and determined that it is improperly stated.
	  The question should not be whether the will is free, but whether man is free.

•   Defining Liberty  
	  Locke defined liberty as the power to act or not to act as one chooses or wills.
	  Liberty is a power to choose, not a state of being.

•   The Proper Question  
	  The proper question is whether man is free, not whether the will is free.
	  Man is free insofar as he can choose the existence or non-existence of an action.

•   The Act of Exercising the Will  
	  Locke determined that, in most cases, man does not have liberty in the act of exercising his will.
	  Once an action is proposed, man must will it.

•   The Result of Exercising the Will  
	  Locke determined that man has liberty in the result of exercising his will.
	  Man wills what he wills and is pleased with what he is pleased with.

•   The Infinite Regression  
	  To question the result of exercising the will is to suppose that one will determines the actions of another will, and so on, ad infinitum.
	  This is a contradictory and impossible scenario.

•   Luther's Agreement with Locke  
	  Luther agreed with Locke that a compelled will is a contradiction in terms.
	  Luther called a compelled will "non-will" (Noluntas) rather than "will" (Voluntas).

•   The Distinction between Faculty and Action  
	  The schoolmen made a distinction between the absolute faculty of the will and that faculty as exercised or in action.
	  This distinction is without a difference, as the faculty is only known when it is exercised.

•   Erasmus's Definition of Freewill  
	  Erasmus defined freewill as the power of the human will to turn towards or away from things that pertain to salvation.
	  Luther denied that there can be any "tertium quid" (third thing) between the will and its actions.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Nature of the Will  
	  The will is not free in the sense that it is not compelled by external forces.
	  However, the will is not entirely free either, as it acts in the dark and is influenced by partial and false evidence.

•   The True Question  
	  The proper question is not "Is the will free?" but rather "Is the understanding free?"
	  This means considering whether a person's perception or understanding is clear and entire.

•   The State of the Natural Man  
	  The natural man has a darkened understanding due to ignorance and alienation from God.
	  He is also possessed by the devil, who maintains and increases his blindness.

•   The State of the Spiritual Man  
	  Even the spiritual man is not entirely free from the influence of the devil.
	  His flesh, or the part of him that is not spiritual, can still be influenced by the devil.

•   The Faculty in Bondage  
	  It is not the will that is in bondage, but rather the faculty that presents objects to the determining faculty.
	  This faculty can present objects erroneously, either by suppressing what should be presented or giving a false appearance to what is.

•   The Paradox of Free Will  
	  The will is both free and not free at the same time.
	  This paradox can be explained by considering the role of perception and understanding in decision-making.

•   The Role of God's Agency  
	  God's agency is involved in shaping our perceptions and understanding.
	  This means that our volitions, while free, are subject to God's agency.

•   The Importance of Self-Knowledge  
	  Ignorance of the real state of man lies at the root of all religious ignorance.
	  Self-knowledge is essential for understanding our moral state and our relationship with God.

•   The Purpose of the Treatise  
	  The treatise aims to explore the present state of the human soul using scripture testimonies and scriptural reasonings.
	  It seeks to avoid abstract philosophical investigation and focus on practical and scriptural understanding.

•   The Significance of the Subject  
	  The subject of free will is momentous and essential for understanding our moral state and our relationship with God.
	  Ignorance of this subject can lead to a lack of understanding of the true nature of Christ and our relationship with Him.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Introduction  
	  The text is a critique of Erasmus' work by Luther
	  Luther's method of investigation is questioned by those who rely on Scripture
    Luther's Approach  
	  Luther's approach is to challenge Erasmus' work through an appeal to the canonical Scriptures
	  He argues that Erasmus should have delivered his challenge in the form of an appeal to Scripture
    The Order of Argumentation  
	  The order of argumentation is shown in the Table of Contents
	  Luther follows the order of Erasmus' work, examining his Preface, Proem, testimonies, and refutation
    The Form of the Treatise  
	  The treatise is written in the form of a letter to Erasmus
	  Luther prefers the division of the work into Parts rather than Chapters
    Luther's Style  
	  Luther's sentences are long and multifarious
	  His language is sometimes eloquent and sometimes simple
    The Excellences of the Treatise  
	  The treatise is based on God's testimony unmixed with human testimony
	  The ground is cleared from objection and an integral part of the truth is firmly established
    The Defects of Luther's Theological System  
	  Luther's theological system is defective and manifest in his best work
	  His system is a whole made up of many parts that are closely interwoven and compacted
    Luther's Method of Investigation  
	  Luther's method of investigation is questioned by those who rely on Scripture
	  He is accused of being an overmatch for Erasmus in metaphysical niceties
    The Subject Matter  
	  The subject matter is the most momentous that can engage the human soul
	  It requires a deep understanding of Scripture and the Father's testimony concerning Jesus
    The Conclusion  
	  Luther concludes the work with a pathetic address, exhibiting a melting mood
	  He is capable of being soft, gentle, and compassionate, contrary to the common idea that he wanted softness.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Truth  
	  The Bible reveals God's truth, which is a unified system of divine operations.
	  This truth is manifest in Christ, who is the image of God.
	  The Gospel or doctrine of the kingdom of God is the declaration of this truth.

•   Understanding the Bible  
	  The Bible should be studied to get to know God, who cannot be fully known without understanding the Bible.
	  The Bible reveals God's whole counsel, not just a proposition.
	  Each part of the Bible should be given its fair and well-considered meaning.

•   Luther's Theological System  
	  Luther's system had defects, including a lack of understanding of God's vast counsel.
	  He did not see that all of God's dealings with creatures are referable to one vast end.
	  This end is the manifestation of God, and it is accomplished through Christ.

•   Christ's Person  
	  Luther had some insight into the mystery of Christ's person, but not fully.
	  Christ is truly God and man, a coequal in the Trinity.
	  He was made man through the Virgin's impregnation by the Holy Ghost.

•   Christ's Union with Humanity  
	  Christ's human person was formed to be both son of Adam and son of God.
	  He had a spotless soul, but a sinful body.
	  He became the sinless savior, who knew no sin and was tempted as we are.

•   Christ's Life and Death  
	  Christ's life was kept without sin by the Holy Ghost.
	  He died to sin once, and his mortality was swallowed up by life.
	  His death and resurrection are essential to the mystery of Christ's person.

•   The Mystery of Christ's Person  
	  The essential element of the mystery of Christ's person is "union yet distinctness".
	  This refers to the union of Christ's divine and human persons.
	  It also refers to his oneness with us.

•   Man's Guilt and Corruption  
	  Luther saw that man comes into the world guilty.
	  He saw that man's nature is vitiated, making him vile and impotent.
	  This is a fact that Luther assumed and reasoned upon throughout his treatise.

•   The Defects of Luther's System  
	  Luther's system had defects, including a lack of understanding of God's vast counsel.
	  He did not see that all of God's dealings with creatures are referable to one vast end.
	  This end is the manifestation of God, and it is accomplished through Christ.

•   The Importance of Understanding God's Counsel  
	  Understanding God's counsel is essential to knowing God.
	  The Bible reveals God's whole counsel, not just a proposition.
	  Each part of the Bible should be given its fair and well-considered meaning.

•   The Trinity and Christ's Actions  
	  Christ's actions exhibit the Trinity in every act he performs.
	  He does the will of the Father continually, not his own will.
	  He is inspired by the Holy Ghost, not by his own godhead.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Plan and Counsel  
	  God had a plan and counsel that was not revealed to Adam
	  Adam was left without insight into God's plan and counsel
	  This ignorance was necessary for God's ultimate manifestation

•   Justice of God  
	  Adam was forced to give up the justice of God
	  He took refuge in a falsehood that God's justice was unknown
	  This ignorance would be resolved on a future day

•   Luther's Ignorance  
	  Luther had some insight into the nature of Holy Ghost influences
	  However, his other areas of ignorance were incompatible with true knowledge
	  He did not understand the gift of the Holy Ghost as a super-creation gift

•   Gift of the Holy Ghost  
	  The gift of the Holy Ghost is a gift of God's personal presence and agency
	  It is a super-creation gift, given when and as God pleases
	  It does not contribute to the justification of a sinner, but enables them to show their justification

•   Justification and Acceptance  
	  Sinners are freely justified, predestined, called, and glorified
	  Their acceptance is not based on their acts, but on God's free choice
	  The Holy Ghost enables and constrains them to believe and obey

•   Luther's Misconceptions  
	  Luther spoke of Adam having the Spirit, as if it were a natural possession
	  He said the Jewish church was not justified by the law because they did not have the Spirit
	  He saw the Spirit as a law-fulfiller, rather than a super-creation gift

•   Law and Spirit  
	  Luther saw the Law as a conde
	  He did not understand the relationship between the Law and the Spirit

•   Adam's State  
	  Adam was not aware of the eternal justification received in Christ Jesus
	  He did not see the state in which believers were brought by Christ's death
	  He did not understand the crown of righteousness that believers receive

•   Differentiation between Righteous and Wicked  
	  The Lord has arranged for the crown of righteousness to bear righteously in God's sight
	  This differentiation is based on the mind of the believer, which is relative to God's manifestation

•   Implications of Ignorance  
	  Adam's ignorance led him to take refuge in a falsehood about God's justice
	  Luther's ignorance led him to misconceptions about the gift of the Holy Ghost and justification

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's view of Plato  
	  Luther was critical of Plato's writings, which he saw as fostering a spirit of proud self-sufficiency
	  He believed Plato's ideas cooperated with other sources of error to contaminate the truth

•   Plato's conception of God  
	  Plato had a vast conception of God, including God's nature, power, and operations
	  He ascribed to God both the modeling of the world and commanding it into being

•   Plato's cosmology  
	  Plato believed in a compound soul, with a material body and an immortal soul
	  He saw the world as having a soul, with a universal soul that brought order out of chaos

•   Plato's ideas and their relation to reality  
	  Plato's "ideas" or "first forms of things" were seen as approaching reality, even if they were not eternal
	  His ideal world, with God reigning supreme, was seen as a glimpse of the invisible glory and repose of God

•   The value of pagan philosophy  
	  The author argues that even pagan philosophers like Plato had a conception and relish beyond many who have received the true light
	  Revelation and tradition have extended more widely than people are aware of

•   The notion of a Trinity  
	  The notion of a Trinity was found to have been a leading principle in ancient schools of philosophy and in the religions of almost all nations
	  Traces of an early popular belief in a Trinity appeared even in idolatrous worship

•   The origin of Platonic doctrine  
	  The Platonists attributed their doctrine to a revelation from the gods, which they called "atheology"
	  This revelation was seen as the means of information for the Platonists, who lived before the Christian era and had no light from the Mosaic writings

•   The source of Platonic doctrine  
	  The author argues that the Platonists' information could only have been drawn from traditions founded on earlier revelations
	  These traditions were seen as fragments of the ancient patriarchal creed, which was universal before the defection of the first idolaters

•   The significance of Socratic and Platonic philosophy  
	  Socratic and Platonic philosophy were seen as the twilight of revelation, after the sun of it had set in the race of Noah
	  The author argues that even pagan philosophers like Plato had a conception and relish beyond many who have received the true light

•   Luther's criticism of Plato  
	  Luther was surprised that Luther should fiercely attack Plato, given that his beloved Augustine acknowledged obligations to him
	  Augustine had read passages in Plato's books that concerned the divine word, similar to those in the first chapter of St John's Gospel.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Augustine's Background  
	  Had a profligate youth and was infected with the poison of the Manichees
	  Sold himself to vain-glory, lasciviousness, pride, and atheism
	  Was later made to bow down before the true God and kiss his Son

•   Augustine's Conversion  
	  Was prepared by God to be the champion of grace in opposition to Pelagianism
	  Was led to use his own experience as an interpreter of Scripture
	  Was persuaded of and maintained the doctrine of predestination, although not in all its vigor

•   Augustine's Understanding of Predestination  
	  Did not thoroughly understand predestination, which is the root of the mystery of God
	  Did not understand justification, redemption, man's state, or the grace he defended
	  His ignorance of predestination led to his ignorance of other key doctrines

•   The Nature of Grace  
	  Grace of the Spirit is a part of the grace of God the Father, given to us in Christ Jesus
	  Augustine did not discern the spring, channel, and mouth of this grace

•   Augustine's Relationship with Luther  
	  Both Augustine and Luther are clear confirmers of the position that the light of divine truth is progressive
	  Augustine knew what Cyprian did not, and Luther knew what Augustine did not

•   The Progress of Divine Truth  
	  The light of divine truth is progressive, with each generation building on the previous one
	  The climax of this progress is with Luther, Calvin, and Cranmer

•   Augustine's Teaching  
	  His distinguishing glory was to teach humility and give God his due honor
	  He was singularly prepared for this by his course of internal experience

•   Augustine's Humility  
	  He had felt human insufficiency completely and knew that in himself dwelt no good thing
	  He was admirably qualified to describe the total depravity and apostasy of human nature

•   Augustine's Writing Style  
	  He wrote with godly simplicity, inexpressible seriousness, and humility
	  He was unmatched in any age in his ability to describe the human condition and the nature of God

•   Augustine's Legacy  
	  He taught men what it means to be humble before God
	  His writings continue to inspire and instruct people to this day

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Contingent and mutable will  
	  A work is considered contingent and mutable if it is done by a will that is not fixed or determined.
	  This is not the case with God's will, which is immutable and infallible.

•   Definition of "necessity"  
	  The term "necessity" is not accurately applied to God's will or human will.
	  It implies compulsion, which is the opposite of willingness.

•   God's will vs. human will  
	  God's will is immutable and infallible, governing human will.
	  Human will, on the other hand, is mutable and wicked, unable to do good on its own.

•   The Sophists' argument  
	  The Sophists argue that all events are necessary, but only by the necessity of a consequence, not by the necessity of a consequent.
	  This distinction is seen as a play on words, with no real substance.

•   The distinction between necessity of a consequence and necessity of a consequent  
	  The Sophists argue that an event is necessary if it is a consequence of God's will, but not if it is a necessary event itself.
	  This distinction is seen as absurd and unnecessary.

•   The truth about necessity  
	  Every event is necessary if it is an act of God or a necessary consequence.
	  However, an event may not exist necessarily, but rather as a contingent and mutable thing.

•   The futility of the Sophists' argument  
	  The Sophists' argument is seen as a foolery, with no real substance or meaning.
	  The truth remains that all events are necessary, but not in the sense that they are God himself.

•   The clarity of the concept  
	  The concept of necessity is clear and straightforward, implying the immutability of God's will and the impotency of human will.
	  The distinction between necessity of a consequence and necessity of a consequent is seen as unnecessary and confusing.

•   The universality of the concept  
	  The concept of necessity is not unique to Christianity, but is also found in heathen poetry and common conversation.
	  The idea that all things are brought to pass by the unchangeable will of God is a widely accepted and understood concept.

•   The importance of understanding the concept  
	  The concept of necessity is not something to be kept secret or hidden, but rather something to be understood and appreciated.
	  The idea that all things are brought to pass by the unchangeable will of God is a fundamental and essential concept in understanding the nature of reality.
