•   The Power of the God of this World  
	  When we are under the power of the god of this world, we are destitute of the work and Spirit of the true God
	  Our will is not free to change or turn to something else, but is instead provoked to will what it already wants

•   The Implications of the Paradox  
	  If the paradox is true, then everything we do is evil and of no profit to our salvation
	  Our will is not free to change or turn to something else, but is instead willing and loving what God wills.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Human Will is Captive  
	  The human will is subject to God's will, as stated in 2 Timothy 2:26.
	  We cannot will anything but what God wills.

•   The Kingdom of Satan  
	  The kingdom of Satan is divided against itself and cannot stand.
	  Christ affirms that Satan's kingdom does stand, implying that it is united in its opposition to God.

•   The Human Will's Nature  
	  Our will is willing and cordial in submitting to God's will.
	  Our will is not a "non-will" when it is compelled, but rather a willing submission.

•   The Human Will as a Pack Horse  
	  The human will is placed between two contending parties: God and Satan.
	  If God mounts the will, it wills and goes where God pleases.
	  If Satan mounts the will, it wills and goes where Satan pleases.

•   The Folly of Claiming Freewill  
	  Erasmus is convicted by his own concession that there is no such thing as freewill.
	  If the will is not free, then it is not a will at all.

•   The Power of Freewill  
	  Erasmus represents the power of freewill as something very dim and ineffective without God's grace.
	  If the will is not free, then it cannot do good without God's grace.

•   The Inefficacy of Freewill  
	  If the will is not free, then it is not absolutely free, but rather a captive and slave of evil.
	  The will cannot turn to good without God's grace.

•   The Definition of Freewill  
	  If freewill is defined as the power to render man fit to be seized by the Spirit and imbued with God's grace, then it is a proper definition.
	  This definition implies that the will is not free without God's grace.

•   The Absence of Freewill  
	  We do all things by necessity, and nothing by freewill, so long as the power of the will is nothing.
	  The will cannot do good in the absence of God's grace.

•   The Title of Freewill  
	  The title of freewill belongs to God alone and cannot be joined with any other being.
	  If the title of freewill is ascribed to man, then it is equivalent to ascribing divinity to man.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Problem with the Term "Freewill"  
	  Theologians use the term "Freewill" to describe human power, but it's a misleading and exaggerated concept.
	  The common people misunderstand the term and think it means humans have complete control over their actions.

•   The Misconception of Freewill  
	  The term "Freewill" implies that humans have a power that can turn itself freely to either side, without being subject to anyone.
	  In reality, this power is small and insignificant, and humans are captive to the devil.

•   The Danger of Misusing Language  
	  Using language in a way that deceives or beguiles people is a serious offense, especially in matters of piety.
	  Theologians should be careful not to use empty or misleading terms that can lead people astray.

•   The Folly of Retaining an Empty Name  
	  Theologians stubbornly retain the term "Freewill" despite its emptiness and the danger it poses to the common people.
	  This is like kings and princes claiming to possess empty titles of kingdoms and countries.

•   The Consequences of Misusing Language  
	  Misusing language can lead to deception and soul-danger, which are injurious to people's spiritual well-being.
	  It's not just a matter of vanity, but a serious issue that can have eternal consequences.

•   The Abuse of Language  
	  Using language in a way that's contrary to common usage can be seen as deceitful or mocking.
	  For example, calling a beggar "rich" because someone might give him money is an abuse of language.

•   The Importance of Plain and Sober Language  
	  Theologians should use language that's plain, pure, and sober, as Paul advises in Titus 2:7-8.
	  This means avoiding empty or misleading terms that can lead people astray.

•   The Limits of Human Freedom  
	  Human freedom is limited to those substances that are inferior to humans themselves, not to those that are superior.
	  Humans have a right to use their faculties and possessions as they see fit, but this right is also controlled by God's will.

•   The Captivity of Human Will  
	  In matters of salvation or damnation, humans have no freedom, but are captive to the will of God or Satan.
	  This means that humans are subject to a higher power and cannot act independently.

•   The Conclusion of Luther's Review  
	  Luther concludes his review of Erasmus' Preface by reducing him to a dilemma and making short work of some of his sharp sayings.
	  Luther argues that Erasmus' language is misleading and that he should be more careful in his use of terms.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Response to Erasmus' Preface  
	  Erasmus' preface complains about the words of God or man
	  Luther argues that if it complains about human words, it's useless; if it complains about God's words, it's profane

•   Luther's Critique of Erasmus' Approach  
	  Erasmus' approach is absurd and ignorant, according to Luther
	  Luther argues that Erasmus' approach is not faithful to Paul's teachings

•   Luther's Defense of His Own Approach  
	  Luther argues that he preaches only Jesus crucified
	  However, this phrase brings with it the wisdom and mystery of God, which is not suitable for children or those who glory in works without faith

•   Luther's Critique of Erasmus' Use of Language  
	  Erasmus uses figurative language to describe God's actions, but Luther argues that this is not necessary
	  Luther argues that the sentiment is clear, even if the words are not

•   Luther's Rejection of Human Authority  
	  Luther argues that human authority is admitted but depreciated
	  He claims that he is not bound by the authority of any other writer

•   Luther's Response to Erasmus' Promise  
	  Erasmus promises to lead the canonical scriptures only
	  Luther accepts this promise, but argues that Erasmus does not do so out of a genuine commitment to the scriptures

•   Luther's Critique of Erasmus' Appeal to Authority  
	  Erasmus appeals to the authority of many learned men, martyrs, and theologians
	  Luther argues that this is not a sufficient basis for authority, and that he is not impressed by the numbers or grandeur of Erasmus' supporters

•   Luther's Defense of His Own Authority  
	  Luther argues that he is not a private man, but rather a man of faith and conviction
	  He claims that he is not bound by the opinions of others, and that he is willing to stand alone in his convictions

•   Luther's Critique of Erasmus' Boast  
	  Erasmus boasts of his own learning and genius, but Luther argues that this is not sufficient
	  Luther argues that Erasmus' boasts are empty and that he is not a true servant of God

•   Luther's Final Rebuke  
	  Luther argues that Erasmus is like a wolf who acknowledges the voice of the nightingale, but does not truly understand its significance
	  He claims that Erasmus talks, but does not truly believe.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Defense  
	  Luther claims that he would have continued to admire the Fathers if not for the goading of his conscience and evidence of facts.
	  He asserts that his actions were not driven by his own free will, but by God's will.

•   Luther's Humility  
	  Luther acknowledges that he is inferior to the Fathers in terms of erudition, genius, numbers, and authority.
	  He invites his opponent to judge him and his works.

•   Challenging the Opponent  
	  Luther challenges his opponent to answer three questions: What is the manifestation of the Spirit? What are miracles? What is sanctification?
	  He claims that his opponent would be unable to answer these questions.

•   Borrowed Language  
	  Luther accuses his opponent of using borrowed language and public discourse to support his arguments.
	  He warns that such language loses its credit and authority when summoned to the bar of conscience.

•   The Fathers' Sanctification  
	  Luther argues that even if the Fathers were sanctified, had the Spirit, and worked miracles, it was not due to their own free will.
	  He claims that their actions were done in the name of and by the power of Jesus Christ.

•   Miracles and Sanctification  
	  Luther argues that the Fathers' miracles and sanctification are not evidence of free will, but of the power of Jesus Christ.
	  He claims that their actions were often forestalled by the flesh.

•   The Apostle's Example  
	  Luther cites the example of the Apostles, who were sometimes forestalled by the flesh despite living under the immediate eye of Christ.
	  He argues that this shows that even holy men can speak and act according to the flesh.

•   The Spirit and Free Will  
	  Luther argues that the Spirit cannot preach free will, as it is a human affair and not a matter of the Spirit.
	  He claims that if the Fathers preached free will, they did so by the flesh and not by the Spirit of God.

•   Luther's Challenge  
	  Luther challenges his opponent to show the effects of free will in the three particular excellencies he has selected.
	  He invites his opponent to manifest the Spirit, publish miracles, and display sanctification.

•   The Burden of Proof  
	  Luther argues that his opponent owes him the things he claims, such as manifesting the Spirit and publishing miracles.
	  He claims that his opponent's assertions about free will are unfounded and lack evidence.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Critique of the Church's Teaching  
	  The Church's teaching is often based on the words of Christ, but also on the words of the Fathers and Saints, which may be flawed due to human infirmity.
	  The Church's teaching is not always consistent, and the Fathers and Saints have said contradictory things.

•   The Problem of Dogma  
	  The Church demands that certain dogmas be accepted without reason or evidence, simply because they are pleasing to the powerful.
	  This is an incredible degree of presumption or insanity, and the Church asserts the existence of things that are confessedly nothing.

•   Deluding the Common People  
	  The Church deludes the poor and wretched common people with "nothing" as if it were something of great moment to their salvation.
	  This is not a worthy conduct for Christian teachers.

•   The Role of Readers  
	  Readers of books often make the dogmas of the Fathers and Saints, which are the offspring of their infirmity, to be of the highest authority.
	  This is similar to leaning on the sanctity and authority of St. Peter to justify false statements.

•   Injustice to the Fathers  
	  Men of this sort choose the bad sayings of the Fathers and leave their good sayings.
	  This is similar to those who sneer at the Gospel and lay hold of every word that is not true.

•   The Assertors of Freewill  
	  The assertors of Freewill do the same thing as those who sneer at the Gospel, but with a different design and through blindness and ignorance.
	  They oppose the better sayings of the Fathers to the worse sayings.

•   Choosing the Better  
	  Why do we not rather choose the better sayings of the Fathers?
	  Many such sayings are in the works of the Fathers, but are not given authority.

•   The Example of Jerome  
	  Jerome's saying "Virginity fills heaven, and marriage fills earth" is an example of a carnal, impious, sacrilegious, and blasphemous statement.
	  Yet the Sophists collect such sayings from the Fathers and maintain a contest of numbers rather than judgment.

•   The Objection to God's Disguise  
	  You say it is incredible that God should have disguised the error of his Church for so many ages.
	  I reply that we do not say that this error has been tolerated by God in his Church or in any of his saints.

•   The Church Governed by the Spirit  
	  The Church is governed by the Spirit of God.
	  The saints are led by the Spirit of God, and Christ remains with his Church until the end of the world.

•   The author's concern  : The author is concerned that some people, including Erasmus, are saying that the Scriptures are obscure or ambiguous.

•   The author's question  : The author asks if the Scriptures are truly obscure or ambiguous.
	  He cites examples of clear and well-known passages, such as "God created the heavens and the earth" and "the word was made flesh".

•   The author's argument  : The author argues that if the Scriptures are obscure, it would be useless for God to declare them to us from heaven.
	  He also argues that if the Scriptures are unclear, it would be impossible to have a clear and certain declaration of them.

	  He argues that if the Scriptures are obscure, it would be impossible to have a clear and certain form of Christianity.

•   The author's conclusion  : The author concludes that if the dogma of Free Will is obscure, it is not in Scripture.
	  He argues that if the Scriptures are unclear, it would be impossible to have a clear and certain doctrine.

•   The author's reference to Peter  : The author references Peter's statement that "the word of God is a lamp shining in a dark place".
	  He argues that if there is a part of the lamp that does not shine, it would become part of the dark place, rather than part of the lamp itself.

•   The author's argument against obscurity  : The author argues that if the Scriptures are unclear, it would be impossible to give heed to them.
	  He argues that Christ commands us to give heed to the Scriptures, but if they are unclear, it would be impossible to do so.

•   The author's conclusion about Free Will  : The author concludes that if the dogma of Free Will is obscure or ambiguous, it does not belong to Christians and the Scriptures.
	  He argues that it should be abandoned and ranked among those fables that Paul condemns Christians for wrangling about.

•   The author's final argument  : The author argues that if the dogma of Free Will is part of the Scriptures, it should be clear, open, and evident, just like all the other articles of faith.
	  He argues that if it is unclear, it would be impossible to have a clear and certain doctrine.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The promise of Christ  
	  Christ promises to give his followers a mouth and wisdom that their adversaries cannot resist.
	  This promise is based on the idea that Christ's followers will be able to confute their adversaries.

•   The meaning of "resist"  
	  The inability of adversaries to resist does not mean they are compelled to abandon their own mindset.
	  It also does not mean they are persuaded to confess or be silent.

•   Examples of Christ's power  
	  Christ was able to put the Sadducees to silence by citing Scripture.
	  He was also able to confute the Pharisees with evident Scriptures and arguments.

•   Stephen's experience  
	  Stephen was able to speak with wisdom and the Spirit, but his adversaries were not able to resist.
	  Despite this, they continued to resist and even became angry.

•   The Pope and his adversaries  
	  The Pope and his partisans are notorious for their impiety and wickedness.
	  Despite this, they were able to resist John Huss and even had him burned at the stake.

•   The nature of adversaries  
	  Adversaries will resist, even if they are unable to resist.
	  This is because they are adversaries, and not friends.

•   The kind of victory we can expect  
	  We should be content with a victory that does not involve our adversaries confessing defeat.
	  Instead, we should be satisfied with being able to confute them.

•   The human desire to resist  
	  People generally prefer to seem knowledgeable rather than be a learner.
	  This is a natural human tendency that we should take into account when dealing with adversaries.

•   The limitations of our power  
	  We should not expect to be able to change our adversaries' minds.
	  Instead, we should focus on being able to confute them and demonstrate the truth.

•   The importance of perseverance  
	  We should be willing to persevere in the face of adversity, even if our adversaries do not change their minds.
	  This perseverance is a key aspect of being a follower of Christ.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Erasmus' Critique of Luther's View  
	  Erasmus argues that Luther's view of Scripture is ambiguous and obscure.
	  He claims that Luther's assertion of Freewill is foolish and ignorant.

•   Erasmus' Syllogism  
	  Erasmus presents a syllogism: either Luther's view of Scripture is correct, or the Scripture is not plain.
	  Since Luther claims the Scripture is not plain, Erasmus argues that Luther's view of Scripture is false.

•   Erasmus' Refutation of Luther's View  
	  Erasmus affirms that the Scriptures are most clear.
	  He argues that those who assert Freewill are most ignorant of the Scriptures.
	  He claims that Luther's assertion of Freewill was made absent-mindedly, without serious consideration.

•   Erasmus' Use of Luther's Testimony  
	  Erasmus quotes Luther's own testimony that nothing certain has been determined on the subject of Freewill.
	  He argues that this testimony is against Luther's own views.

•   Erasmus' Critique of Luther's Definition of Freewill  
	  Erasmus examines Luther's definition of Freewill as the power of the human will to apply itself to eternal salvation.
	  He argues that this definition is defective, as it does not fill up the thing defined.

•   Erasmus' Argument Against Freewill  
	  Erasmus argues that Freewill belongs only to God.
	  He claims that the term Freewill is properly applied to that which can do and does, towards God, without being confined by any law or command.

•   Erasmus' Critique of Luther's View of Scripture  
	  Erasmus argues that Luther's view of Scripture is not supported by a single word or fact in Scripture.
	  He claims that this view belongs to the "truestories" of Lucian, rather than to Christian doctrine.

•   Erasmus' Refusal to Engage with Luther's View  
	  Erasmus argues that he has already refuted Luther's view of Freewill.
	  He claims that he will proceed to confute Luther's arguments and defend his own.

•   Erasmus' Plan of Argument  
	  Erasmus plans to confute Luther's arguments in favor of Freewill.
	  He will defend his own confuted arguments.
	  He will make his stand for the grace of God in direct conflict with Freewill.

•   Erasmus' Critique of Luther's Method  
	  Erasmus argues that Luther's method is to throw dust in the eyes of the common people.
	  He claims that Luther's words are vain and intended to give trouble to his cause.

	  If God is absent, then Satan is present, and there is no will in us but towards evil.

•   The Nature of Freewill  
	  When we attempt to do good, we most of all do evil and oppose good.
	  Thus, Diatribe is as much a speaker as Freewill is an actor.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Preacher's statement  : The Preacher says "He added precepts and commands. If you will" (Ecclesiasticus 15:1), which is interpreted as implying that man has a free will to keep or not keep the commandments.

•   Interpretation of "If you will"  : The author argues that the verb "If you will" is in the conjunctive mood, which asserts nothing indicatively, and therefore does not prove the existence of free will.

•   Alternative interpretations  : The author suggests that the passage can be interpreted in a way that is consonant with the whole tenor of Scripture, rather than supporting free will.

•   The Preacher's words  : The Preacher's words "If you are willing to keep the commandments. they shall preserve you" (Ecclesiasticus 15:2) do not prove the existence of free will, according to the author.

•   Diatrib's argument  : Diatribe argues that the Preacher's statement implies that there is a will in man both to keep and not to keep the commandments, which is absurd.

•   Human reason's argument  : Diatribe argues that human reason would not say to a blind person "If you will see, you will find a treasure" or to a deaf person "If you will hear, I will tell you a pretty story", as this would be ridiculous.

•   The author's response  : The author responds that these are the arguments of human reason, which is prone to pouring out foolish sayings, and that he must dispute not only with the Preacher but also with human reason.

•   Diatrib's method  : Diatribe interprets the Scriptures of God by her own consequences and syllogisms, drawing them wherever she will, without considering the whole tenor of Scripture.

•   The author's criticism  : The author criticizes Diatribe for measuring the things and words of God by the things and usage of men, which is perverse, as the one is earthly and the other is heavenly.

•   Counterexample  : The author provides counterexamples of how human reason would use the phrase "If you will" in a way that is not ridiculous, such as when parents command their children to do something they know they cannot do.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Diatribe's Inference  
	  Diatribe infers that if God says "if you will" or "if you will do", it means that man's will is free.
	  However, this inference is based on a misunderstanding of God's nature and man's condition.

•   God's Nature and Man's Condition  
	  God deals with man as a father deals with his children, showing man his impotency.
	  Man is blind to his own strength and proud, thinking he knows and can do all things.

•   God's Purpose in Giving Laws  
	  God gives laws to humble man's pride and ignorance.
	  Propounding laws is God's most effective remedy for man's pride and ignorance.

•   Diatribe's Error  
	  Diatribe thinks that man is sound and whole, just like he is in the sight of his fellow men.
	  However, Scripture declares man to be corrupt and captive, and a proud despiser of God.

•   Diatribe's Inconsistency  
	  Diatribe says that man is mocked by words like "if you will" unless his will is free.
	  However, this is inconsistent with her own opinion that man cannot will good.

•   Diatribe's Failure to Follow Her Own Inference  
	  If Diatribe thinks that the inference "if you will, therefore you can will freely" is good, she should follow it consistently.
	  However, she does not follow it, and instead says that man cannot will good.

•   The Problem of Freewill  
	  Diatribe's argument is self-contradictory and mutually devouring.
	  It is like the Midianites destroying themselves in a mutual slaughter.

•   The Preacher's Statement  
	  The Preacher says "if you will keep the commandments, they will preserve you".
	  This implies that man has the power to keep the commandments.

•   The Implication of the Preacher's Statement  
	  If we draw inferences like Diatribe does, we would infer that man can keep the commandments.
	  This would imply that man has the power to keep the commandments.

•   The Pelagian Error  
	  The Pelagians deny grace altogether and attribute everything to Freewill.
	  Diatribe's argument would lead to this error, if taken to its logical conclusion.

	  This is because the Preacher's words are meant to warn people about their own impotency and inability to do good without divine help.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Imperative verbs  
	  Express what ought to be done, not what can be done
	  Used to give commands or instructions
	  Examples: "choose", "keep", "do"

•   Indicative verbs  
	  Express what is done or can be done
	  Used to describe actions or states
	  Examples: "I choose", "I keep", "I do"

•   Theological error  
	  Theologians mistakenly infer indicative verbs from imperative verbs
	  This leads to a misunderstanding of human power and ability

•   Scripture passages  
	  Deuteronomy 30 and other passages use imperative verbs to express what ought to be done
	  These passages do not imply human power or ability to do what is commanded

•   Freewill  
	  The Diatribe argues that freewill is proven by Scripture passages
	  However, these passages only express what ought to be done, not what can be done

•   Consequence of Diatribe's argument  
	  If the Diatribe's argument is valid, it would imply that humans have the power to do what is commanded
	  This would be a Pelagian view, which is contrary to the Christian doctrine of original sin and the need for grace

•   Similes and analogies  
	  The Diatribe uses similes and analogies to illustrate its points
	  However, these similes and analogies actually prove the opposite of what the Diatribe intends

•   Pelagianism  
	  The Diatribe's argument is similar to Pelagianism, which holds that humans have the power to do good without the aid of grace
	  This view is contrary to the Christian doctrine of original sin and the need for grace

•   Theological implications  
	  The Diatribe's argument has significant theological implications
	  If humans have the power to do what is commanded, it would undermine the doctrine of original sin and the need for grace

•   Conclusion  
	  The Diatribe's argument is flawed and leads to a misunderstanding of human power and ability
	  The correct interpretation of Scripture passages is that they express what ought to be done, not what can be done.

	  The Diatribe's argument for free will from the command to love God is inconsistent.

	  The command to love God shows what one ought to do, but not what one can do.
	  This is true of all commands that demand action.

	  Even the schoolmen, with the exception of the Scotists and Moderns, assert that man cannot love God with his whole heart.
	  This implies that man cannot fulfill any of the other commandments.

	  The Diatribe infers an indicative sense from Zechariah's saying "Turn to me" and maintains that it proves a power of endeavouring in free will.
	  This is an absurd interpretation of the text.

•   The Meaning of "Turn"  
	  The Diatribe attributes an endeavouring sense to the word "turn", which is not its proper meaning.
	  In Scripture, "turn" has two senses: a legal sense and an evangelical sense.

•   The Legal Sense of "Turn"  
	  In its legal sense, "turn" is an exacting and commanding word that requires a change of life.
	  Jeremiah frequently uses "turn" in this sense, requiring a change of life.

•   The Evangelical Sense of "Turn"  
	  In its evangelical sense, "turn" is a word of divine promise and consolation.
	  It offers the grace of God to those who turn to Him.

•   Zechariah's Saying  
	  Zechariah's saying "Turn to me" is a summary law word that requires a change of life.
	  It is also a word of divine promise and consolation.

•   The Importance of Distinguishing between Law and Grace  
	  A wise reader of Scripture must distinguish between law words and grace words.
	  This is necessary to avoid jumbling them together, as the Diatribe does.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Diatribe's Argument  
	  Diatribe argues that the words "if you will" in Matthew 19:17 are meaningless if human will is not free.
	  She claims that if human will is not free, then it cannot will good and will necessarily serve sin if grace is not present.
    Erasmus' Response  
	  Erasmus responds that Diatribe's argument is based on a misunderstanding of the words "if you will".
	  He argues that the words "if you will" do not imply that human will is free, but rather that it is dependent on God's power.
    Meaning of "If"  
	  Erasmus argues that the conjunction "if" is often used to express impossibility or impotency, rather than freedom of will.
	  He cites examples from literature where "if" is used to express impossibility, such as "if you would equal Virgil, you must sing others' songs".
    Scriptural Interpretation  
	  Erasmus argues that similar words in Scripture, such as "if you will be perfect" or "if you love me", do not imply freedom of will.
	  He claims that these words are used to express the impotency of human will, but also to intimate that God's power can accomplish what is impossible for humans.
    Impotency vs. Power  
	  Erasmus argues that the words "if you will" can be used to express both impotency and power, depending on the context.
	  He claims that if human will is impotent, then it cannot do what is commanded, but if God's power is involved, then all things are possible.
    Predestination and Grace  
	  Erasmus argues that the words "if you will" can be used to express the idea that God's predestination and grace are involved in human actions.
	  He claims that this is the only way to make sense of the words "if you will" in Scripture.
    Erasmus' Objection  
	  Erasmus objects that Diatribe's argument is based on a misunderstanding of the words "if you will".
	  He claims that Diatribe's argument is based on a false dichotomy between human will and God's power.
    Erasmus' Solution  
	  Erasmus proposes a solution that combines the ideas of impotency and power.
	  He argues that human will is impotent, but God's power can accomplish what is impossible for humans.
    Implications of Erasmus' Argument  
	  Erasmus' argument implies that human will is not free, but rather dependent on God's power.
	  He claims that this is the only way to make sense of the words "if you will" in Scripture.
    Erasmus' Response to Diatribe's Objection  
	  Erasmus responds to Diatribe's objection that precepts are given and merit is ascribed to human will.
	  He argues that this objection is based on a misunderstanding of the words "if you will" and the nature of human will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Necessity and Merit  
	  Necessity is not the same as merit or reward
	  If we speak of necessity as compulsion, it's true, but if we speak of necessity as immutability, it's false

•   Reward and Punishment  
	  Reward or punishment follows naturally from our actions
	  We don't give rewards to unwilling workers, but to those who willfully do good or evil

•   Worthiness vs. Consequence  
	  The inquiry into merit and reward is either about worthiness or consequence
	  If we look at worthiness, there is no such thing as merit or reward

•   Freewill and Grace  
	  Freewill can only will good through grace, not on its own
	  The will to do good, along with its merit or reward, is of grace only

•   Diatribe's Inconsistency  
	  Diatribe argues for freedom of the will from merit, but also asserts that there is merit and reward
	  She is in the same condemnation as those she opposes, fighting against herself

•   Consequences of Actions  
	  There is nothing good or bad that doesn't have its consequence
	  We get into mistakes by speaking of merits and rewards instead of consequences

•   Hell and Judgment  
	  Hell and judgment await the wicked by a necessary consequence
	  The wicked don't desire or think of such a reward for their sins, but it follows naturally

•   The Kingdom of God  
	  The kingdom of God awaits the godly, though they neither seek it nor think of it
	  The kingdom is a possession prepared for them by their Father, not just before they existed, but even before the foundation of the world

•   The Sons of God  
	  The sons of God do good through a gratuitous good pleasure
	  They don't seek any reward, but seek the glory of God and aim to do the will of God

•   Earning the Kingdom  
	  The kingdom of God is not earned by its possessors, but is prepared for them
	  The children of the kingdom don't earn the kingdom, but are prepared for it

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Nature of Seeking Eternal Life  
	  Those who truly do good seek eternal life, but not through a mercenary disposition.
	  They still seek eternal life, which is the way to arrive at and obtain it.

•   The Purpose of Scripture  
	  The Scriptures declare what will happen as a result of a good or evil life.
	  This is to instruct, warn, excite, or terrify people.

•   The Role of Law and Promises/Threatenings  
	  The law points out our sin and impotency, but does not imply we have power.
	  Promises and threatenings warn us of what follows from our sin and impotency, but do not ascribe worthiness to our merit.

•   The Function of Words of Reward and Exhortation  
	  Words of reward stand in place of exhortation and threatening to stir up, comfort, and revive the godly.
	  They encourage people to persevere and conquer in doing good and enduring evil.

•   God's Revival of Abraham  
	  God revives Abraham by saying, "I am your exceeding great reward."
	  This is a type of consolation that the Scripture frequently uses.

•   The Limitations of Reason  
	  Reason objects to the account of God's will, but is answered that it is simply the will of God.
	  We should reverence, love, and adore God's will, rather than demanding reasons for it.

•   The Purpose of Hope and Expectation  
	  The things hoped for will certainly take place, although godly men do not hope because of the things themselves.
	  Ungodly men are terrified and cast down by words of threatening that announce a judgment to come.

•   The Relationship between the Will and the Word  
	  The will cannot turn itself either way, and does not perform more through hearing the word.
	  The Spirit is necessary to move the soul within, and the will depends on the power and work of the Holy Ghost.

•   God's Determination to Give the Spirit through the Word  
	  God has determined to give the Spirit by the word, and not without it.
	  The word is used to sound out what God alone and by himself breathes within, producing effects that he could doubtless accomplish without the word.

•   The Limitations of Human Understanding  
	  We should not demand reasons for God's will, but rather reverence, love, and adore it.
	  It is enough for us to know that God wills so, and to put a restraint on rash reason.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Diatribe's argument against Freewill  
	  Diatribe claims that expressions of Freewill in the New Testament are vain, superfluous, and meaningless.
	  She argues that these expressions prove an entire Freewill, which is not supported by the Bible.

•   Diatribe's interpretation of Matthew 7:16  
	  Diatribe claims that the verse "You shall know them by their fruits" means that works are not truly ours if they are performed by necessity.
	  She argues that if we receive things from others, we should not claim them as our own.

•   Counterarguments to Diatribe's claims  
	  The author argues that just because we receive things from others, it doesn't mean we can't call them our own.
	  The author points out that we have received many things from God, including our own bodies and abilities.

•   Diatribe's interpretation of 1 Corinthians 4:7  
	  Diatribe claims that the verse "What do you have that you did not receive?" means that we have nothing that is truly ours.
	  The author argues that this verse actually supports the idea that we have received many things from God.

•   Diatribe's argument against the idea of "our" fruits  
	  Diatribe claims that if we say that our fruits are truly ours, then what becomes of grace and the Spirit?
	  The author argues that this is a ridiculous and superfluous argument.

•   Diatribe's interpretation of Luke 23:34  
	  Diatribe claims that the verse "Father, forgive them; for they know not what they do" is against Freewill.
	  The author argues that this verse actually testifies that they cannot will good.

•   Counterarguments to Diatribe's claims  
	  The author argues that if they know not what they do, then they cannot will good.
	  The author points out that this is a clear affirmation against Freewill.

•   Diatribe's handling of Scripture  
	  Diatribe's handling of Scripture is audacious and attempts to make the word of God ambiguous and obscure.
	  The author argues that this is a ridiculous and superfluous argument.

•   Diatribe's argument against the idea of Freewill  
	  Diatribe claims that the idea of Freewill is proved by the fact that we can will nothing good.
	  The author argues that this is actually a proof of a Freewill that can do everything.

•   Conclusion  
	  The author concludes that Diatribe's arguments are ridiculous and superfluous, and that the Bible actually testifies against Freewill.

•   Diatribe's argument against John 1:12

	  The author argues that faith in Jesus is necessary to become sons of God.

	  Diatribe is seen as unable to distinguish between law and promise.

	  The author argues that Diatribe's interpretation of passages is absurd and contradictory.
	  This is seen as a result of Diatribe's contemptuous and aversive mindset.

	  Paul's words suggest that contempt for God's goodness and patience is not possible if human will is free.

•   The author's response to Diatribe's objections  
	  The author argues that Diatribe's objections are not relevant to the discussion.
	  The author sees Diatribe's arguments as a result of her own contemptuous and aversive mindset.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Diatribe's argument against Freewill  
	  Diatribe argues that Freewill is impossible because God invites repentance, but only helps those who are willing to repent.
	  She claims that God's help is necessary for man to will good, and that without it, man is unable to do so.

•   Contradiction in Diatribe's argument  
	  Diatribe's argument contradicts her own statements about Freewill, which she claims can do everything.
	  She quotes Scripture to support her argument, but her own conclusions contradict the meaning of the texts.

•   Luther's response to Diatribe's argument  
	  Luther argues that Diatribe's argument is ridiculous and meaningless because it is based on a false premise.
	  He claims that the Apostle Paul's writings do not support Diatribe's view of Freewill.

•   Diatribe's use of Scripture  
	  Diatribe collects a number of imperative or conjunctive verbs from Paul's writings to support her argument.
	  However, Luther argues that these texts do not support her view of Freewill, but rather show that Christians are led by the Spirit of God, not by their own Freewill.

•   Wickliff's confession  
	  Luther quotes Wickliff's words, which he deliberately owns, stating that "all things are done by necessity" and that our will is incapable of doing good by its own power.
	  Diatribe defends Wickliff's views in conjunction with Luther's, but her own proof establishes the direct contrary.

•   Diatribe's attack on Luther's views  
	  Diatribe attacks Luther's views on Freewill, but Luther argues that her own views are contradictory and absurd.
	  He claims that Diatribe's attack is meaningless because she has no present opponent who can fulfill her demands.

•   Luther's defense of certain testimonies  
	  Luther defends certain testimonies against the view of Freewill, which he argues is negated by Scripture.
	  He claims that Diatribe's host of texts to support Freewill is contemptible in number and that there are only two passages which stand out on this side of the argument.

•   Diatribe's two main passages  
	  Diatribe's two main passages are from Exodus 9:12 and Malachi 1:2-3.
	  Luther argues that these passages do not support Diatribe's view of Freewill, but rather show God's sovereignty and power.

•   Luther's response to Diatribe's passages  
	  Luther will come to Diatribe's reserve to show his forces against her two passages.
	  He claims that if one text of Scripture has conquered Freewill, there is no need for forces, and that the passage from Joshua 23:10 is sufficient to show this.

•   Diatribe's failure to establish Freewill  
	  Luther argues that Diatribe has failed to establish the reality of Freewill, despite her numerous texts and arguments.
	  He claims that her own conclusions contradict the meaning of the texts and that her attack on his views is meaningless.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Diatribe's method of eluding texts  
	  Diatribe resolves opposing texts into tropes to defend her position
	  She chooses to understand simple and clear forms of speech as tropes
	  This allows her to twist words of divine promise and affirmation to suit her argument

•   Diatribe's use of tropes  
	  She demands that others also use tropes to escape the force of imperative and conjunctive law words
	  She pretends that others are prone to making their own escape by discovering tropes
	  She uses examples such as "Stretch out your hand to whomever you will" to illustrate her point

•   Luther's interpretation  
	  Diatribe accuses Luther of introducing a violent and forced interpretation
	  However, she acknowledges that the most approved doctors may also interpret the text in a similar way
	  The dispute is not about the text itself, but about tropes and interpretations

•   The problem with tropes  
	  Tropes and inferences can lead to endless debate and no clear conclusion
	  Scripture may be reduced to a "reed shaken by the winds" or a "sort of Vertumnus"
	  This can lead to nothing certain being affirmed or proved about any article of faith

•   Avoiding tropes  
	  Tropes should be avoided as a destructive poison that Scripture does not compel us to receive
	  Even the great trope-master Origen was criticized for his use of tropes
	  The Arians and new prophets in the author's day were also criticized for their use of tropes

•   The result of false exposition  
	  Many heresies and errors have arisen from false exposition of Scripture
	  These errors often proceed from neglecting the simple use of words and inventing tropes or inferences
	  The simple sense of words is often neglected in favor of more complex and forced interpretations

•   Luther's denial of using tropes  
	  Luther denies having used tropes in his interpretation of "Stretch out" and "Make yourself"
	  He claims that Diatribe has misrepresented him in a published treatise
	  He accuses Diatribe of being distracted and beguiled by her own tropes and inferences

•   The importance of simple sense  
	  The simple, pure, and natural sense of words should be preferred
	  This sense is directed to by the art of grammar and the common use of speech as God created it in man
	  Tropes and inferences should only be admitted when an evident context and some absurdity constrain us to such an interpretation

•   The danger of tropes

	  They can reduce Scripture to a mere reed shaken by the winds

•   The need to avoid tropes

	  Even the great trope-master Origen was criticized for his use of tropes
	  The Arians and new prophets in the author's day were also criticized for their use of tropes

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Diatrib's argument is flawed  
	  Diatrib cannot prove that a trope exists in certain passages of Scripture.
	  Therefore, she is obliged to concede to the opposing argument.

•   Literal interpretation is necessary  
	  The words of Scripture must be understood according to their literal import.
	  This means that the same trope cannot be used to interpret different passages in different ways.

•   Diatrib's interpretations are unproven  
	  Diatrib's interpretation of Moses' saying "I will harden Pharaoh's heart" is unproven.
	  She claims it means "My leniency in bearing with a sinner," but there is no proof for this interpretation.

•   Other interpretations are possible  
	  Jerome and Origen interpreted the same passage differently.
	  Who is to say that their interpretation is correct?

•   Scripture alone is the authority  
	  The author contests Diatrib's interpretations based on the authority of Scripture alone.
	  Human teachers, such as Jerome and Origen, are not authoritative.

•   Diatrib's interpretations are absurd  
	  Diatrib's interpretations lead to absurd conclusions, such as:
		- God hardening Pharaoh's heart means Pharaoh hardens himself through God's leniency.
		- God having mercy means inflicting evil and punishing.

•   Tropes are misused  
	  Diatrib misuses tropes to interpret Scripture.
	  This leads to contradictions, such as God having pity on the children of Israel when they were carried away into Assyria and Babylon.

•   God's actions are misinterpreted  
	  Diatrib misinterprets God's actions, such as:
		- Sending Christ into the world as an act of hardening, not mercy.
		- Destroying Jerusalem and the Jews as an act of mercy, not wrath.

•   Good and bad men are confused  
	  Diatrib's interpretations confuse good and bad men, making it seem as though God's mercy is actually wrath and vice versa.

•   Scripture is distorted  
	  Diatrib's interpretations distort the meaning of Scripture, making it seem as though God's actions are contradictory and absurd.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Critique of Diatribe's definition of hardening  
	  Diatribe defines hardening as God's indulging the wicked with forbearance and kindness.
	  The author argues that this definition is incorrect and that hardening is actually God's visiting and cutting short.

•   God's compassion and hardening  
	  The author argues that God's compassion is not indulging the wicked, but rather visiting and cutting short.
	  God's hardening is not a lack of severity, but rather a perpetual kindness.

•   Erasmus' trope makes nonsense of Moses  
	  Erasmus' trope (definition of hardening) makes Moses' words nonsensical.
	  The author argues that Erasmus' definition is a mere invention and has no worth.

•   God's mercy and severity  
	  The author argues that God's mercy is not immediately chastising and calling the sinner, but rather inviting repentance through severity.
	  God's severity is not a lack of mercy, but rather a means of calling the sinner to repentance.

•   Pharaoh's case  
	  The author argues that Pharaoh's case is an example of God's hardening, not mercy.
	  Pharaoh was afflicted with many signs and plagues, and yet he was not moved to repentance.

•   God's forbearance and kindness  
	  The author argues that God's forbearance and kindness towards Pharaoh were not acts of mercy, but rather a means of hardening his heart.
	  God's kindness took away Pharaoh's plagues, forgave his sins, and restored his blessings, but ultimately did not lead to repentance.

•   Necessity remains  
	  The author argues that even if God's hardening and mercy are admitted in their highest degree, the act of hardening remains.
	  The hardening of which Moses speaks must be of one sort, and what Diatribe is dreaming of must be another.

•   Diatribe's evasion  
	  The author argues that Diatribe is evading the fact that we do everything by God's will alone and by a necessity laid upon us.
	  Diatribe is trying to excuse God from being the author and blameworthy cause of our induration.

•   Man's necessity  
	  The author argues that man nevertheless necessarily serves sin.
	  Man is made better by the forbearance of a long-suffering God, but is necessarily made worse unless through the mercy of God the Spirit is added to it.

•   God's bearing with men  
	  The author argues that God seems to be cruel in bearing with men out of leniency.
	  God is thought to be bearing with men through our representation that he hardens in the exercise of his inscrutable will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Diatribe's critique of God's leniency  
	  Diatribe argues that God's leniency is actually cruel because it allows people to continue in their sinful ways.
	  God could intervene to stop people's calamities, but chooses not to.

•   Freewill's impotency  
	  Diatribe defines Freewill as a power that can will nothing good.
	  This means that Freewill is equally impotent in all men and cannot be divided into different sorts.

•   Rejection of similes  
	  Diatribe rejects similes used by others to describe God's relationship with humanity, such as comparing God's forbearance to the sun and rain.
	  These similes are not to the point and do not accurately describe the relationship between God and humanity.

•   God's role in election  
	  Diatribe argues that if God does not will to give the Spirit, then He does not will to save anyone.
	  This means that men are saved and damned without God's knowing it.

•   God's lack of election  
	  Diatribe argues that God has not separated the saved and the damned by a determined election.
	  Instead, God bestows kindness and pity on all, but this only hardens some and converts others.

•   Men's responsibility  
	  Diatribe argues that men are responsible for their own salvation or damnation.
	  God has left it up to men to determine whether they will be saved or damned.

•   God's inaction  
	  Diatribe describes God as stepping out of the way, allowing men to determine their own fate.
	  This is compared to a potter who makes vessels out of the same lump of clay, some to honor and some to dishonor.

•   Aristotle's similar view  
	  Diatribe notes that Aristotle also describes God as sleeping and allowing men to use and abuse His goodness and severity.

•   Human reason's limitations  
	  Diatribe argues that human reason is limited in its ability to understand God's ways.
	  Instead of reverencing God's secrets, human reason tries to scrutinize them and excuse God.

•   Consequences of human reason's limitations  
	  Diatribe notes that when human reason tries to excuse God, it often ends up vomiting blasphemies instead of offering a single plea in excuse.
	  This leads to a forgetfulness of oneself and a chattering against God and oneself in the same breath.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Diatribe's argument against the concept of God hardening hearts  
	  Diatribe argues that it's absurd to say God hardened Pharaoh's heart to manifest His power through Pharaoh's wickedness.
	  She runs back to Origen's idea that God gave occasion for the induration, but blames Pharaoh for it.

•   Origen's view on God's role in hardening hearts  
	  Origen said God gave occasion for the induration, but didn't make Pharaoh wicked.
	  He believed God beheld all Pharaoh's works and they were good.

•   Diatribe's criticism of Moses' and Paul's words  
	  She says their words are absurd and should be understood in a simple and literal sense.
	  She implies that human reason is offended by the idea of God hardening hearts.

•   Consequences of rejecting the concept of God hardening hearts  
	  Diatribe argues that if we reject this concept, we'll deny all articles of the Christian faith.
	  She says it's the most absurd thing possible and contradicts the Gospel.

•   Diatribe's proposal to invent tropes to avoid the absurdity  
	  She suggests inventing tropes like the Arians to prevent Christ from being God absolutely.
	  She also proposes making Christ out to be a sort of phantom that glided through the virgin like a ray of sun through glass.

•   Limitations of Diatribe's proposed tropes  
	  She admits that these tropes don't get us any further forward and don't serve to evade the absurdity.
	  The absurdity remains in the eye of reason that a just and good God demands impossibilities of Freewill.

•   Diatribe's demand for a comprehensible God  
	  She wants to be able to touch and see how it is that God is just and not cruel.
	  She proposes a God who pities everyone, saves everyone, and doesn't harden anyone.

•   Faith and the Spirit's view on God's goodness  
	  They believe God is good, even if He were to destroy all men.
	  They see no use in elaborate speculations to remove the blame of induration from God to Freewill.

•   Diatribe's vehemence in defending God's goodness  
	  She becomes boisterous and vehement in excusing and defending the just and beneficent God.
	  She's willing to go to great lengths to justify God's actions.

•   Faith and the Spirit's judgment on Diatribe's views  
	  They judge Diatribe's views as blind, deaf, foolish, impious, and sacrilegious in her dealings with God's words and works.
	  They see her as trying to be the judge of God's works and words.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. God's sovereignty and human nature  

  God's sovereignty is not diminished by human sin or weakness.
  Human nature is corrupted and wicked, and this is not a result of God's creation, but rather the result of sin entering the world.

  2. The origin of evil  

  Evil is not created by God, but rather it is a result of sin and corruption.
  God forms and multiplies a nature that has been corrupted by sin, just as a carpenter makes statues out of rotten wood.

  3. God's creation and human nature  

  God's creation is not limited to what is good, but also includes what is evil.
  Many things that appear good to God may appear bad to humans, and vice versa.

  4. God's sovereignty and human will  

  God works all things in all things, and nothing is done without his permission.
  Human will and nature are subject to God's sovereignty, and he can move and actuate all things in all things.

  5. The relationship between God and Satan  

  Satan and the wicked man are not nothing, but rather they have a nature and will that is subject to God's sovereignty.
  Their nature and will are corrupt and averse to God, but they are still creations of God.

  6. God's omnipotence  

  God is omnipotent and can do all things.
  His omnipotence is not limited by human sin or weakness.

  7. The mystery of God's ways  

  God's ways are mysterious and not fully understood by humans.
  What appears good or evil to humans may be seen differently by God.

  8. The role of human reason  

  Human reason is limited and prone to error.
  It is foolish to try to understand God's ways through human reason alone.

  9. The importance of scripture  

  Scripture is the primary source of truth about God's nature and ways.
  It is foolish to rely on human reason or speculation when interpreting scripture.

  10. The need for humility  

  Humans should approach the study of God's nature and ways with humility and reverence.
  It is arrogant to think that humans can fully understand God's ways through human reason alone.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Omnipotence and the Wicked  
	  God acts in wicked people according to what they are and what they are capable of.
	  God's power drives wicked people to do evil, but this does not mean God is evil.
	  God cannot do evil because He is good.
    God's Use of Evil Instruments  
	  God uses evil instruments to accomplish His purposes.
	  The fault lies in the instruments, not in God.
	  God is the impeller of the wicked, but He does not allow them to remain idle.
    The Wicked Man's Condition  
	  The wicked man is a creature of God and is subject to His power.
	  The wicked man cannot will or do good because of his corruption.
	  God's omnipotence makes the wicked man unable to escape God's moving and driving.
    God's Hardening of the Wicked  
	  God cannot relinquish the exercise of His omnipotence because of the wicked man's aversion to Him.
	  The wicked man cannot change his aversion to good will.
	  The wicked man errs and sins perpetually until he is rectified by the Spirit of God.
    Satan's Reign  
	  Satan reigns in peace and keeps his palace in quietness, subject to God's impulse of omnipotence.
	  Satan is the prince of the wicked man.
    The Wicked Man's Focus  
	  The wicked man is occupied with himself and his own matters.
	  He does not inquire after God or care for things that are God's.
	  He seeks his own wealth, glory, wisdom, and power.
    The Wicked Man's Rage  
	  The wicked man is incapable of restraining his rage, desire, and pursuit.
	  He is incapable of putting an end to his existence.
	  His rage is stirred up against his adversary.
    The Gospel's Effect  
	  The Gospel condemns the wicked man's desires for glory and riches.
	  The Gospel provokes the wicked man and hardens him.
	  The Gospel is stronger than the wicked man and conquers him.
    God's Provoking of the Wicked  
	  God provokes the wicked man by saying or doing something contrary to their wishes.
	  This provoking hardens and burdens the wicked man.
	  The wicked man is turned more out of the way and made worse by being resisted and robbed.
    God's Hardening of Pharaoh  
	  God hardened Pharaoh's heart by provoking him with the words of Moses.
	  God did not give Pharaoh the Spirit within, but allowed his wicked nature to grow.
	  Pharaoh's heart was weighed down and his wicked nature was accompanied by vain confidence and contemptuousness.
    Mistakes Prohibited  
	  Do not let mistakes or errors occur in understanding God's ways and purposes.
	  Do not let the wicked man's condition or God's hardening of him be misunderstood.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's relationship with evil  
	  God does not create evil, but rather allows it to exist through human faultiness.
	  God works through evil instruments, but makes good use of them for His glory and our salvation.

•   God's agency and omnipotence  
	  God's agency is a necessary factor in all His creatures, and He allows none of them to have a "holiday".
	  God's omnipotence means He can work through evil instruments, but not through any fault of His own.

•   Satan's will  
	  God finds Satan's will to be evil, but does not create it as evil.
	  God holds onto Satan's will and moves it according to His own purposes.

	  David says that Shimei's curse is from God, but there is no external commandment to curse.
	  God speaks, and it is done; He carries out His will through His eternal word.

•   Pharaoh's hardening  
	  God hardens Pharaoh when he presents his words and works to his wicked and evil will.
	  Pharaoh's will is hardened through his own innate faultiness and natural corruption.

•   God's relationship with Pharaoh  
	  God does not change Pharaoh's will inwardly, but rather presents and imposes His words and works.
	  Pharaoh is puffed up and exalted by his own fancied greatness, and is rendered a proud despiser of Moses.

•   Pharaoh's response to Moses  
	  The more Moses urges and threatens Pharaoh, the more Pharaoh is provoked and aggravated.
	  Pharaoh's evil will is not stirred to action by itself, but is driven by God's omnipotent act.

•   Pharaoh's hardening  
	  Pharaoh's hardening is completed when God presents from without that which naturally irritates and offends his will.
	  Pharaoh cannot avoid being hardened, any more than he can avoid the agency of God's omnipotence.

•   Pharaoh's obstinacy  
	  Pharaoh is made obstinate to a degree that he neither hears nor has any understanding.
	  He is hurried away under the possession of the devil, like one mad and raving.

•   Understanding God's word  
	  We should understand God's word literally, without making excuses for Him or accusing Him of injustice.
	  We should explode the tropes and glosses of men, and see God's word as it truly is.

	  The verse describes the potter's power to create and shape vessels

	  Diatribe overlooks the cause of the comparison and focuses on the words used
	  She is captious and contentious about words, rather than seeking the meaning

•   The meaning of the simile  
	  The meaning of the simile is that the Lord knows those who are his
	  The simile is meant to illustrate the idea that vessels do not prepare themselves, but their master prepares them

•   The force of the simile  
	  The force of the simile is that vessels do not prepare themselves, but their master prepares them
	  This idea is supported by Romans 9:21

•   Diatribe's evasion  
	  Diatribe evades the matter for which Paul uses the simile
	  She focuses on the words used, rather than the meaning and cause of the comparison

•   The indicative verb  
	  The simile is expressed in indicative verbs, not conjunctive verbs
	  This means that the simile is a statement of fact, rather than a conditional statement

•   The purpose of the simile  
	  The purpose of the simile is to illustrate the idea that the Lord knows those who are his
	  The simile is meant to show that the fault is not in the vessel, but in the potter.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Critique of Human Judgment of God  
	  Humans demand that God act according to human law and morality.
	  They question God's justice and goodness if He doesn't follow human rules.

•   Absurdity of Human Judgment  
	  It's absurd to think that God should damn someone who can't avoid deserving it.
	  This absurdity leads humans to question God's mercy and justice.

•   Diatribe's Explanation  
	  Diatribe explains that God's work is like a potter's, making vessels for honor or dishonor based on previous deservings.
	  This explanation is seen as a way to moderate the absurdity of human judgment.

•   Problem with Diatribe's Explanation  
	  If God's work is based on human merits, then why do humans murmur and expostulate?
	  This explanation undermines the power of the potter to do what He pleases.

•   Respect for Merits vs. Power of the Potter  
	  Respect for human merits is at variance with the power and liberty of the potter.
	  This is proven by the parable of the householder who opposes the liberty of his will to the murmurs of his laborers.

•   Exposed by Asking Questions  
	  Why not cavil against the salvation of the saved?
	  If God looks at merits in the damned, then He must also look at merits in the saved.

•   Unjust to Have the Unworthy Crowned  
	  It's equally unjust to have the unworthy be crowned as it is to have the unworthy be punished.
	  God must justify on the ground of previous deservings or else be declared unjust.

•   Human Heart's Reaction  
	  When God saves the unworthy without merit, the human heart does not accuse Him of unfairness.
	  However, when God condemns the undeserving, the human heart sees it as unfair and intolerable.

•   Diatribe's Judgment  
	  Diatribe and her friends do not judge according to equity, but according to whether their interest is affected.
	  They would argue with God for crowning the unworthy just as much as they do for condemning the undeserving.

•   Conclusion  
	  Diatribe's explanation is seen as a way to promote her own personal interest rather than judging God according to equity.
	  The human heart looks at self and its own things in God, rather than at God and His things.

	  Luther argues that Diatribe is not in earnest when pleading this cause
	  He claims that she is not interpreting the text correctly, but rather molding it into a new shape with her own words

•   Diatribe's response to Luther  
	  Diatribe argues that the saying in Proverbs 21:1 has no efficacy in her view
	  She claims that the heart of the king is in the hand of the Lord, but this is not a matter of compulsion, but rather a necessity of immutability

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  God's Inclining of the Heart  

  God's inclining of the heart is a powerful and unavoidable operation
	  It is not a lazy or sleepy thing, but rather a force that cannot be changed or avoided
	  It is the will of God that is being expressed through the heart of man
  This operation is what gives man a will, and it is what hurries along with God's own motion
	  Man's will cannot escape the actuation of the omnipotent God

  The King's Will  

  The king's will is not free, but rather is inclined by God
	  The king's heart is moved by God to evil, but this is not because God is evil, but rather because He allows the king to be driven by his passions
  The king's will is not an exception, but rather a general principle that applies to all men
	  If the king's will cannot escape the actuation of God, then how much less can any other man's will do so?

  Freewill and the Scriptures  

  The Diatribe claims that many testimonies from the Scriptures can be interpreted to support the idea of freewill
	  However, this is a convenient interpretation that ignores the clear meaning of the text
  The Diatribe relies on a clever but flawed interpretation of John 15:5
	  The phrase "without me you can do nothing" is taken to mean "you can do nothing perfectly", rather than "you can do nothing at all"

  The Diatribe's Tactics  

  The Diatribe uses rhetoric and clever wordplay to make its arguments seem convincing
	  However, this is often at the expense of ignoring the clear meaning of the text and its context
  The Diatribe is willing to distort the meaning of the Scriptures in order to support its own views
	  This is evident in its treatment of John 15:5, where it ignores the clear meaning of the text in order to make it seem ambiguous.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Author's Critique of the Opponent  
	  The author is criticizing an opponent who uses a "commodious interpretation" of Scripture.
	  This interpretation involves redefining words to fit one's own views.

•   Commodious Interpretation  
	  The opponent redefines words like "nothing" and "not" to mean "small" or "imperfect".
	  This interpretation is seen as a way to justify heretical views.

•   Examples of Commodious Interpretation  
	  The opponent might interpret "without him was nothing made" as "very little was made".
	  This interpretation is seen as a way to undermine the authority of Scripture.

•   Consequences of Commodious Interpretation  
	  The author argues that this interpretation would lead to a "window of license" for corrupted minds and deceiving spirits.
	  It would undermine the certainty of sacred Scripture.

•   The Author's Position  
	  The author stands firm in the natural and grammatical signification of words.
	  They reject the opponent's commodious interpretation as a way to justify heretical views.

•   The Role of Freewill  
	  The author argues that the human will is not a free agent, but is influenced by God and Satan.
	  They reject the idea that the human will can will good without God's influence.

•   The Nature of Satan's Kingdom  
	  The author argues that Satan's kingdom is a real and powerful force in the world.
	  They reject the idea that Satan's kingdom is a mere "nothing" in men.

•   The Opponent's View of Freewill  
	  The opponent seems to believe that the human will is a free agent, able to will good without God's influence.
	  The author rejects this view as a form of heresy.

•   The Author's Challenge  
	  The author challenges the opponent to prove that their commodious interpretation is correct.
	  They argue that the opponent's views are based on a flawed understanding of Scripture.

•   The Author's Conclusion  
	  The author concludes that the opponent's views are a form of heresy, based on a flawed understanding of Scripture.
	  They argue that the natural and grammatical signification of words is the best way to understand Scripture.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's argument  
	  Luther argues that Satan is a powerful and crafty prince of this world, and that humans are slaves to sin and Satan.
	  Humans cannot will anything but what Satan pleases, and cannot will anything good.

	  Luther argues that the word "nothing" in the phrase "Satan's captive packhorse" must be taken literally, meaning that the person is completely powerless.
	  This is in contrast to Diatribe's argument that "nothing" can be taken to mean "a little".

•   Scripture supports Luther's argument  
	  Luther cites Scripture (John 15:6) to support his argument that a person who does not abide in Christ is cast out and withers.
	  Christ's words explicitly declare that a person who does not abide in him is cast out and withers.

•   The consequences of being cast out  
	  Being cast out and withering means being delivered over to the dominion of the devil and being continually made worse.
	  This is not the same as having power or endeavouring to do something.

•   Diatribe's argument is flawed  
	  Diatribe's argument that "nothing" can be taken to mean "a little" is based on a flawed assumption that words should be taken out of their natural meaning.
	  Luther argues that words should be kept to their natural meaning unless the contrary has been demonstrated.

•   Examples of "nothing"  
	  Luther argues that even if Diatribe had proved that "nothing" can be taken to mean "a little" in some places, it would not affect the argument in this particular case.
	  Diatribe's examples are flawed and do not support her argument.

•   Diatribe's argument is ineffective  
	  Even if Diatribe had proved something, her argument would have been ineffective because she is arguing for a "nothing" that is not a perfect nothing.
	  Her argument is based on a flawed assumption and is therefore not convincing.

•   The importance of considering the subject matter  
	  Luther argues that words must be considered according to the subject matter and with relation to the intention of the speaker.
	  A person never calls something "nothing" when they are in action, but rather when they are talking about the effect of their action.

•   Diatribe's argument is based on imagination  
	  Luther argues that Diatribe's argument is based on her imagination, rather than on any actual common saying or practice.
	  Diatribe takes the liberty of imagining that a certain phrase is a common saying, when in fact it is not.

•   Luther's conclusion  
	  Luther concludes that his argument is based on a clear and natural interpretation of Scripture, while Diatribe's argument is based on a flawed assumption and is therefore not convincing.

	  Luther responds that these concerns are already addressed in his previous writings

	  He rejects the idea of allowing a small degree of power to free will to avoid inconsistencies in Scripture
	  He argues that this approach neither helps nor harms the cause of truth

	  He claims that this is the only way to resolve the inconsistencies in Scripture and avoid the inconveniences of allowing free will
	  He argues that this approach will show that the Scriptures are not inconsistent with themselves

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  Paul's Argument  

  Paul argues that all men are ungodly and unjust, and deserve God's wrath.
	  He claims that even the most eminent individuals, including those with the law, justice, wisdom, and virtues, are ungodly and unjust.
	  He asserts that this is because they do not know what is just in the sight of God.

  The Gospel and Righteousness  

  Paul says that the Gospel reveals a righteousness of salvation that is by faith.
	  He argues that this means that all men are ignorant of righteousness and faith.
	  He claims that this is why God reveals a righteousness of salvation, to show that men are ignorant of what is just.

  The Consequences of Ignorance  

  Paul concludes that all men are unable to will or do any good thing.
	  He argues that this is because they are ignorant of what is just and do not know how to extricate themselves from God's wrath.
	  He claims that this is why men cannot even try to be saved, because they do not know what to do.

  Experience Confirms Paul's Argument  

  Paul says that experience confirms his argument that men are unable to conceive or endure the truth.
	  He points to the fact that even the holiest and most righteous men do not believe in the Gospel.
	  He notes that the Jews, who have been taught by many miracles and prophets, have rejected the Gospel and even persecuted Christ.

  The Failure of Human Effort  

  Paul argues that human effort is unable to achieve righteousness and salvation.
	  He notes that even the most excellent men have neglected to cultivate their free will and have been ignorant of the method of righteousness.
	  He claims that now that the Gospel has been published, men have repelled it with hatred and have been eager to destroy it.

  The Universality of Paul's Argument  

  Paul says that his argument applies to all men, without exception.
	  He claims that this is why he does not need to make distinctions between individuals or nations.
	  He argues that his argument is confirmed by the fact that even the most eminent individuals are ungodly and unjust.

	  The law only shows us our disease of sin, but does not provide a remedy
    Another light is necessary to show the remedy  
	  Another light, the word of the Gospel, is necessary to show the remedy for sin
	  This light displays Christ as the deliverer from all sin and its consequences
    Reason and Freewill are not sufficient  
	  Reason and Freewill are not sufficient to make Christ known
	  They are instead in darkness, needing the light of the law to see their own disease of sin
    The law is ordained for the sake of transgressions  
	  The law was ordained for the sake of transgressions, until the seed comes
	  The law was not given to restrain sin, but to show it and its consequences
    The law does not prove Freewill  
	  The law does not prove the existence or power of Freewill
	  Instead, it shows that Freewill is blind and ignorant, and needs the law to know sin
    The law is a means to show sin, not to justify  
	  The law is a means to show sin, not to justify or save
	  It is a light that shows disease, sin, wickedness, death, hell, and the wrath of God
    The Gospel is the remedy for sin  
	  The Gospel displays Christ as the deliverer from all sin and its consequences
	  It is the light that shows the remedy for sin, not Reason or Freewill.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  The Necessity of the Law  

  The law is necessary to make sin known, so that people may be humbled and seek grace.
	  Without the law, people would not know the extent of their sin.
	  The law is necessary to reveal the baseness and vastness of human sin.
  The law is necessary to make people aware of their sin, so that they may seek remedy and hope.
	  The law is like a physician who diagnoses and treats sin.

  The Problem of Freewill  

  If people do not know what sin is, how can they strive for righteousness?
	  If people do not know what righteousness is, how can they strive for it?
  The concept of freewill is problematic, as it implies that people can choose to be righteous without the law.
	  The law is necessary to reveal the righteousness of God, which is independent of human works.

  The Righteousness of God  

  The righteousness of God is manifested without the law, being witnessed by the law and the prophets.
	  This righteousness is available to all who believe in Jesus Christ.
  The righteousness of God is not obtained through human works or the law, but through faith in Jesus Christ.
	  This is the first thunderbolt against freewill.

  The Independence of Righteousness  

  The righteousness of God is perfectly independent of the works of the law.
	  This means that the works of the law have no worth or power to obtain righteousness.
  The endeavor of freewill is absolutely nothing, as it is unable to obtain righteousness through its own efforts.
	  The word "without" in Romans 3:21-25 clears away all moral good and righteousness.

  The Futility of Freewill  

  Even if freewill were able to make progress towards good works or moral righteousness, it would still not advance towards the righteousness of God.
	  The righteousness of God is not obtained through human efforts or endeavors.
  The endeavor of freewill would be futile, even if it were able to advance towards the holiness of angels.
	  This is the second thunderbolt against freewill.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Paul's Argument  
	  Paul argues that the power of free will is different from faith in Christ
	  He denies that anything outside of faith in Christ is righteous before God

•   Righteousness and Sin  
	  According to Paul, everything done by humans is either righteousness or sin
	  Righteousness is done in faith, while sin is done without faith

•   The Ungodly  
	  The ungodly sin against God in everything they do
	  They use God's creations wickedly and ungratefully, without giving glory to God

•   All Have Sinned  
	  Paul says that all have sinned and come short of the glory of God
	  There is no exception or distinction between individuals

•   The Glory of God  
	  The glory of God is understood as the favor and acceptance of God
	  It is the glory that believers have in God, and which they can boast about

•   The Faithful  
	  The faithful have confidence in the glory of God and know that their actions are pleasing to Him
	  They can say with certainty that their endeavors are well-pleasing to God

•   The Unbelieving  
	  The unbelieving are devoid of the glory of God and lack confidence in their actions
	  They do not know with certainty whether their actions are pleasing to God

•   Conscience and Unbelief  
	  The conscience of the unbelieving is a witness against them, proving their unbelief
	  They doubt the favor of God and do not believe that their actions are pleasing to Him

•   The Crime of Unbelief  
	  The crime of unbelief is to doubt the favor of God
	  It is necessary to believe with the most assured faith that God favors us

•   The Testimony of Conscience  
	  The testimony of the conscience of the unbelieving proves that they are sinners
	  They are convicted by their own conscience of their unbelief and sin

	  However, this is not consistent with their teachings, which imply that individuals can earn merit through their efforts.

	  The defenders of Freewill are worse than the Pelagians because they deny merit while still implying that individuals can earn it through their efforts.
