	  The defenders of Freewill are led into error by their desire to dissent from the Pelagians.
	  They deny merit while still implying that individuals can earn it through their efforts, which is a more Pelagian position than they admit.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Paul's View of Justification  
	  Paul affirms that justification is a free gift from God
	  He rejects the idea that justification is earned through works or merit

•   Pelagians and Sophists  
	  Paul criticizes the Pelagians and Sophists for their views on justification
	  They believed that justification was earned through good works or a small amount of merit

•   Free Justification  
	  Paul asserts that justification is free and not earned through works
	  He says that justification is a gift, not a reward for good works

•   No Personal Worthiness  
	  Paul says that justification is not based on personal worthiness
	  He quotes Romans 11:6, "If by grace, then it is no longer by works; otherwise grace is no longer grace"

•   Condemnation of Works  
	  Paul says that all works, both great and small, are condemned
	  He excepts none, but condemns all equally

•   The Fathers Overlooked Paul  
	  The early Fathers overlooked Paul's clear and explicit words on justification
	  They were blind to Paul's teaching on the free gift of justification

•   Paul's Argument  
	  Paul makes his boast of grace in opposition to works
	  He says that justification is free and not earned through works

•   Abraham's Example  
	  Paul uses the example of Abraham to illustrate justification by faith
	  He says that Abraham was justified by faith, not by works

•   Two Righteousnesses of Abraham  
	  Paul distinguishes between two righteousnesses of Abraham
	  One is a righteousness of works, which is moral and civil, but not before God

•   Justification by Faith  
	  Paul says that justification is by faith, not by works
	  He quotes Romans 4:2, "Abraham believed God, and it was counted to him for righteousness"

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The Futility of Works  
	  No one can achieve righteousness through their own works.
	  All works, desires, and endeavors of free will are accounted as ungodly, unjust, and wicked in the sight of God.
    The Righteousness of Faith  
	  The other righteousness is that of faith, which does not stand in any works but in God's favor and accounting of us through grace.
	  Faith is counted as righteousness, not because of our works, but according to the purpose of God's grace.
    Imputation of Righteousness  
	  Righteousness is imputed to the non-worker, if he but believes.
	  It is not possible for free will to escape or slip away from this reckoning.
    The Comparison of Worker and Non-Worker  
	  Paul compares the worker and the non-worker, leaving no middle ground.
	  Righteousness is not imputed to the worker, but to the non-worker who believes.
    The Condemnation of Works  
	  If righteousness is not imputed to the worker, then their works are nothing but sins, wicked, and ungodly acts in the sight of God.
	  No Sophist can argue that a wicked person's work may not be wicked.
    The Purpose of Grace  
	  The purpose of God's grace is to take away free will's ability to do anything else except sin and be damned.
	  If grace comes from God's purpose or predestination, then it comes by necessity, not by our pains or endeavors.
    The Power of the Law  
	  The law is the strength of sin, manifesting and not taking it away, making the conscience guilty before God and threatening wrath.
	  This is what is meant by the saying, "The law works wrath."
    The Election of God  
	  If God promised grace before the law, then it does not come from our works or the law; otherwise, the promise would be nothing.
	  If works have any efficacy, then faith would be nothing.
    Original Sin  
	  We are all under sin and damnation through the one offense of Adam.
	  We cannot attempt anything that is not sin and which is not damnable.
    The Condemnation of Free Will  
	  Free will is comprehended among all, with the others, in the condemnation of sin.
	  We could not have sinned and been condemned by Adam's sin, unless it were our sin.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Critique of Paul's Writings  
	  Luther argues that Paul's writings are often misunderstood and misinterpreted.
	  He believes that Paul's words are clear and explicit, but are often ignored or distorted.

•   Luther's Objection to Freewill  
	  Luther objects to the idea of freewill, which he believes is contradicted by Paul's writings.
	  He argues that Paul's use of universal terms such as "all," "none," and "not" is clear and explicit.

•   Luther's Interpretation of Paul's Words  
	  Luther interprets Paul's words as meaning that without faith in Christ, there is nothing but sin and damnation.
	  He believes that Paul's use of contrast and distribution in his writings reinforces this interpretation.

•   Luther's View of Paul's Authority  
	  Luther is astonished that Paul's clear and explicit words have been ignored or distorted.
	  He believes that Paul's writings are a powerful argument against the idea of freewill.

•   Luther's Critique of Luther's View  
	  Luther's own view of Paul's writings is that they are clear and explicit, and that they contradict the idea of freewill.
	  He believes that his own interpretation of Paul's words is the correct one.

•   Paul's Division of Humanity  
	  Paul divides humanity into two parts: flesh and Spirit.
	  Those who are after the flesh are carnal, and those who are after the Spirit are spiritual.

•   Luther's Interpretation of Paul's Division  
	  Luther believes that Paul's division of humanity is clear and explicit.
	  He argues that those who do not have the Spirit are necessarily in the flesh and under Satan.

•   Luther's Objection to Freewill in the Carnal  
	  Luther objects to the idea that the carnal can please God through their own endeavour and power.
	  He believes that Paul's words are clear and explicit on this point.

•   Luther's Promise to Confute Freewill  
	  Luther promises to confute the idea of freewill through a perpetual commentary on Paul's writings.
	  He believes that his interpretation of Paul's words is the correct one, and that it will ultimately prevail.

•   Luther's View of the Gift of God's Spirit  
	  Luther believes that the gift of God's Spirit is necessary to bring people to the correct understanding of Paul's words.
	  He acknowledges that it is not within his power to bring people to this understanding, but rather it is a gift of God's Spirit.

•   The Mind of the Flesh is Death

	  It is hostile to God (Rom 8:7)

	  The flesh is death itself, enmity itself (Rom 8:7)
	  It cannot obey God (Rom 8:7)
	  It is weak and disobedient (Rom 8:3)

•   Origen's View of Affection  
	  Origen believed in three types of affection: flesh, soul, and spirit
	  The soul is the middle ground between flesh and spirit
	  Paul rejects this view and calls the flesh "death" (Rom 8:6)

•   The Flesh is Dead  
	  The highest virtues of the best men are "in the flesh" (Rom 8:6)
	  They are dead, enemies of God, and not subject to the law of God (Rom 8:6)

•   Christ's View of the Flesh  
	  A corrupt tree cannot bring forth good fruit (Matt 7:18)
	  An evil person cannot speak good things (Matt 12:34)
	  We are evil and cannot do good, even in giving good things (Matt 7:11)

•   The Nature of Sin  
	  Sinners are corrupt trees that bear corrupt fruit (Rom 6:20)
	  Sinners are not justified and are still in their sins (Rom 6:20)

•   Freewill is a Servant of Sin

	  It does not do good and cannot attempt to do good (Rom 6:20)

	  The Gentiles did not seek God, but God was found by them (Isa 65:1)
	  They did not inquire after God, but God was made manifest to them (Isa 65:1)

•   The Example of the Jews  
	  The Jews sought righteousness with great pains and endeavours (Rom 9:32)
	  They were disappointed and did not attain to righteousness (Rom 9:32)

•   The Futility of Freewill  
	  The Gentiles, who were filled with impiety, received righteousness freely (Rom 9:30)
	  The Jews, who sought righteousness with great pains, were disappointed (Rom 9:30)
	  This shows that Freewill endeavours in vain and often turns bad into worse (Rom 9:30)

•   John's View of Freewill

	  John uses the word "world" to refer to the whole human race, without exception
	  He means that whatever he says about the world is meant to describe the state of humanity in general

•   John's Critique of Freewill  
	  John says that the world lies in wickedness and is controlled by Satan
	  He says that all that is in the world is the lust of the flesh, the lust of the eyes, and the pride of life
	  He warns against loving the world

•   Christ's Relationship to the World  
	  Christ says that he has taken his followers out of the world and constituted them as his own
	  This suggests that Christ's followers are no longer part of the world in its current state

•   The Power of Freewill  
	  If Freewill were truly powerful, John would have moderated his language to exclude those who strive for good
	  Instead, John's language suggests that Freewill is responsible for the world's wickedness

•   The Birth of Sons of God  
	  John says that those who receive Christ are given the power to become sons of God
	  This is in contrast to those who are born of blood, will of the flesh, or will of man

•   The Meaning of "Bloods"  
	  John means the Jews, who boasted of their descent from Abraham and the Fathers
	  They had a mind to be sons of the kingdom because of their ancestry

•   The Meaning of "Will of the Flesh"  
	  John means the pains and efforts that people exercise in law-works
	  This refers to carnal persons who do not have the Spirit

•   The Meaning of "Will of Man"  
	  John means the pains that mankind in general takes to find favor with God
	  This refers to people who try to find God through human means, whether under the law or without it

•   The Implication for Freewill  
	  If people are not born of the flesh, nor trained by the law, nor obtained by any human discipline, but are born again of God
	  Then Freewill is of no avail, and people are not made sons of God through their own efforts.

	  The advocates of Freewill deny the Lord Christ who bought us, as evidenced by their own life and practice
	  This is incompatible with the teaching of Scripture, which emphasizes the importance of faith and the merit of Christ in salvation.

•   The concept of salvation is unique and exclusive

	  This truth is not something that humanity could have discovered on its own

	  This means that humanity's own powers are not sufficient to seek or understand the things that belong to salvation

	  On the other hand, humanity claims to have a power of free will that can know and apply itself to salvation

	  The ignorance of free will is actually a knowledge of Christ, which is the way, the truth, and the life

	  Whatever is not Christ is not the truth, but falsehood

	  This means that the assertions of free will are actually a form of blasphemy against Christ.

    The Consequences of Free Will

	  This results in a delusion of monstrous fictions.
    The Baptist's Statement (Again)  
	  The author quotes the Baptist's statement that "a man cannot receive anything unless it has been given to him from heaven."
	  The author argues that this statement undermines the doctrine of free will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Nature of Man  
	  The text is not about the natural constitution of man, but about the relationship between man and God.
	  The question is whether man has free will towards God, or if God has free will over man.

•   Baptist View  
	  The Baptists believe that man can receive nothing unless it is given to him from heaven.
	  This implies that free will is nothing, and that man is entirely dependent on God.

•   Jesus' Teachings  
	  Jesus teaches that those who are of the earth speak earthly things, while those who come from heaven speak heavenly things (John 3:31).
	  This implies that free will is earthly and limited to earthly concerns.

•   Jesus' Words in John 8  
	  Jesus says that the people to whom he spoke had free will, but they were still of the world (John 8:23).
	  This suggests that free will is not enough to overcome the influence of the world.

•   Jesus' Words in John 6  
	  Jesus says that no one comes to him unless the Father draws them (John 6:44).
	  This implies that even the will to believe is not entirely free, but is drawn by God.

•   Augustine's Simile  
	  Augustine uses a simile to describe the relationship between God's drawing and man's free will.
	  The simile is that God draws man just as a shepherd draws a sheep with a branch.

•   The Role of the Spirit  
	  The Spirit is necessary for man to understand and believe in God.
	  Without the Spirit, the word of God is heard in vain.

•   The Ungodly  
	  The ungodly do not come to God, even when they have heard the word, unless the Father draws them.
	  This implies that the ungodly are not capable of making a decision to believe in God on their own.

•   The Godly  
	  The godly are able to follow God because they have been drawn by the Father.
	  This implies that the godly have a special relationship with God that is not based on their own free will.

•   The Sin of Unbelief  
	  The sin of unbelief is not just a matter of not believing in Christ, but of not having the will to believe.
	  This implies that free will is not entirely free, but is influenced by God's drawing.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Scripture preaches Christ through comparison and opposition  
	  The Scriptures represent everything that does not have the Spirit of Christ as the subject of Satan, ungodliness, error, darkness, sin, and the wrath of God.
	  This is evident in the numerous testimonies that speak of Christ, which all fight against the concept of Freewill.

•   The concept of Freewill is condemned by Scripture  
	  Not a single jot or tittle of Scripture will remain that does not condemn the dogma of Freewill.
	  This is a fundamental aspect of Christian doctrine, known and confessed by all Christians.

•   The existence of two kingdoms: Satan's and Christ's  
	  There are two kingdoms in the world, which are most adverse to each other.
	  Satan reigns in one kingdom, and is called the Prince of this world and the God of this age.
	  Christ reigns in the other kingdom, and his kingdom is continually resisting and fighting with that of Satan.

•   Translation into Christ's kingdom is by divine power  
	  We are translated into Christ's kingdom not by our own power, but by the grace of God.
	  This is evident in the fact that we are delivered from this present wicked age and snatched out of the hands of the power of darkness.

•   The knowledge of the two kingdoms is sufficient to refute Freewill  
	  The knowledge and confession of the two kingdoms, as perpetually fighting against each other with such might and resolution, would be sufficient to refute the dogma of Freewill.
	  This is because we are compelled to serve in the kingdom of Satan, unless we are rescued from it by divine power.

•   Common believers know and confess the two kingdoms  
	  The common believers know and abundantly confess the two kingdoms by their proverbs, prayers, efforts, and whole life.
	  This is evident in their daily experiences and struggles with sin and the power of darkness.

•   The conflict between flesh and spirit is a mighty one  
	  In Romans 7 and Galatians 5, Paul teaches that the conflict between flesh and spirit is a mighty one in the sanctified and godly.
	  This conflict is not limited to gross affections, but also includes the works of the flesh, such as heresy, idolatry, dissensions, contentions, and mischiefs.

•   The flesh fights against God in the ungodly  
	  If the flesh maintains a conflict against the spirit in the saints, then much more will it fight against God in the ungodly and their Freewill.
	  This is evident in Romans 8, which calls it "enmity" against God.

•   The argument from the conflict between flesh and spirit  
	  This argument is a strong one against Freewill, and has not been adequately addressed by opponents.
	  If the flesh fights against the spirit in the saints, then how would it endeavour after good in those who are serving under Satan in the old man?

•   The unwillingness to have Freewill  
	  The author would be unwilling to have Freewill given to him, or to have anything left in his own hand that might enable him to endeavour after salvation.
	  This is because he would not be strong enough to maintain his standing and keep his hold of it, and would be compelled to toil forever uncertainly and to fight as one who beats the air.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Salvation  
	  God has taken salvation out of human hands and received it into His own hands.
	  He has promised to save us by His own grace and mercy, not by our own work or running.
	  We are therefore at ease and certain of our salvation because God is faithful and will not lie to us.

•   God's Power and Greatness  
	  God is great and powerful, so that no number of devils or adversities can wear Him out or pluck us out of His hand.
	  No one can pluck us out of God's hand, because His Father who gave us to Him is greater than all.

•   The Problem of Unsaved People  
	  If all are not saved, some are indeed saved, and many are.
	  We are fearlessly sure that we please God not by the merit of our own work, but by the favor of His mercy which He has promised us.

•   The Difficulty of Maintaining God's Mercy and Justice  
	  It is difficult to maintain the mercy and equity of God in the face of the undeserving, namely ungodly men.
	  These men are compelled by the necessity of their nature to sin and perish.

•   Honoring God's Mercy and Wisdom  
	  We must honor and reverence the exceeding great mercy of God in His dealings with those whom He justifies and saves.
	  We must make some concession to His divine wisdom, believing Him to be just, even when He seems unjust to us.

•   The Incomprehensibility of God's Justice  
	  God's justice is incomprehensible and inaccessible to human reason.
	  Paul cries out, "O the depth of the riches both of the wisdom and knowledge of God, how incomprehensible are His judgments and His ways unsearchable."

•   The Limitations of Human Knowledge and Power  
	  What is man compared with God? What is our power, strength, wisdom, knowledge, and substance compared with His?
	  Our everything is absolutely nothing when compared with God's everything.

•   The Arrogance of Human Judgment  
	  We pull and haul at God's justice and judgment, arrogating too much to our own judgment.
	  We should say that our judgment is nothing if compared with the divine judgment.

•   The Conviction of Human Reason  
	  Reason is compelled by conviction to acknowledge that she is foolish and rash in not allowing the judgment of God to be incomprehensible.
	  We confess all the other properties of God to be incomprehensible, but not His judgment.

•   The Promise of God's Justice  
	  God has promised us that after He has revealed His glory, it will come to pass that all of us will both see and feel that He has been and is just.
	  We will then understand that His justice is not unjust, but rather, it is divine justice.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Problem of Evil  
	  The wicked often prosper, while the good are afflicted.
	  This seems unjust and raises questions about God's nature.

•   The Epicureans and Aristotle  
	  They denied the existence of God and attributed events to chance.
	  They believed it would be painful to see so much evil and injustice.

•   The Prophets and Demosthenes  
	  They believed in God's existence and were tempted by the idea of God's injustice.
	  Demosthenes and Cicero, despite their good deeds, received a wretched death.

•   The Light of the Gospel  
	  The Gospel teaches that the wicked may flourish in their bodies, but perish in their souls.
	  This provides a solution to the problem of evil.

•   The Three Lights  
	  The light of nature cannot explain why the good are afflicted and the wicked prosper.
	  The light of grace resolves this question by showing that the fault lies not in the wicked man, but in an unjust God.

•   The Light of Glory  
	  When the light of glory arrives, it will show that God's judgment is just and incomprehensible.
	  This will be manifest in the reality of God's majesty.

•   The Sum of the Argument  
	  The author believes that God foreknows and predestines everything.
	  This leads to the conclusion that there can be no such thing as free will in man or angel.

•   The Role of Satan  
	  Satan is the Prince of this world, perpetually plotting against the kingdom of Christ.
	  He does not let his captives go unless driven out by divine power.

•   The Implications of Predestination  
	  If God foreknows and predestines everything, then nothing is done outside of his will.
	  This leads to the conclusion that there can be no free will.

•   The Conclusion  
	  The author believes that he has said enough to satisfy the pious mind.
	  He is prepared to lead the cause further, if needed.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Luther's Argument  
	  Luther argues that humanity is inherently evil and cannot turn itself to good without God's grace.
	  He cites examples of the Jews and Gentiles to illustrate this point.
    The Role of Grace  
	  Luther emphasizes that without God's grace, humanity will only do evil.
	  He argues that this is evident in the experiences of the Jews and Gentiles.
    The Nature of Humanity  
	  Luther believes that humanity is completely undone by sin.
	  He argues that if Christ redeemed humanity, then the whole person was redeemed, not just a part of it.
    Luther's Admonition to Erasmus  
	  Luther asks Erasmus to submit to the authority of Scripture and tradition.
	  He commends Erasmus for attacking the heart of the issue, rather than peripheral topics.
    The Importance of Scripture  
	  Luther emphasizes the importance of understanding Scripture and the teachings of the Fathers.
	  He suggests that Erasmus may not have a sufficient understanding of these sources.
    Luther's Desire for Erasmus to Focus on Literature  
	  Luther encourages Erasmus to focus on his literary pursuits, rather than getting involved in theological debates.
	  He acknowledges that Erasmus has served him well in this area.
    Luther's Humility  
	  Luther expresses humility and acknowledges that he is not equal to Erasmus in terms of learning and eloquence.
	  He asks God to make Erasmus his superior in this area.
    The Role of God in Human Affairs  
	  Luther believes that God is sovereign and can instruct anyone, regardless of their background or education.
	  He suggests that Erasmus may not know Christ, and therefore has failed in his aim.
    The Importance of Humility  
	  Luther emphasizes the importance of humility and recognizing one's limitations.
	  He suggests that Erasmus may not be as knowledgeable as he thinks he is.
    Luther's Conclusion  
	  Luther concludes by asking God to shine his light on Erasmus and make him a vessel of honor and glory.
	  He ends with a prayer, asking God to make Erasmus his superior in the area of theology.

Here are 14 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Martin Luther's Background  
	  Born in 1483 in Eisleben, Germany
	  Studied law and philosophy at the University of Erfurt
	  Became a monk and priest in the Augustinian order
    Luther's Views on Salvation  
	  Believed that salvation comes through faith in Christ, not through good works
	  Fought against the idea that people can earn salvation through their own efforts
	  Preached that people should not fear God, but rather trust in Christ's righteousness
    Luther's Sermon in Dresden  
	  Preached that people should not fear salvation, but rather trust in Christ's promise
	  Emphasized the doctrine of predestination, which holds that God has chosen certain people for salvation
	  Argued that the doctrine of predestination is not a reason to fear God, but rather a reason to trust in Christ
    Luther's Correspondence  
	  Wrote to a friend in 1516, expressing his desire to know what his friend's soul was doing
	  Discussed the importance of trusting in Christ's righteousness, rather than one's own efforts
    Key Figures in Luther's Life  
	  Pope Leo X (1475-1510), who was born Giovanni di Lorenzo de' Medici
	  Johann Tetzel, a Dominican friar who was a vocal supporter of indulgences
	  Thomas Cajetan, a Dominican theologian and opponent of Luther
	  Emperor Maximilian I, who was the King of the Germans and Holy Roman Emperor
    Important Events in Luther's Life  
	  1517: Luther wrote his 95 Theses, which challenged the practice of selling indulgences
	  1519: Luther was challenged to the Leipzig Debate by Johann Eck
	  1521: Luther published his treatise on the Lord's Supper, which argued that the bread and wine of the Eucharist are the true body and blood of Christ
    Other Key Figures  
	  Andreas von Karlstadt, a German Protestant theologian who was a friend and ally of Luther
	  Philipp Melanchthon, a German author and theologian who was a friend and ally of Luther
	  Karl von Miltitz, a liaison between the papal court and Elector Frederick the Wise, who met with Luther in 1519 and negotiated a settlement.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Silence and Reconciliation  
	  Luther's silence was contingent on the silence of his opponents, Johann Tetzel and Albert of Mainz.
	  Luther's statements at the Leipzig Debate made reconciliation impossible.

•   Luther's Treatises  
	  Luther published three treatises in 1520:
		- To the Christian Nobility of the German Nation
		- On the Babylonian Captivity of the Church
		- On the Freedom of a Christian

•   Miltitz's Investigation  
	  Miltitz investigated Tetzel and accused him of fraud and embezzlement.
	  Miltitz was later discredited and his accusations dismissed.

•   Luther's Defect  
	  Luther's statement on the believer's union with Christ was defective.
	  He did not mark or discern the origin and foundation of the union.

•   Frederick the Wise  
	  Frederick III, Elector of Saxony, worked for constitutional reform of the Holy Roman Empire.
	  He protected Martin Luther after Luther was placed under the imperial ban in 1521.

•   Frederick's Role in Securing Luther's Safety  
	  Frederick welcomed Luther to the Wartburg castle, where Luther translated the Bible into German.
	  Frederick appointed Luther and Philipp Melanchthon to the University of Wittenberg.

•   Luther's View on a Future Council  
	  Luther acknowledged the authority of a future council, but maintained that it must be legally convened.
	  He believed that the decisions of the council must be regulated by the word of God.

•   Luther's Principles  
	  Luther's principles emphasized the authority of Scripture and the importance of individual conscience.
	  He believed that the testimony of Scripture was only valid to his conscience if it coincided with the general decision.

•   Johann the Steadfast  
	  Johann the Steadfast was Elector of Saxony from 1525 to 1532.
	  He organized the Lutheran Church in the Electorate of Saxony, aided by Martin Luther.

•   Maurice, Duke of Saxony  
	  Maurice betrayed the Protestant Faith and assured his father-in-law, Philip of Hesse, that he would not be imprisoned if he surrendered to the emperor.
	  Maurice was promoted to Elector of Saxony and tried to reintroduce Catholicism into the Protestant territories.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Maurice's Rise to Power  
	  Seized the opportunity to raise an army
	  Signed compacts with France and Germany's Protestant princes against the Habsburgs
	  Signed a treaty with Henry II of France to wage a campaign against Emperor Charles V

•   Luther's Disguise  
	  Allowed his beard and hair to grow
	  Assumed a knightly sort of dress
	  Passed for a country gentleman under the name of Yonker George

•   Veit Ludwig von Seckendorff  
	  Chancellor of Saxe-Zeitz and founding chancellor of the University of Halle
	  Scholar of the Reformation and supporter of Pietists
	  Wrote a scholarly history of the Reformation to refute Louis Maimbourg's Histoire du Lutheranisme

•   Justus Jonas  
	  German theologian and reformer
	  Friend of John Lange, a Greek scholar at Luther's Augustinian house in Erfurt
	  Took sides with Luther after the Leipzig Debate and was deposed as canon at St. Severi in Erfurt

•   Luther's Theology  
	  Maintained that the best acts of the best men have the nature of sin
	  Defended himself against charges of heresy

•   Ithuriel  
	  A cherub in John Milton's Paradise Lost
	  Dispatched by Gabriel to locate Satan in the Garden of Eden
	  Touches Satan with his spear, causing him to resume his proper likeness

•   Leander Van Ess  
	  German theologian and opponent of Luther
	  Criticized Luther's work, but faced no opposition from Roman Catholics

•   Jerome Emser  
	  German antagonist of Luther
	  Initially sided with the reformers, but wanted a practical and moral reformation of the clergy
	  Was attacked by Luther for his views

•   Luther's Character  
	  Was obstinate, fierce, and contemptuous, but also had "bowels of mercies"
	  Was not unkind, rash, or implacable
	  Weighed his words and deeds deliberately

•   Luther's Preaching Style  
	  Had one Gospel for all, and told it all to all
	  Had powers of language, facility of illustration, and simplicity of expression that made him intelligible and affecting to the most illiterate

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Conquest of Constantinople  
	  The Ottoman Army conquered the capital of the Eastern Roman Empire, Constantinople, in 1453.
	  This event had significant consequences for the Eastern Roman Empire and the spread of Christianity.

•   The Great Defenders of the Faith  
	  Zuingle and Oecolampadius were two prominent defenders of the faith who opposed Luther's views.
	  They argued that the real presence of Christ in the Eucharist was not supported by Scripture.

•   Zuingle's Commentary  
	  Zuingle wrote a commentary on true and false religion, published in 1525.
	  He argued that the doctrine of the real presence was rooted in error and needed to be rooted out.

•   Oecolampadius' Treatise  
	  Oecolampadius wrote a treatise on the genuine meaning of Christ's words, "This is my body."
	  He argued that the Fathers of the Church had not held to a literal interpretation of these words.

•   Gibbon's Conversion  
	  The historian Edward Gibbon was converted back to the Protestant faith by arguments similar to those of Zuingle and Oecolampadius.
	  He argued that the text of Scripture that seemed to support the real presence was weak and the real presence itself was disproven by three of the senses.

•   Palliation  
	  Palliation refers to the act of causing an offense to seem less serious.
	  This concept is relevant to the discussion of the real presence and the arguments of Zuingle and Oecolampadius.

•   Calumnious Fierceness  
	  Zuingle and Oecolampadius were accused of being calumnious and fierce in their opposition to Luther.
	  They argued that their opponents were using fallacious reasoning to support the doctrine of the real presence.

•   Miracles and the Real Presence  
	  Zuingle argued that the real presence of Christ in the Eucharist was not a greater miracle than the diffusion of the soul through the body.
	  He argued that the glorified body of Christ could be present in many places at once, just as the soul is present in every part of the body.

•   The Voice of a Man  
	  Zuingle argued that the voice of a man can penetrate the hearts of multitudes of men.
	  He argued that this was a greater miracle than the real presence of Christ in the Eucharist.

•   The Sacramentarian Pestilence  
	  Zuingle argued that the doctrine of the real presence was a pestilence that was spreading and causing harm.
	  He prayed for relief from the "lassitude" that was possessing him, which he attributed to the divine displeasure.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Disagreement with Sacramentarians  
	  Luther disagreed with the Sacramentarians, who believed in a spiritual presence of Christ in the Lord's Supper.
	  He saw their views as a "delusion of Satan" and a "pestilentious, sacrilegious heresy".

•   Luther's Treatise Against Sacramentarians  
	  Luther wrote a treatise against the Sacramentarians, titled "Against the Fanatical Spirits or the Sacramentarians".
	  The treatise was written in German and published in February or March.

•   The Marburg Conference  
	  In 1529, a conference was held at Marburg to promote mutual understanding and peace between Lutherans and Sacramentarians.
	  The Sacramentarians acknowledged that the body of Christ was present in the Lord's Supper, but in a spiritual manner.

•   Zwingli's Role in the Conference  
	  Zwingli, a Swiss divine, participated in the conference and expressed a desire to agree with Luther.
	  However, Luther proved to be unyielding and intolerant, refusing to budge on the issue of the Lord's Supper.

•   Luther's Refusal to Compromise  
	  Luther refused to compromise on the issue of the Lord's Supper, even when faced with opposition from the Sacramentarians.
	  He saw himself as a champion of truth and refused to yield to those who disagreed with him.

•   The Nature of the Disagreement  
	  The disagreement between Luther and the Sacramentarians was not just about theology, but also about personality and temperament.
	  Luther's vehemence and intolerance were matched by the Sacramentarians, who were also passionate and argumentative.

•   The Role of Satan  
	  Luther saw the disagreement as a result of Satan's influence, who was using the Sacramentarians to spread heresy and division.
	  He believed that the Sacramentarians were deceived by Satan and were not acting in good faith.

•   The Importance of Charity  
	  Despite the disagreement, Luther advocated for charity and mutual respect between the parties.
	  He believed that even in the midst of disagreement, Christians should strive to maintain a friendly and peaceful relationship.

•   The Legacy of the Disagreement  
	  The disagreement between Luther and the Sacramentarians had significant consequences for the Protestant Reformation.
	  It led to the development of different theological traditions and the emergence of new denominations.

•   The Character of Luther  
	  Luther's character was marked by his passion, intolerance, and refusal to compromise.
	  He was a complex figure, driven by a strong sense of conviction and a desire to defend the truth.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Purpose of Reviving Luther's Theology  
	  The author believes that Luther's theology is essential for understanding God and humanity.
	  The author argues that modern theologians have decompounded God and man, making it difficult to understand their true nature.

•   The Problem with Modern Theology  
	  Modern theologians, such as Anselm, Peter Abelard, and Thomas Aquinas, have decompounded God and man.
	  They have imagined a God with attributes, rather than understanding God's essence.

•   The Importance of the Bible  
	  The author believes that the Bible is the word of God and must be taken for granted.
	  The Bible provides a clear understanding of God's nature and humanity's relationship with Him.

•   The Nature of Christ  
	  Christ's essence is God-manhood, being both God and man in one substance.
	  Christ's manhood is generated by the Holy Ghost and is a pure, spotless, and sinless spirit.

•   The Relationship between Christ's Body and Manhood  
	  Christ's body is his connecting link with humanity, being part of the stock of Adam.
	  Christ is not just a man, but the Son of Adam, who has his being through and of the stock of Adam.

•   The Commandment to Repent and Believe  
	  The commandment to repent and believe is congruent with God's manifestation of Himself in Christ.
	  This commandment is a manifestation of God's love, grace, and mercy.

•   The Relationship between the Law and the Gospel  
	  The law is a perfect transcript of creation man's duty, in enigma.
	  The law is a typicalemblem of Christ as the unblemished Lamb and of the law of the Spirit of life laid up in Him.

•   The Importance of Understanding God's Nature  
	  Understanding God's nature is essential for understanding humanity's relationship with Him.
	  The author believes that Luther's theology provides a clear understanding of God's nature.

•   The Problem with Human Dealing with God  
	  Humans have no right to deal with themselves as if they were God.
	  The author argues that humans must recognize their place in relation to God.

•   The Importance of the Gospel  
	  The gospel is the manifestation of God's love, grace, and mercy.
	  The gospel is the means by which God deals with humanity, according to justice and equity.

•   Luther's Teachings

	  The author cites the example of the fourth Article of Faith, which has been clarified by new understandings of the nature of Christ's body.

	  The author argues that the Reformation did not absorb the spiritual Sun, but rather allowed its rays to continue to shine.

	  The author argues that the Spirit does not teach and show all truth at once, but rather reveals it in degrees over time.
	  The author notes that the Bible and other records show that there has been a progression in God's teaching, from Abraham to Moses to David to the prophets and apostles.

•   The Importance of Understanding  
	  The author believes that understanding is essential for spiritual growth and progress.
	  The author notes that the Bible and other records show that there has been a progression in God's teaching, and that this understanding has been clarified over time.

	  The author believes that their critique of Luther's teachings is necessary and justified.
