Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Plato and the Ammonians  
	  Were not inventors, but rather retailers of mutilated revelations
	  Passed on ancient doctrines from Plato to Parmenides, and from there to the Pythagorean sect
	  The Pythagoreans to Orpheus, the earliest Greek mystagogue
	  The Orphic theology was influenced by the secret lore of Egyptian priests

•   The Trinity in ancient theology  
	  A triple principle was present in Persian and Chaldean theology
	  Vestiges of a Trinity worship were found in Roman superstition in a late age
	  The Romans received this worship from their Trojan ancestors, who brought it from Phrygia

•   The worship of the Three Mighty Ones  
	  Traced to the worship of Jupiter, Juno, and Minerva in Samothrace
	  This worship was established in Samothrace before the days of Abraham
	  The joint worship of the Three Mighty Ones in Samothrace was the origin of the Roman capital's triad

•   Erasmus' Diatribe  
	  A disquisition or disputation on the question of free will
	  Erasmus borrowed the term from ancient philosophical debates
	  Luther often personified free will in his writings

•   Greek hymns and praise  
	  Io Pan was a hymn of praise, especially one sung to invoke or thank a deity
	  The term was used to describe a hymn or song of praise

•   The schoolmen and Aristotle  
	  The schoolmen, led by Peter Lombard, idolized Aristotle
	  Their theology was full of metaphysical subtleties and resembled the debates of Greek sophists

•   Luther's views on predestination  
	  Luther believed that divine predestination took away liberty from man
	  All things happen according to divine predestination, including external actions and internal thoughts
	  The judgment of the flesh hates this sentiment, but the judgment of the spirit embraces it

•   Melancthon's Common Places  
	  The earlier editions of Melancthon's work contained passages not found in later editions
	  Luther referred to these passages in his testimony to Melancthon's work
	  Melancthon may have changed his sentiments on the subject in later life

•   Changes in opinion  
	  Too much has been made of supposed changes in the opinions of learned and pious divines
	  We must draw our light from the clear spring of knowledge, not from the polluted and uncertain stream

•   Greek mythology  
	  Scylla was a sea nymph transformed into a sea monster in Greek mythology
	  The term "lubricus et flexiloquus" was used to describe someone who slips out of your hands or has words of doubtful or pliable meaning

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Proteus in Greek mythology  
	  A demigod with the power to change his shape
	  A prophet who knew all things past, present, and future
	  Disliked divulging what he knew, and had to be surprised and bound to consult him

•   Ministering of Christ  
	  The business spoken of in the text
	  Corresponds to "res bellica", "res navalis", and "res judiciaria"
	  The trade, occupation, and sole concern of Christ's ministers

•   Officium  
	  A Latin word meaning "office" or "function"
	  Implies a relation or duty, such as "munus officium oculorum" (the office of the eye)

•   Gravas and ornas  
	  "Gravas" means to weigh down or clog
	  "Ornas" means to beautify with apparel
	  The figure is mixed, combining two opposing ideas

•   Pedibus discessurus  
	  A Roman phrase meaning "to walk over to the opposite side"
	  Used in the Roman method of voting in the senate

•   Never bis ludamur  
	  A Latin phrase meaning "that we may not be mocked by words"
	  Used to describe the sport of words or oratory

•   Vel util lead Rhombum  
	  A phrase from Juvenal's fourth Satire
	  Describes a blind informer who praises a fish (the Rhombus) without seeing it

•   Erasmus's comparison  
	  Erasmus compares the orator and the writer
	  Suggests that the orator is a venal and frivolous tribe, while the writer is frenzied and blasphemous

•   Praesumere  
	  A Latin word meaning "to presume" or "to anticipate"
	  Used in a rather peculiar sense, corresponding to our English word "presume"

•   The force of the comparison  
	  The comparison lies in Erasmus discussing the phantom of his own imagination, rather than the real Rhombus
	  If Erasmus calls the real Rhombus (the Church's confession of Freewill) dubious or useless, he writes sacrilegiously

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Catullus' speeches  
	  Catullus did not make two speeches
	  No authority supports the use of "praesumere" in this context
	  Catullus' rhetoric has a specific sense of preoccupying the adversary's ground

•   Luther's interpretation of Plerophory  
	  Luther's interpretation is not supported by authority
	  Plerophory means full evidence or assurance of a fact or truth
	  Luther is correct in substance, but his interpretation is not accurate

•   Anticyra and Hellebore  
	  Anticyra is a famous island known for its cure for madness
	  The phrase "Naviget Anticyram" means "Sail to Anticyra"
	  The reference is to a previous quote

•   Apprehending God's existence  
	  We must apprehend God's existence before attributing creation to Him
	  This is based on Romans 1:20 and Hebrews 11:6
	  Luther does not discuss Erasmus' skepticism and infidelity

•   Erasmus' skepticism and infidelity  
	  Erasmus' object was to rise upon the ruins of Luther
	  However, the Pope and Princes could not prefer an infidel
	  This is a galling part of Luther's attack on Erasmus

•   Confidence in the Holy Ghost's teachings  
	  We are more sure of the truth of the Holy Ghost's assertions than we live
	  We hold them more firmly than other results of experience
	  This is a beautiful testimony to the confidence inspired by the Holy Ghost's teachings

•   Abstrusa and Exposita  
	  Abstrusa means thrust from us or hidden from view
	  Exposita means set out in broad day or goods displayed for sale
	  Luther understands this text as referring to eternal election

•   Luther's affirmation and argument  
	  Luther affirms that all truth of God is explicitly and intelligibly declared in Scripture
	  Some passages are more obscurely taught due to our ignorance of words
	  Other passages are more manifestly and unequivocally taught

•   Resectum and the close cutting of nails  
	  Resectum means the close cutting of nails or hair or beard
	  It implies that all ambiguity is not yet withdrawn, though some of it may be
	  Luther refers back to this passage in the progress of his work

•   Scripture and its instrumentality  
	  Scripture reveals truth to the ear and to the heart
	  The word which falls upon the ear is a plain and clear word
	  The truth contained in Scripture is understood by the heart

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Curiosa   (99)
	  Refers to curiosity or inquisitiveness, whether true or false
	  Can be applied to things we have no business with

•   Vanos   (100)
	  Corresponds to supervacaneos, meaning unprofitable or idle
	  Used to describe idle speculators

•   Greek Mythology   (101)
	  Refers to the myth of Scylla, a sea nymph transformed into a sea monster
	  Lived on one side of a narrow strait and drowned sailors who tried to escape Charybdis

•   Status   (102)
	  Refers to a rhetorical question that expresses the conflict of causes
	  The question is: "What is the status of this matter?"

•   Omni   (103)
	  Means "all things in all men" and "all things in all things"
	  Every jot and tittle in every single thing that is done

•   Partem alteram   (104)
	  Refers to considering the sum of Christ's doctrine as divisible into two integral parts
	  Opposed to "alteram partem" in the next section

•   Curious   (105)
	  Means fussy or picky, overly concerned
	  Can be used to describe someone who is overly curious or inquisitive

•   An praesciat   (106)
	  Refers to the question of whether something is foreknown or not
	  If it is not inevitable or necessary, what is the point of foreknowledge?

•   Definis   (107)
	  Refers to defining or laying out the subject matter of debate in propositions
	  Supporting those propositions with argument, such as Luther's 95 Theses

•   Efficiunt quod tentant   (108)
	  Means they do not go through with the matter in hand, but leave it short
	  The "vires et opera" are still undefined, neither distinctly affirmed nor satisfactorily proved

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Erasmus' Character  
	  Erasmus is portrayed as a hireling who is only half-hearted in his cause.
	  He is accused of lacking due reverence for Scripture.

•   James' Words  
	  James' words about breaking one commandment being equivalent to breaking all are applied to Erasmus.
	  Erasmus' actions are seen as a breach of authority, leading to impiety.

•   Scripture and Authority  
	  Erasmus' actions imply a lack of reverence for Scripture.
	  This lack of reverence leads to all sorts of impiety.

•   Erasmus' Intentions  
	  Erasmus' intentions are seen as impious and lacking in reverence.
	  His actions are seen as a breach of authority.

•   The Human Body of Christ  
	  The description of the human body of Christ is seen as a connection to the damned substance of his people.
	  This connection is seen as entering into the mystery of godliness.

•   Luther's Critique  
	  Luther critiques Erasmus for being a friend of liberty, but also for being a hireling.
	  Erasmus is accused of satirizing the reigning abuses, but also of being galling in his exposure.

•   Free Preaching  
	  Erasmus is accused of preaching that men are free to observe or neglect the law according to their own conscience.
	  This is seen as a lack of reverence for Scripture.

•   Erasmus' Witnesses  
	  Erasmus' witnesses are seen as not proving anything, but rather as illustrating his cleverness.
	  Erasmus is accused of using similes to avoid answering solid arguments.

•   Marpesian Rocks  
	  The Marpesian rocks are mentioned as a reference to Greek mythology.
	  Alexander the Great built the Caspian Gates there to keep out barbarian hordes.

•   Erasmus' Defense  
	  Erasmus is accused of being guilty of all impieties, because one leads to all.
	  Erasmus is seen as making a distinction between what is good and what is evil, but Luther argues that this is not James' meaning.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Erasmus' Interposition  
	  Erasmus intervenes as an advisor or physician, criticizing others' methods and suggesting a better approach.
	  He modifies the truth to suit different times, places, and people.

•   Preaching Christ  
	  Despite pretense or sincerity, Christ is preached in every way.
	  Erasmus rejoices in this, but Luther's response is not explicitly stated.

•   Seeking Personal Gain  
	  People seek their own interests, not those of Jesus Christ.
	  This is a reference to Philippians 2:21.

•   Obliquanda  
	  The term "obliquanda" means bending or making crooked what is straight.
	  Luther opposes this approach, advocating for truth to be preached in its straightness.

•   The Rod of Truth  
	  Luther alludes to Psalm 14:6, where the rod or scepter represents the truth.
	  He understands the rod as a symbol of his own personal conduct as a prince.

•   Respect of Persons  
	  The word of God teaches that there is no respect of persons, and God does not regard human distinctions.
	  Luther argues that this is contrary to Erasmus' counsel to accommodate the ministry of the word to human tastes.

•   God's Distinctions  
	  God makes distinctions, but they are not based on human or earthly distinctions.
	  He is not bound by human categories, as seen in Colossians 3:11.

•   Myrmidon  
	  A myrmidon is a follower who carries out orders without question.
	  Luther uses this term to criticize Erasmus' approach to decision-making.

•   Effrontery  
	  Luther accuses Erasmus of having no face or shame, implying that he is impudent or audacious.
	  The term "effrontery" is used to describe Erasmus' behavior.

•   God's Sovereignty  
	  Luther argues that God works all things in us, rewarding good and punishing evil.
	  He implies that God's sovereignty is not limited by human understanding or darkness.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Infinity and Revelation  
	  God's infinity is beyond human understanding
	  Despite this, God has revealed himself to humanity through his counsel of manifestation
	  This revelation is recorded in Scripture

•   Luther's Understanding of God  
	  Luther, as a prodigy, did not have a clear understanding of God's manifestation
	  He counted much that is light as darkness, indicating a lack of understanding

•   Super-erogemus  
	  "To lay out and bestow over and above what is due"
	  This term is properly applied to public money, which is transferred to private expenditure

•   Quot and Emperveniat  
	  The contrast between the direct approach to God of the truly humbled sinner and the self-centered expectations of the unrepentant
	  The latter does not yet know the full extent of their lostness and impotency

•   Nihileligit  
	  The indirect contrast with the "sibi praesumit, sperat, optat" of the former sentence
	  The individual does not desire or expect a particular combination of time and place to perform a great work for themselves

•   Cognoscant  
	  "To know" or "to be aware of" something not known before
	  Opposed to "agnosco", which means "to not know"

•   Hackneyed  
	  Made too familiar by overuse and repetition
	  A term used to describe something that has lost its original meaning or impact

•   Necessariodamnabiles  
	  We were created to be objects of God's eternal damnation due to our sinful nature
	  This necessity arises from God's appointments, arrangements, and operations

•   Fides Vitae  
	  Faith of eternal life; the belief that one will possess eternal life
	  Exercised by the dying man in the moment when God is killing them

•   Suspicioneveritatis  
	  A sort of "surmise" or "conjecture" that may be true
	  A suspicion or doubt about the truth of something

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Nature of Free Will  
	  Luther argues that free will is a gift from God, but also a faculty that can be corrupted by temptation.
	  The faculty of free will is a rational will that distinguishes humans from animals.

•   Erasmus' Argument  
	  Erasmus' argument is characterized by dubitative remarks and questions, rather than bold statements.
	  Luther sees Erasmus' approach as a false candor and ruinous forbearance.

•   The Relationship between God's Grace and Human Exertion  
	  Luther argues that God's grace and human exertion are closely related, but not identical.
	  He believes that humans must be made to know themselves and God's proper praise can only be rendered through this separation.

•   The Power of Willing  
	  Luther asserts that the power of willing is free, but also acknowledges that it is subject to spiritual influences.
	  He distinguishes between the natural will and the rational will, which is capable of spiritual acts.

•   The Spirit in Man  
	  Luther believes that humans have a spirit that can be renewed and invigorated by the Holy Ghost.
	  This spirit enables humans to discern spiritual objects and perform spiritual acts.

•   The Reality of Natural Blindness and Impotency  
	  Luther argues that the natural will is blind and impotent, but this does not affect the reality of free will.
	  He believes that humans are free agents, but not free-willers in spiritual things.

•   The Name of God  
	  Luther argues that the name of God should be a property that separates individuals from others.
	  He does not mean that God should be called by this name, but rather that it is a characteristic of God.

•   Odibilis and Antiphrasis  
	  Luther uses the term "odibilis" to describe Erasmus' argument, which he sees as wicked and deceitful.
	  He also uses the term "antiphrasis" to describe Erasmus' use of words in a sense opposite to their normal sense.

•   Quadruplatorum  
	  Luther uses the term "quadruplatorum" to describe Erasmus' exaggerations and mercenary behavior.
	  He may be alluding to the Roman law, which used this term to describe public informers.

•   Propria, Pura, Sobria  
	  Luther argues that Erasmus' statements are plain, simple, and temperate, but also unprofitable, unjust, and unsafe.
	  He believes that God always interposes in human affairs and that humans are not free-willers in spiritual things.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Laurentius Valla  
	  A humanist theologian who lived from 1407-1457
	  Proved that the "Donation of Constantine" was a forgery
	  Advocated for a return to the Scriptures, using close textual study in the original languages

•   Erasmus' Ambiguity  
	  Erasmus stressed the pretended ambiguity of Scripture
	  Luther would later powerfully and satisfactorily repel this argument

•   Luther's Respect for Erasmus  
	  Luther claims respect for Erasmus' conscientiousness, scrupulous investigation of truth, and full consciousness of the evil he was encountering
	  Luther's light was poured in gradually and cautiously, and he would have been glad to hold his tongue when speaking or writing

•   Luther's Defense  
	  Luther declines to make a defense of his character and conduct, and instead acknowledges his inferiority to the Fathers
	  He concedes that Erasmus' three points of competition (Spirit, miracles, sanctification) are not to be contested

•   Luther's Concessions  
	  Luther concedes that Erasmus' cause should be tried even at his judgment-seat
	  He does not reserve a single point of superiority for himself

•   Luther's Critique of Erasmus  
	  Luther criticizes Erasmus for not being able to define the three points of competition
	  He argues that Erasmus could not prove that he possessed these properties concerning any individual of his vaunted host

•   Luther's Order of Critique  
	  Luther proceeds to critique Erasmus' Proem in the following order:
		1. You cannot prove that they possessed these properties
		2. If they had them, they did not come at them by free will
		3. Show you the same
		4. At least define the power
		5. How absurd your conduct is with respect to the Fathers
		6. Some desultory objections

•   Luther's Definition of Sanctification  
	  Luther defines sanctification as the state of being a saint, or one effectually called by the Holy Ghost
	  This effectual calling, or separation of the Spirit, is the act by which the eternally separated of the Father are drawn into a realized and recognized union with the separated one, even the Lord Jesus Christ

•   Luther's Critique of Human Authors  
	  Luther critiques human authors who teach that sanctification is a gradual work, the result of repeated actions of the Spirit on the substance of the natural soul
	  He argues that sanctification is one complete and final operation, not a gradual process

•   Luther's Conclusion  
	  Luther concludes that Erasmus is reduced to a dilemma by his own arguments
	  He argues that Erasmus' conduct is absurd with respect to the Fathers, and that he has not been able to prove his points.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's distinction between sanctimonia and habere spiritum  
	  Luther distinguishes between the concept of sanctimonia (being holy) and habere spiritum (having the Holy Spirit)
	  He argues that sanctimonia is a spiritual state that is not the same as the presence of the Holy Spirit

•   Horace's quote on futile labor  
	  Luther quotes Horace's phrase "sudet multum frustra quaeliboret" (he labors much in vain)
	  He uses this quote to illustrate the futility of human efforts without divine assistance

•   Public sermon vs. private meditation  
	  Luther contrasts public sermons with private meditation and the secret testimony of one's own heart
	  He argues that public sermons can be superficial and lacking in true spiritual depth

•   Jesus Christ's doctrine  
	  Luther emphasizes that Jesus Christ's doctrine is not just a set of teachings, but a living reality that is directly opposed to the concept of free will

•   Luther's challenge to Erasmus  
	  Luther challenges Erasmus to show the effects of free will in three areas: the miraculous, the moral, and the intellectual
	  He argues that Erasmus' concept of free will is empty and lacks any real substance

•   God's design in miracles  
	  Luther argues that God's design in miracles is to convict and render inexcusable those who are reprobate and ungodly
	  He questions whether God's design is limited to the gracious object of the miracle

•   Luther's contempt for Erasmus' views  
	  Luther expresses contempt for Erasmus' views on free will, using words such as "fleer" (mockery) and "supercilious" (arrogance)

•   Luther's willingness to come under the hammer  
	  Luther uses the phrase "sub hasta libenter ibimus" (we will freely come under the spear) to indicate his willingness to be sold as part of Erasmus' spoil

•   The puffed cheek  
	  Luther uses the phrase "buceaverborum" (the puffed or distended cheek) to express anger, pride, or boastfulness
	  He argues that Erasmus' views on free will are a prime example of this kind of behavior

•   The silent man  
	  Luther uses the phrase "batrachovek Serife" (a silent man, who cannot speak or sing) to describe someone who is unable to express themselves
	  He argues that Erasmus' concept of free will is similarly silent and lacking in real substance

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Publicans  
	  Were government officers responsible for collecting public revenues
	  Lived on the produce of their work and were of the equestrian order
	  Held in honor, but were also known for their corrupt practices

•   Deucalion  
	  A figure from Greek mythology, son of Prometheus and survivor of the flood
	  Mentioned in Ovid's Metamorphoses

•   Socordes  
	  A phrase meaning "without heart" or "carnal"
	  Used to describe those who are sinful and lacking in spiritual intellect

•   Qua forma  
	  A dialectical term meaning "in a certain form"
	  Used to describe the process of defining and specifying a concept

•   Quinedicitisquidem  
	  A phrase meaning "you are not even a nightingale"
	  Used to criticize those who speak but cannot answer questions or demands

•   Nequespeciemnequenomen  
	  A phrase meaning "they cannot define it or find a suitable name for it"
	  Used to describe those who are unable to articulate their thoughts or ideas

•   Mendacivocabulo  
	  A phrase meaning "with a false or deceitful word"
	  Used to describe those who use words that are not genuine or sincere

•   Appellamus  
	  A forensic expression meaning "we address" or "we call"
	  Used to describe the process of addressing a person by name

•   Inanibusbullisverborum  
	  A phrase meaning "prettinesses of style"
	  Used to describe the ornate and flowery language of some writers

•   Vertumnus and Proteus  
	  Figures from Roman and Greek mythology known for their ability to change shape
	  Used as examples of those who are adaptable and able to assume different forms

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Hercules' 5th Labor  
	  Eurystheus orders Hercules to clean King Augeas's tables
	  This is a reference to Hercules' famous labor of removing the accumulated filth of 3000 oxen

•   Scripture Reference  
	  Luther connects the words "pillar and ground of truth" to the ancient tabernacle
	  The tabernacle had boards and sockets (pillars and silver foundations) that held it together

•   Church and Truth  
	  Luther infers the immaculateness of the militant and visible Church from Scripture
	  The Church is a congregation of faithful men where the pure word of God is preached and sacraments are administered

•   Error in the Church  
	  The Church of Rome has erred in matters of faith and living
	  The same remark applies to each individual of the faithful
	  No one has lived without error in their lifetime

•   Public and Private Men  
	  Luther notes that men of public station (publicus) are different from private men (privatus)
	  Erasmus uses the terms publicus and privatus

•   Remnant of Israel  
	  Frequent promises are made in the Prophet that a remnant shall be left
	  The remnant of Israel and those who have escaped from the house of Jacob will return to the mighty God

•   Arianism  
	  Arianism arose in the 4th century, about 300 years before the rise of the Papacy
	  It was condemned by Councils but adopted by several of Constantine's successors

•   Arianism's Nature  
	  Arianism denied the co-eternity, co-equality, and co-essentiality of the Lord Jesus Christ with the Father
	  Arius said the Son proceeded out of a state of non-existence and was not before he was made

•   Answering Arianism  
	  The only solid answer to Arianism is to show the whole person of Christ in its complexity
	  Christ is a divine person and a human person held together by an indissoluble union

•   The Trinity  
	  The Trinity is referred to the Father personally
	  The complex person of Christ manifests the preeminent object of display in the Trinity

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Nature of Personhood  
	  According to William Paley, a person is a being that can contrive, design, and have consciousness and thought.
	  This implies a centre of perceptions and volitions, which is the mind.
	  The acts of a mind prove the existence of a mind, and whatever a mind resides in is a person.

•   The Human Mind of Jesus Christ  
	  The Lord Jesus Christ had a human mind distinct from his divine person, according to Scripture and Christian admission.
	  This human mind is a person, separate from his divine person.

•   The Union of Divine and Human Persons  
	  The divine person can take up a human person in union with himself and act in that person.
	  This union is real and subsists between the divine person and the human person.
	  The union is constant and unbroken, even when the divine person acts in and by the human person.

•   The Acts and Sufferings of Jesus Christ  
	  The acts and sufferings of Jesus Christ are the acts and sufferings of the co-equal of the Father and the Holy Ghost.
	  There is no diminution of his essential godhead in his voluntarily submitting to act by and in the human person.

•   The Complexity of Person in God  
	  The person in God can transact as though he actually were the complex person of both divine and human natures.
	  This is possible because Jehovah's will is both immutable and irresistible.

•   The Propriety of Calling Things as Though They Were  
	  It is proper to call things which are not as though they were, and to give realized being to substances which exist in predestination.
	  This is the case when God chooses a people of mankind to be in the complex person as head and gives grace to that people before the world began.

•   Meeting Arius on His Own Ground  
	  The author can meet Arius on his own ground and confound him, even if Arius admits all that he says about the Lord Jesus Christ.

•   The Catholic Church  
	  The Catholic Church is opposed to heretical churches, which are partial and do not profess the whole faith.
	  The term "Catholic" comes from the Greek term "airesivairetikov", meaning "selection" or "partiality".

•   Public Recognition of the Church  
	  The Catholic Church was publicly called and recognized as Christ's Church and performed its public functions.
	  This was the case in the early Church, where the Church was a public institution.

•   The Inquisition and Papal Abuses  
	  The Inquisition and the system of espionage, confiscation, excommunication, and violence used by the Church to achieve its goals are condemned.
	  This system was used to suppress heresy and dissent, and was led by the Pope, who was seen as the great heresiarch.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Huss and Luther  
	  Huss is said to have sung of Luther's coming before his time
	  Huss is considered a precursor to Luther

•   Vulgaris  
	  "Vulgaris" means "common" or "ordinary"
	  The Church and Saints are in everyone's mouth, but the things they signify are select and few

•   Gloriam Dei  
	  The Church is an invisible community
	  The saints are hidden and cannot be discerned by the eye
	  This is in line with Scripture, which says the wicked shall not see God's glory

•   Dominion gloria ecucifixissent  
	  This phrase is a strained application of Scripture (1 Cor 2:8)
	  The sentiment is correct, but the application is not

•   Locus satisfidelem  
	  "Locus" means a fund of arguments or a place of reasoning
	  "Fid" means trustworthy or one who has faith

•   Quamvis baptisatum  
	  Luther states that the judgment of charity is moderate and indulgent
	  However, there are deflections in faith and practice that place many baptized unbelievers beyond the bounds of charity

•   Unde explorabimus Spiritum  
	  This phrase refers to 1 John 4:1
	  Erasmus talks about Paul's recommendation to test the spirits, but Luther's allusion is to John's words

•   Neque adeo Scriptura  
	  It is not the authority of Scripture that is in dispute, but its right interpretation
	  The lack of clearness is hinted at, rather than affirmed

•   Neque nihil, neque omniadicis  
	  Erasmus says that the spirits must be tried, but there is no test of them
	  The tests he proposes are bad

•   Judicium publicum in ministerii in verbo  
	  This phrase refers to the judgment of a synod or council
	  Luther refers to the judgment of a tribunal, to which he is willing to submit his own objections
	  However, he states that such a judgment cannot be infallible

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Private Judgment vs. Public Judgment  
	  Luther emphasizes the importance of private judgment guided by the Spirit
	  He is thankful for the judgment of pure synods, but stands by a higher Master

•   Scripture as the Standard  
	  All dogmas should be brought to the standard of Scripture
	  Public leaders should declare their decisions based on Scripture, stating the grounds of their decision

•   Gainsayer  
	  A gainsayer is someone who contradicts or denies the truth of God

•   Externe vs. Internus  
	  Externe refers to a light of the Spirit within the soul
	  Internus is the opposite, referring to a light that is external

•   Causarum Quaestiones  
	  The book of laws recognizes certain broad principles to which facts are applied
	  These principles must be determinately fixed, admitted, and perspicuously affirmed

•   Status Causae and Quaestio Causae  
	  Status causae is the question of fact at issue
	  Quaestio causae is the principle of law to which it is referable

•   The Word as Light  
	  If people do not speak according to the Word, it is because there is no light in them
	  The testimony of the Word is equally conclusive as the clearness of the Word

•   Deducet and Via et Semita  
	  Deducet means to escort or accompany someone to their home
	  Via et semita refers to the broad carriage-road and the narrow foot-path

•   Gloriosus Disputat  
	  The Apostle compares the glory of the Gospel ministry to that of Moses
	  The scope and effect of the comparison is to magnify the Apostle's own office

•   Declarant and Copiae  
	  Declarant means to make clear or cause to be seen
	  Copiae refers to the Scripture testimonies and reasonings of the declarant

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

  1. Definition of "Humour"  
	  "Humour" is derived from the Latin word "humor", meaning "frame of thought or feeling"
	  It refers to the common judgment or feeling of mankind, also known as "commonsense"

  2. Luther's Defence  
	  Luther's defence was presented in Acts 6:10
	  He was accused of blasphemy against the Temple and Moses

  3. The Charge of Blasphemy  
	  The charge was that Luther spoke blasphemous words against Moses and God
	  False witnesses were brought forward to testify against him

  4. Luther's Defence Strategy  
	  Luther's defence was not just a simple denial of the charge
	  He aimed to prove that the Jewish nation had always resisted the Holy Ghost

  5. The Jewish Nation's Resistance  
	  Luther argued that the Jewish nation had always resisted the Holy Ghost
	  He cited examples from the Patriarchs to the present day

  6. The Temple as a Proof of Resistance  
	  Luther argued that the Temple was a proof of the Jewish nation's resistance to the Holy Ghost
	  He claimed that the Temple was not God's ordinance, but rather a human device

  7. The Temple's Origins  
	  Luther argued that the Temple was not built according to God's plan
	  He claimed that David's suggestion to build the Temple was forbidden by God

  8. The Tabernacle as a Better Example  
	  Luther argued that the tabernacle was a better example of God's worship
	  He claimed that the tabernacle was built according to God's plan, unlike the Temple

  9. The Meaning of "Subsumit"  
	  The word "subsumit" is not found in any authority
	  It implies a secret or partial retraction of a concession

  10. The Context of Luther's Defence  
	  Luther's defence was presented in the context of a broader argument
	  He aimed to prove that the Jewish nation had always resisted the Holy Ghost, and that this resistance continued to the present day.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Council of Constance (1415)   
  • This event marked the dawn of the Reformation.
  • It was a significant moment in history, comparable to the time of Christ and his first martyr.

•   Erasmus' Hypocrisy   
  • Erasmus claimed to be humble, but rejected clear evidence of truth.
  • He even fought against what he knew to be true, which is a worse offense.

•   Miraculous Regeneration   
  • The Holy Ghost's regenerating energy is a miracle that produces a supernatural constitution and state.
  • Every act of regeneration is a miracle, an exercise of super-creation grace and supernatural power.

•   John 12:37-41   
  • This passage of Isaiah is quoted more often than any other in the New Testament.
  • It is found in each of the Evangelists, Acts 28, and Romans 11.

•   Apprehendunt   
  • This word means to comprehend or take in the whole of a substance.
  • Erasmus did not lay hold of the truth, but remained in darkness.

•   1 Corinthians 1:23   
  • The authorized version reads "Greeks," but the more authentic reading is "Gentiles."
  • This distinction is important for understanding the context of the passage.

•   Exordium   
  • An exordium is an introduction or preface to a topic.
  • It is a way of setting the stage for a discussion or argument.

•   Putet, sentiat   
  • These words refer to the matter of reasoning and argument, and the matter of sense.
  • Both are intermixed in the passage, but each has its distinct appropriation.

•   Luther's Error   
  • Luther does not distinguish between what Satan has made of us and what Satan personally does in us.
  • This distinction is important for understanding the nature of sin and redemption.

•   Forced Application   
  • Luther's application of the words is forced and confusing.
  • He seems to be confusing the Lord's words with his own thoughts, as expressed in Luke 19:22.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Cornutus' Syllogism  
	  A type of argumentation where the "horns" of the argument are so disposed that to escape one, you must run into the other.
	  The term "horns" is used to describe the disposition of forces, like naval or military forces.

•   Disputatiuncula  
	  A diminutive form of "disputatio", implying a discussion subordinate to the main point in debate.
	  Used to describe a minor or secondary argument.

•   Lucian and Erasmus  
	  Lucian, an Epicurean philosopher, ridiculed all religions, including Christianity.
	  Erasmus, like Lucian, depreciated fashionable and reigning idolatry, but without meaning to serve Christianity.

•   Bald and Bare  
	  A term used to describe a definition that is not commensurate with the thing defined.
	  An idea that is not fully developed or illustrated.

•   Vertible  
	  A term used to describe something that is able to turn or be turned.
	  Changeable or variable.

•   Andabatae  
	  A term used to describe a man who fights in the dark, with his eyes blinded.
	  A type of gladiator who fought on horseback with his eyes covered.

•   Duns Scotus  
	  A Franciscan friar and a great opponent of Thomas Aquinas.
	  Known as the "subtiled doctor" due to his complex and subtle arguments.

•   Moderni  
	  A term used to describe the Schoolmen, including Duns Scotus and his contemporaries.
	  Compared to the ancient logicians and the Fathers, they were considered "men of today".

•   Crasse  
	  A term used to describe something that is dull, heavy, and fat-headed.
	  Used to contrast with the refined and subtle arguments of the Schoolmen.

•   Babbler  
	  A term of contempt used to describe a person who speaks loosely and ignorantly.
	  One who retails common-place scraps and refuses without order or measure.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Peter Lombard  
	  A scholastic theologian and Bishop of Paris (c. 1096-1160)
	  Arrived in Paris around 1134 and taught at the cathedral school of Notre Dame
	  Met Peter Abelard and Hugh of St. Victor during his time in Paris

•   Works of Peter Lombard  
	  Authored "Four Books of Sentences", a standard textbook of theology
	  Martin Luther wrote glosses on the Sentences, and John Calvin quoted from it over 100 times in his Institutes
	  Lombard's most controversial doctrine was identifying charity with the Holy Spirit (Book I, dist. 17)

•   Philosophical Influences  
	  Adherents to the philosophies of Plato and Aristotle were excluded from Lombard's universal comprehension of God
	  Erasmus made free will greater than itself, and Luther made a pun on this, implying he had even out-heroded Herod

•   Peter Lombard's Legacy  
	  Considered the father of scholastic theology, which succeeded the theology of the Fathers
	  His work, "Four Books of Sentences", was considered the great source of that science in the Latin church
	  Died in 1164

•   Mysticism and Charity  
	  Lombard's doctrine of charity with the Holy Spirit was a form of mysticism widespread in the Middle Ages
	  He said that when a Christian loves God and their neighbor, this love literally is God, and the Christian becomes divine and partakes in the life of the Trinity

•   Free Will and Discernment  
	  Lombard and his followers ascribed the power of discerning to God, but the power of choosing good conditionally to humans

•   Catholicum and Adomnes pertinens  
	  A title with which Peter Lombard was dignified, from his work "The Sentences"
	  A phrase meaning "what all are bound to receive as true"

•   Scholastic Theology  
	  Succeeded the theology of the Fathers and was considered a great source of that science in the Latin church
	  Peter Lombard's work, "Four Books of Sentences", was a key text in this tradition

•   Figures and Proverbs  
	  "Velut externe affingunt" - the gift of the Spirit, though not inherent, was represented as separably attached to the free will
	  "Inflatura" - a figure taken from blowing a bladder or making a musical instrument sound loud
	  "Ecclesiasticus" - also known as Wisdom or Sirach, in the Apocrypha

•   Lost Texts and Proverbs  
	  The Greek text of Ecclesiasticus omitted certain words and phrases
	  The Syriac or vulgar Hebrew text of Ecclesiasticus was lost, although Jerome professed to have seen it
	  The phrase "Graecalendae" - a day that will never come - was a Latin proverb taken from the Greek having no calendars to their months

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Erasmus' Deception  
	  Erasmus is described as playing a trick on people with his words, giving the same word many different faces like Vertumnus.
	  He is said to be able to say and unsay everything with his copiousness, versatility, and ambiguity of words.

•   Luther's Description  
	  Luther describes Erasmus as harsh, implying that he is critical of Erasmus' opinions.
	  Erasmus is said to be unable to will good, leaving people in possession of desire and endeavor.

•   Definition and Approvable Opinions  
	  The definition of a word is given, stating that it can apply to certain things.
	  The approvable opinion is that the word cannot will good.

•   Pillion and Quod Dis Diapason Conveniat  
	  A pillion is described as a seat behind a horse rider.
	  Quod dis diapason conveniat is a Greek proverb meaning the greatest possible dissimilitude.

•   Free Will and Accountability  
	  Luther objects to the distinction between free will and accountability.
	  He argues that Adam's free will did not imply independence from God or a will inaccessible to divine control.

•   Grammar and Desequela  
	  Grammar is described as moods or verb tenses in Greek that affect the meaning of a word.
	  Desequela is defined as what follows or is supposed to follow from an assertion proved or admitted.

•   Luther's View on Reception  
	  Luther does not deal with the cause of the difference in reception of the word.
	  He only discusses the fact that the Lord tests and proves different characters of men by such calls to obedience.

•   The Law and Justification  
	  The law is described as the knowledge of sin.
	  The law was added because of transgressions, and it serves to bring us to Christ.

•   Pelagianism and Tutum Libero Arbitrio Tribuentibus  
	  The Pelagians are said to have spoken more wisely than many who oppose them.
	  They maintained the integrity of free will as an absolute power of willing good.

•   Luther's Reference to Ephesians 2:8  
	  Luther refers to Ephesians 2:8, stating that salvation is by grace through faith.
	  However, his interpretation is incorrect, and the text actually speaks of the whole salvation as a gift of God.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Argument  
	  Luther assumes Adam had the Spirit in his creation state
	  However, there is no proof, and the contrary seems evident
	  Adam was formed to glorify God, implying a substance distinct from and existing in a state of severance from his Creator

•   Adam's Trial  
	  Adam's trial and manifestation would furnish an inference about other creatures
	  This trial could not have been made if Adam had possessed the Spirit
	  The Spirit's withdrawal during temptation implies a union with God was dissolved

•   The Command to Adam  
	  The command was given to Adam having the Spirit
	  Adam was tested, not having the Spirit
	  This demonstrates that Adam did not have the Holy Ghost

•   Argument from Adam to Humans  
	  The argument from Adam's state to ours is strong enough without assuming Luther's unwarranted assumption
	  Humans, made in God's image, could not stand against a single temptation

•   Luther's Use of Apocryphal Testimony  
	  Luther quotes a passage from Ecclesiasticus, which contradicts his own argument
	  The author regrets that Luther gave space to this Apocryphal testimony

•   The Preacher's Statement  
	  The Preacher speaks of Adam, whose will is not the matter in debate
	  Adam's will was left free to choose, and if it chose good, good would result

•   Consequences of Sin  
	  Sin leads to a loss of dominion over oneself
	  The sound of sin, as opposed to its sense or real import, is emphasized

•   Biblical Hebrew  
	  Biblical Hebrew has no future or past tenses, but has the indicative perfect or imperfect
	  This affects the interpretation of certain passages

•   Luther's Principle  
	  The author admits Luther's principle, but demurs to its application
	  The original passage is one of great difficulty, and the interpretation is disputed

•   Cain's Offering  
	  Cain's offering is seen as a "stale and rejected" thing
	  God's words to Cain are adapted to quiet and instruct him, rather than to condemn him

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Nature of Access to God  
	  The text discusses the idea that a person can only come to God through a specific way, which is not explicitly stated in the text.
	  This idea is contrasted with the concept of free will, which is debated by various scholars, including Luther and Erasmus.

•   The Role of Free Will  
	  The text questions whether free will is under the influence of a wrong bias or not.
	  This is a central theme in the discussion, with some scholars arguing that free will is not entirely under our control.

•   God's Sovereignty  
	  The text references God's doing everything, and our actions being done by necessity.
	  This idea is used to argue that even the way of evil is only broad and easy if God permits it.

•   The Law and Its Purpose  
	  The text defines the law as a principle that is both framed for a purpose and effects that purpose.
	  This idea is used to argue that the law is not just a set of rules, but a guiding principle for human behavior.

•   Knowledge and Understanding  
	  The text discusses the idea that knowledge is not just a general concept, but a specific understanding of a particular thing.
	  This idea is used to argue that the law provides knowledge of sin, and that imperative verbs are not indicative.

•   Luther's Response  
	  Luther is quoted as responding to the passage from Deuteronomy, arguing that it is too much to expect people to follow the law perfectly.
	  Luther's response is seen as unanswerable by some scholars.

•   Erasmus' Argument  
	  Erasmus is accused of using long, desultory arguments to prove his point.
	  Erasmus is also accused of supporting a small remaining particle of free will, which he claims to prove through his text.

•   The Sciolist and Pelagian Accusations  
	  Erasmus is accused of being a sciolist, meaning he knows superficially or imperfectly.
	  Erasmus is also accused of being a Pelagian, which is a heresy that denies the doctrine of original sin.

•   The Role of Endeavour  
	  The text discusses the idea that endeavour is not left to be ascribed to free will's own power.
	  This idea is used to argue that even our efforts are not entirely under our control.

•   The Argumentation  
	  The text uses the term "cont" to express contrast, comparison, or antithesis.
	  This idea is used to argue that Moses' folly is set in array against man's power.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Understanding of Grace  
	  Luther defines "grace" as the "free favor of God", not just the gift of the Spirit
	  He emphasizes the essence of grace as God's free favor, not just a gift

•   The Law and Ridicule  
	  The law does not mock or make a fool of people, even if its ordinances are impossible to follow
	  The law does not call people to expose themselves to ridicule or subject them to derision

•   Allusions and References  
	  Luther makes allusions to Cerberus, the three-headed dog from Greek mythology
	  He references Virgil's Aeneid and the myth of Cerberus guarding the entrance to Hades

•   Percurrere and Animadversion  
	  Luther applies the term "percurrere" to his review of Erasmus' preface, implying a short and lively criticism
	  He implies that Erasmus' preface is more of a recital or enumeration rather than a grave and elaborate research

•   Obruatur Coid and the Dragon  
	  Luther makes an allusion to the dragon from Revelation 12:15
	  He references the verse where the dragon casts water out of its mouth to cause the woman to be carried away by the flood

•   References to Scripture  
	  Luther references Isaiah 45:20, 52:1-2, Jeremiah 15:19, and Malachi 3:7
	  He notes that the reference was originally to Zechariah, but seems to properly belong to Malachi

•   Verb Gratiae Oblate  
	  Luther criticizes the expression "offers of grace" as implying freedom of choice, which is contrary to his position
	  He prefers the term "promises of God" received in a way that is generally set forth in holy Scripture

•   Quaerat Unde Possit  
	  Luther connects the words to the preceding sentence, but the punctuation forbids this connection
	  He implies that the seeking of power is not just a matter of finding one's own impotency, but also a gift and a conversion itself

•   More Literal Translation  
	  Luther provides a more literal translation of the phrase "sincethemeaningofthe commander and the demander"
	  He emphasizes the importance of understanding the meaning of the commander and the demander

•   Luther's Paraphrase  
	  Luther's paraphrase of Jeremiah 15:19 and Malachi 3:7 is not objectionable
	  However, the quotation from Jeremiah seems out of place and is a personal matter between the Lord and his prophet

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Distinction between Law and Gospel  
	  Luther distinguishes between law words and gospel words, but his distinction is arbitrary, indefinite, and unavailing.
	  He fails to provide a fixed rule to determine what is a law word and what is a gospel word.

•   Law Words  
	  Law words are those that require action or obedience, such as "return" or "repent".
	  They are meant to show what one ought to do, but do not imply any power towards law or gospel.

•   Gospel Words  
	  Gospel words are those that promise and exhibit God's mercy and forgiveness.
	  They are meant to prepare the way for faith and reveal God's love.

•   The Nature of the Law  
	  The law is the law of the Ten Commandments, which is a transcript of the creation law of man.
	  It is a preparation for and a fore-preached gospel, and a law word can be a gospel word.

•   Luther's Confusion  
	  Luther speaks confusedly about the origin, design, and nature of the law.
	  He fails to discern the distinction between law and gospel.

•   Scotist and Modernist Views  
	  See above, Part iii, Sect. 2, notes.

•   Luther's Distinction between Law and Gospel Words  
	  Luther's distinction is arbitrary, indefinite, and unavailing.
	  He fails to provide a fixed rule to determine what is a law word and what is a gospel word.

•   The State of Sin  
	  The state of sin is a state of eternal death, the just punishment of sin.
	  When converted, one is delivered from this state of punishment.

•   Revocare and Erigere  
	  Revocare implies departure from God, while erigere implies fallen or thrown down.
	  These words are used to describe the state of sin and the need for mercy.

•   Mercy and the Elect  
	  Mercy is to be preached and offered to all men, but only the elect will receive it.
	  Luther's offer is an nugatory offer to all but the elect, who must receive it morally.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Erasmus' Argument  
	  Erasmus argues that the promise of God's word proves that man could only despair without it.
	  Luther responds that this argument proves nothing on either side.

•   Luther's Response  
	  Luther says that the gospel word is a consolation for those who are law-stricken.
	  He claims that we have no right to ask questions about why some people are law-stricken and others are not.

•   The Law and Man's Nature  
	  Luther assumes that the law (the law of the Ten Commandments) was given to man from the beginning.
	  However, the Bible (Romans 5) shows that the law was not given until Moses.

•   The Role of the Holy Spirit  
	  Luther says that it is the Holy Spirit, not the law, that does not need the law to convict people of sin.
	  The Holy Spirit has enough proof to show people their sinful nature without the law.

•   The Election of Grace  
	  Luther says that the question of why some people are elect and others are not is a mystery of God.
	  He claims that we have no right to ask this question, but the Bible does provide an answer: it is the manifestation of God.

•   The Secret of God  
	  Luther says that the secret of God is why some people are elect and others are not.
	  He claims that we have nothing to do with this secret.

•   The Manifestation of God  
	  Luther says that the manifestation of God is the end of all his counsels and operations.
	  This is why some people are elect and others are not.

•   Religious Considerations  
	  Luther says that we are prohibited from trying to penetrate the secret of God.
	  He claims that this secret begins with the fact that some people are touched by the law and others are not.

•   The Worshipped God  
	  Luther interprets the text "above all the proclaimed and worshipped God" as referring to a God who is above all human conceptions.
	  However, this interpretation is questioned by the author.

•   The Root of the Answer  
	  Luther's distinction between the worshipped God and the true God is the root of the answer to Erasmus' objection.
	  However, this distinction is not entirely sound, according to the author.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Antichrist's claims  
	  The Antichrist would claim to be above every object of worship, true and false
	  This would be allowed by his followers, who would distinguish him from other gods

•   The true God  
	  The true God is distinct from the Antichrist's claims
	  The true God is the legislator, governor, and judge of creation

•   God's will  
	  God's will is divided into two parts: legislative and personal
	  The legislative will is revealed to us, while the personal will remains hidden

•   God's nature  
	  God is a single being, subsisting in a trinity of co-equal persons
	  God is infinite and all-blessed, existing before creation

•   God's purpose  
	  God's end is seated in himself
	  He will show himself to finite creatures, making them capable of apprehending and enjoying him

•   Creation  
	  Creation is a result of God's will, including all that belongs to Creator and creature-ship
	  The true distinction between the hidden and revealed God is God the revealer and God the revealed

•   The hidden God  
	  The hidden God is the same as the revealed God, but not circumscribed by relations
	  He has revealed himself in his relative and circumscribed capacity for the purpose of making himself known

•   God's will  
	  God's personal will is distinct from his legislative will
	  The legislative will is subservient to the personal will, and is an instrument for accomplishing it

•   Luther's views  
	  Luther did not apprehend the union and concordance of the two distinct views of God and his will
	  His answer was evasive and sophistical, and unsatisfying and repulsive

•   The knowledge of God  
	  The knowledge of God is the highest gift of promise
	  The communication of this knowledge is the end of creation and revelation

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's interpretation of God's sovereignty  
	  Luther believes God's word is in direct opposition to God's self, implying God's word is not to be trusted.
	  This interpretation is seen as incorrect by the author.

•   God's power and will  
	  God is a potter with power over clay, using that power to shape and mold individuals.
	  This power is not limited to salvation, but also includes condemnation and destruction.

•   Luther's view of salvation  
	  Luther believes salvation is a separate work from God's revelation, implying it's not a direct result of God's self-revelation.
	  This view is seen as incorrect by the author.

•   God's inscrutable will  
	  Luther warns against trying to understand God's inscrutable will, implying it's not to be known or understood.
	  The author argues that God's will is not entirely inscrutable, but rather has aspects that are knowable.

•   Luther's response to criticism  
	  Luther responds to criticism by saying "No, but O man, who are you that replies against God?"
	  This response is seen as evasive and unhelpful by the author.

•   God's sovereignty and longsuffering  
	  God endures the vessels of wrath with much longsuffering, allowing them to fulfill their purpose.
	  This is seen as a demonstration of God's sovereignty and power.

•   God's glory and riches  
	  God's glory is revealed through the vessels of mercy, which he has prepared for glory.
	  This is seen as a demonstration of God's sovereignty and power.

•   Luther's view of God's word  
	  Luther believes God's word is in direct opposition to God's self, implying it's not to be trusted.
	  This view is seen as incorrect by the author.

•   God's sovereignty in all things  
	  God is sovereign in all things, including life and death.
	  This sovereignty is exercised through God's legislative will, which regulates human duty as a moral creature.

•   God's power to save and destroy  
	  God saves and destroys through his legislative enactments, not in contradiction to them.
	  This power is distinct from God's hidden or absolute power.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's relationship with humanity  
	  God's relationship with humanity is distinct from his legislative relation
	  God gives humanity law, but does not withhold power or temptations
	  Humanity is a free agent, responsible for its choices

•   Creation and the Fall  
	  In creation, God gave humanity law (Genesis 2:17)
	  Humanity was created upright, but fell due to its own choice
	  The whole human race shared in Adam's ruin

•   God's dealings with Israel  
	  God gave Israel law, which kept them for his own purposes
	  Israel broke the law, but this prepared the way for God's future dealings
	  God saved and destroyed individuals within Israel through his personal will

	  God gives the church law, which is the law of repentance and faith
	  The church is commanded to repent and believe the Gospel
	  This demand has been implicit, but not explicit, since the Fall

	  God saves and destroys individuals through his personal will, not through the law itself
	  The law is not a means of salvation, but rather a means of judgment

•   God's sovereignty and human responsibility  
	  God is sovereign, and his will is not bound by the law
	  Humanity is responsible for its choices, and must repent and believe
	  God's demand for repentance and faith is the only demand made on humanity since the Fall

•   The Gospel and the law  
	  The Gospel is the means by which God saves individuals
	  The law is not a means of salvation, but rather a means of judgment
	  The Gospel is the only means by which humanity can be restored to God

•   God's relationship with humanity as king  
	  God's relationship with humanity as king is distinct from his legislative relation
	  God gives humanity law, but does not withhold power or temptations
	  Humanity is a free agent, responsible for its choices

•   The distinction between God's revealed and hidden will  
	  Luther distinguishes between God's revealed and hidden will

•   The purpose of the law  
	  The law is a means by which God fulfills his personal will
	  The law is not a means of salvation, but rather a means of judgment
	  The law serves to mark the difference between those who continue in apostasy and those who are restored to God.

	  The text considers the case of Israel and the visible church as a manifestation of God's sovereignty.

	  The text quotes Luther as saying that God's choice of some individuals is not a contradiction to human free will.
	  Luther's language may seem to imply otherwise, but his intention is to affirm God's sovereignty.

	  The text uses the metaphor of nipped fruit to describe the effect of God's sovereignty on human free will.
	  Without free will, human exhortations and efforts have no warmth, life, or power.

•   Horace's quote  
	  The text quotes Horace as saying that the best teacher is one who is always better than his students.
	  This is seen as a fitting expression of the first process in the art of teaching.

•   The allusion to the lobster or pelican  
	  The text alludes to the lobster or pelican as an example of an animal that feeds on its own stomach or intestines.
	  This is seen as a metaphor for the way God's sovereignty can lead to self-destruction.

•   The use of "bestia" and "bellua"  
	  The text discusses the use of the words "bestia" and "bellua" to describe wild and noxious animals.
	  These words are not confined to these animals, but can also express size or ferocity.

	  The text quotes Luther's commentary on Deuteronomy, where he discusses the unjustifiable use of the Sophists.
	  Luther argues that Moses' words can only be fulfilled under the Gospel.

	  The text discusses the application of the text to the Gospel, arguing that Moses' words can only be fulfilled under the Gospel.
	  This is seen as a natural understanding of the text, rather than a forced interpretation.

	  The author notes that the word "scrutandis" means to search for something.
