Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's dealings with Israel  
	  God had a special relationship with Israel
	  Israel had a history, institutions, and leaders that set them apart from other nations
	  They had a unique responsibility to obey God's commandments

•   Christ's relationship with Israel  
	  Christ came to Israel personally and visibly
	  He said that if He had not come, Israel would not have sinned
	  They had seen and hated both Christ and God the Father

•   God's legislative will  
	  God's will is legislative, meaning it is a declaration of what is to be done
	  Those connected with God by special relationships are expected to obey
	  Obedience is not the same as having the power to obey

•   Israel's guilt and punishment  
	  Israel's guilt was extreme and justly punished
	  The punishment was a consequence of their own actions and the actions of their ancestors

•   God's compassion and justice  
	  God is manifest in His dealings with man
	  He is moved with compassion at the grief and pain He inflicts in judgment
	  His will is not contrary to His justice

•   Christ's human soul  
	  Christ had a human soul as part of His complete human person
	  He was tempted as we are, yet without sin
	  He could feel compassion and weep at the woe He was about to inflict

•   God's pleasure in judgment  
	  God does not take pleasure in the death of the wicked
	  He does not afflict willingly or grievously
	  His judgment is just and righteous

•   Luther's understanding of Christ's will  
	  Luther believed that Christ's will was distinct from the Father's will
	  He thought that Christ's will was contrary to the Father's will
	  This understanding is not exactly as Luther intended

•   Christ's agency from the beginning  
	  Christ's will and agency were distinct from the Father's from the beginning
	  This is true, but not exactly as Luther understood it

•   God's complex nature  
	  God has a complex nature, with both legislative and personal aspects
	  His will is both just and compassionate
	  He is manifest in His dealings with man

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Criticism  
	  Luther criticized those who inquired about God's "secret will" or "sovereignty"
	  He believed such inquiries were unnecessary and could lead to a lack of joy and gratitude in worship

•   God's Address to the World  
	  God addresses the world through the Gospel, offering salvation to those who have a softened heart
	  This does not imply that God has reprobates, but rather that He is willing to save those who are willing to be saved

•   The Melting Heart  
	  A softened heart is not the heart of a reprobate, but rather a heart that is open to God's love
	  The fact that God has reprobates does not deter one from seeking God's will

•   The True God  
	  Without a testimony to God's sovereignty, the God one serves is not the true God
	  This testimony is necessary for a proper understanding of God's character

•   Epicycles  
	  Epicycles are small circles that account for anomalies in planetary motion
	  They are an example of how humans try to explain complex phenomena through unnecessary complexity

•   Scrutiny of God's Will  
	  Luther's criticism is directed at those who seek to scrutinize God's will, not at those who investigate it soberly
	  The will of God is distinct from His legislative will, and should not be impugned or denied

•   The Will of God  
	  The will of God is not just His legislative will, but also His secret will or sovereignty
	  This will is not expressed by Christ, but rather wills death and destruction

•   Rationem Scrutari  
	  Rationem Scrutari refers to the method of inquiring into God's will
	  This inquiry is not necessarily bad, but rather a questioning of God's right to have such a will

•   Investigating God's Will  
	  Investigating God's will is not the same as making war against Him
	  A proper investigation involves submitting to God's record and rejoicing in His sovereignty

•   Luther's Protest  
	  Luther's protest is against those who deny or impugn God's sovereignty, not against those who investigate it soberly
	  His answer to such cavils is that there is another will of God that is contrary to His legislative will.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's View on Free Will  
	  Luther believes that free will is not entirely absent, but rather it is limited.
	  He argues that humans can make choices, but they are not entirely free from God's influence.

•   The Nature of God's Will  
	  Luther believes that God's will is not a simple "yes" or "no", but rather a complex and multifaceted concept.
	  He argues that God's will is not always clear or consistent, and that it can be contradictory at times.

•   Erasmus' Inconsistency  
	  Erasmus is accused of being inconsistent in his views on free will and God's will.
	  Luther argues that Erasmus' views on free will are contradictory, and that he is unable to reconcile them with his views on God's will.

•   The Gospel and the Law  
	  Luther distinguishes between the Gospel and the Law, arguing that the Gospel is a message of grace and salvation, while the Law is a message of threat and condemnation.
	  He argues that the Gospel is offered to all people, but that it is only truly understood by those who are elect.

•   The Nature of Human Impotency  
	  Luther believes that humans are impotent and unable to save themselves.
	  He argues that humans are unable to understand God's will or to make choices that are pleasing to God without God's help.

•   The Role of Predestination  
	  Luther believes that God's predestination is a key aspect of his will.
	  He argues that God has chosen some people to be saved, while others are left to their own devices.

•   The Relationship between the Spirit and Grace  
	  Luther distinguishes between the Spirit and grace, arguing that the Spirit is given to the justified, while grace is a broader concept that includes the Spirit and other aspects of God's will.
	  He argues that the Spirit is a key aspect of God's will, and that it is necessary for salvation.

•   The Problem of Inconsistency  
	  Luther argues that Luther's views on the Gospel and the Law are inconsistent with his views on free will and God's will.
	  He argues that Luther's language is illusive and perplexing, and that it can be used to defame God rather than to glorify him.

•   The Nature of God's Design  
	  Luther believes that God's design is to manifest himself to humanity.
	  He argues that God's design is not always clear or consistent, and that it can be contradictory at times.

•   The Importance of Understanding God's Will  
	  Luther argues that understanding God's will is essential for salvation.
	  He believes that God's will is complex and multifaceted, and that it requires careful consideration and reflection to understand.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's argument  
	  The Old Testament is not contrary to the New Testament
	  Both testaments offer everlasting life through Christ
	  The Law is a Gospel in disguise

•   The role of the Apostles  
	  They were to be what Christ described, but they were not yet
	  They knew Christ "after the flesh" until the day of Pentecost
	  The Lord's precepts were for the regulation of their conduct and that of all his converted people

•   Luther's response to Erasmus  
	  Erasmus argued that the text in Matthew 5:12 does not apply
	  Luther countered that the text does apply, but to real members of Christ's kingdom

•   The distinction between sacrament and reality  
	  Baptism is a sacrament of union with Christ
	  The reality of this union is experienced when the Spirit is given to convert and dwell in a person

•   The comparison with the Ottoman Empire  
	  The Ottoman Empire's conquests were not won by wishing for the fruits of war
	  This is a logical fallacy, as Luther strongly emphasizes

•   The meaning of "detestentur" and "execrentur"  
	  "Detestentur" means to be opposed to or to curse
	  "Execrentur" means to be removed from sacred relations or to be subjected to a curse

•   The allusion to 2 Peter 2:10-15  
	  The text refers to people who speak evil of things they do not understand and will perish in their own corruption

•   The seeking of one's own glory  
	  People seek their own glory, even from God
	  This is a form of idolatry, as they seek good from God rather than from Christ

•   Merit and reward in Scripture  
	  Erasmus argued that the mention of good works and reward in Scripture is inconsistent with mere necessity
	  Luther responded that merit and reward are not inconsistent with necessity, but rather with compulsion

•   Luther's evasion and subtlety  
	  Luther's reasoning is evasive and subtle, but correct in his conclusion
	  He argues that merit and reward are not necessarily merit of worth, but rather a consequence of actions.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Answer  
	  Luther's fourth reply provides a solid answer to the objection of merit.
	  However, Luther does not fully understand the force and marrow of his own argument.

•   Merit and Reward  
	  If Christ's merit is the same for both the elect and the reprobate, then either:
		- There must be merit in the individuals of the elect.
		- There is respect of persons with God, who awards different rewards to some than others.

•   God's Justice  
	  God's justice demands differentiation, which cannot be in Christ alone.
	  Therefore, the difference must be in the individuals themselves.

•   Kingdom of Heaven  
	  The kingdom of heaven earns its children through distinct, super-creation relationships with God in Christ.
	  These relationships communicate the power necessary to inherit the kingdom.

•   Kingdom of Hell  
	  The kingdom of hell has relationships that have procured its inhabitants and inheritors.
	  The devil has power to draw people into his service and bring them to torment.

•   Free Grace  
	  Merit and reward are stumbling blocks to the maintainers of free grace.
	  However, these stumbling blocks can be overcome and removed.

•   Weak Defenses  
	  Sincere advocates of truth often defend their cause weakly and dangerously.
	  They may assume that there is no such thing as "recompense of reward" in the Bible.

•   Reward and Consequence  
	  The reward is not merely a matter of consequence, but rather a demonstration of God's justice.
	  God sees that which demands His justice and differentiates accordingly.

•   New Powers and Relations  
	  God can give new powers and relations to the natural man, under a new title.
	  This new creature can act in a manner worthy of its new relations and entitle itself to the results attached to those relations.

•   Equivalent Punishment  
	  God can accept an equivalent for the punishment that is the just reward of the natural man's sin.
	  This equivalent can be found in the new powers and relations given to the natural man.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Regeneration and Resurrection  
	  Regeneration is a partial fulfillment of the personal resurrection of the Lord's elect
	  It is the resurrection of the soul or spirit
	  The hour is coming when the dead will hear the voice of the Son of God and those who hear will live

•   Good Works  
	  Good works are not law works, but rather those that are consistent with the relations under which we stand
	  Good works are not a means of earning salvation, but rather a result of being saved
	  The law is for creation man, while the Gospel is for super-creation man

•   Obeying God  
	  The obedience of a redeemed sinner is a result of being called in Christ Jesus
	  This obedience is analogous to the full and more distinct manifestation of God in his new kingdom
	  Many have been created or built in Christ Jesus from the very first, such as Abel

•   Grace and Reward  
	  Grace reigns, but it is through righteousness
	  The connection of these words means that righteousness is not merely through Christ's personal righteousness, but through and by way of righteousness as it respects the persons of his people

•   The Nature of the Kingdom of God  
	  The members of the kingdom of God are persons already saved
	  They are not men striving for life to get life, but already-living men
	  They are joined to the Lord and are one spirit with him

•   Walking Worthy of the Vocation  
	  The members of the kingdom of God should walk worthy of the vocation with which they are called
	  They should walk worthy of God, who has called them to his kingdom and glory
	  They should manifest him to be the righteous God in recompensing rest to them

•   The Reward of Good Works  
	  The reward of good works is not of mere consequence, but of merit
	  The merit has worth and dignity in it, yet it is grace-free and sovereign

•   The Distinction between Law and Gospel  
	  The law is for creation man, while the Gospel is for super-creation man
	  The Gospel is the obedience of a redeemed sinner, which is a result of being called in Christ Jesus

•   The Nature of Super-Creation Grace  
	  Super-creation grace is true and sovereign
	  It is the grace that reigns, but it is through righteousness
	  It is the means by which the members of the kingdom of God are brought into a resurrection state

•   The Importance of Understanding the Kingdom of God  
	  Many people lack understanding of the true nature, origin, design, constituent subjects, and provisions of the kingdom of God
	  This lack of understanding leads to a failure to distinguish between things that essentially differ
	  It leads to a failure to understand that the members of the kingdom of God are persons already saved

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's View of Grace  
	  Luther argues that grace is not only favor, but also free favor that separates between those who have deserved a contrary sort of treatment.
	  He asks if the reader has received a new and super-creation title that differentiates between Adam's self-destroyed and wholly-destroyed sons.

•   The Devil's Power  
	  Luther asserts that the devil earns and earns his subjects through a power given to him by God.
	  He notes that the devil could not exist or continue to exist without God's will.

•   Merit and Worthiness  
	  Luther argues that merit and worthiness are not the same as merit that has worth in itself.
	  He claims that reward follows as a consequence, but there is nothing of meritorious worthiness in the subject.

•   Disinterestedness  
	  Luther overstates the matter of disinterestedness and later virtually contradicts himself.
	  He affirms that we are not called to be insensitive to the end, but to keep it in view as a source of encouragement.

•   The End of Salvation  
	  Luther argues that the end of salvation is not hidden or undesired.
	  He quotes phrases that show there is nothing mercenary in the Christian life, but the end is still the goal.

•   Excitement, Consolation, and Uplifting  
	  Luther describes the process of being excited, consoled, and uplifted as a beautiful process.
	  He notes that this process is described in Ezekiel 34.

•   Luther's Quotations  
	  Luther quotes words as if they were part of the same sentence, but they are actually from different passages (1 Corinthians 15:58 and 1 Corinthians 16:13).

•   God's Will  
	  Luther notes that it is not an arbitrary will, but the counselled will of God that accomplishes the best end by just and necessary means.
	  He argues that the declaration of his truth by the word is necessary for the manifestation of himself through his dealings with men.

•   God's Word  
	  Luther argues that it is God's word that imparts power to the natural bread to nourish.
	  He notes that God is pleased to use that bread, and similarly uses the spiritual bread of the word to nourish.

•   Good Works  
	  Erasmus argues that it is necessary for good works to be done by our own natural powers in order to be called our own.
	  Luther responds that if good works are done by our own natural powers, then there is no Spirit and no grace in them.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Scripture and Criminality  
	  The use of Scripture is considered criminal.
	  The term "Malleus" refers to a club or a tool used to punish.

•   Latin Terms  
	  "Viinsita" means "what is inserted as a graft" or "what is natural, innate, inherent".
	  "Assumsit" means "he has taken" or "he has assumed".

•   Christ's Nature  
	  Luther's view of Christ's nature is considered exceptional.
	  The distinction between the divine and human person of Christ is crucial.
	  Christ's actions and history are not a Babel of contradictions if we understand him as the co-equal of the Father and Holy Ghost.

•   Free Will  
	  The text does not prove the existence of free will.
	  The phrase "as many as received him" does not specify the power by which they received him.

•   Language and Absurdity  
	  "Ineptissim" means "extremely absurd" or "incongruous".
	  "Absurdus" means "repugnant" or "abhorrent".

•   Annoyance and Harm  
	  "Annoy" means to harm or molest someone.
	  The figure of a bailiff seizing a person's person and demanding payment is used to illustrate the concept.

•   Scripture and Human Nature  
	  Scripture can manifest what is in a person, revealing their true nature.
	  God can harden or convert people through Scripture, depending on his pleasure.

•   Gospel Precepts  
	  Gospel precepts are words to the Lord's called, showing how the saved should walk.
	  These precepts are not meant to be obeyed by the unsaved.

•   Consciousness and Self-Possession  
	  "Concipit" means to form an idea or to comprehend.
	  Luther's illustration of the carpenter's tools is not a happy one, as we are led freely and delightfully.

•   Confession and Testimony  
	  "Quaesaneagnosco" means to acknowledge or confess something perfectly.
	  "Fateorenim" implies a vow made under circumstances that might tempt suppression.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Wickliff's greatness  
	  Wickliff was a truly great man who saw and taught much of God's mystery
	  He was a champion of truth and a precursor to the Protestant Reformation
	  Many reformers, including Luther, acknowledged his worth and influence

•   The Dean's treatment of Wickliff  
	  The Dean did not do justice to Wickliff's legacy
	  He attached too much importance to Melancthon's judgment, which was influenced by the Sacramentarian Controversy
	  The Dean did not thoroughly understand Wickliff's doctrine of election of grace

•   Wickliff's doctrine of election of grace  
	  This doctrine was considered the center and heart of his creed
	  The Dean did not explain what Wickliff held about it, nor did he mention the paradox

•   Wickliff's legacy  
	  He was the first open champion and declarer against the abominations of Antichrist
	  He gave the Bible to their nation in English and vindicated the right of common people to read it

•   Reformers' recognition of Wickliff  
	  Many reformers of the 16th century owned Wickliff as their forerunner
	  His revilers cast him in their teeth, but he remained a champion of truth

•   The Council of Constance  
	  It was a numerous, turbulent, and long council that put down three Popes and erected one
	  It raved about reform but confirmed the outrages of the Teutonic Knights in Poland and Prussia

•   Luther's views on Wickliff  
	  Luther considered Wickliff as "the leader of an army of reserve"
	  He saw Wickliff as a champion of truth and a precursor to the Protestant Reformation

•   The concept of Fortune  
	  Luther rejected the idea of Fortune as the cause of success or failure
	  He believed that the God of battles was the one who brought about victory

•   The interpretation of Scripture  
	  Luther believed in a natural and legitimate interpretation of Scripture
	  He rejected the idea of using Scripture to pass off assertions as truths

•   The legacy of Wickliff and the reformers  
	  Wickliff and the reformers were champions of truth and precursors to the Protestant Reformation
	  They were willing to suffer for their convictions and to stand up against the abominations of Antichrist

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Adj   (450)
	  "Addere" and "adjungere" are Latin words related to adding or joining
	  The discussion is divided into two parts, one where Freewillism is maintained and the other where its opponents are repelled

•   Incomprehensibilis   (451)
	  This word means "uncatchable" or "unfathomable"
	  It is used to describe something that is beyond human understanding

•   Superciliousness   (452)
	  This trait involves displaying arrogance by patronizing those considered inferior
	  It is a characteristic that is often associated with pride and haughtiness

•   Ubiurgemur   (453)
	  This phrase means "where we are driven along"
	  It is used to describe a state of being driven by external forces, such as a goad or spear

•   Extendemanum   (453)
	  This phrase means "to stretch out the hand"
	  It is used to describe a gesture of offering or extending one's hand

•   Nondetextuipso   (454)
	  This phrase means "not in the texture of the text"
	  It is used to describe a meaning that is not explicitly stated in the text

•   Simplicem, purumque   (455)
	  This phrase means "simple and pure"
	  It is used to describe a meaning that is free from figurative language and human additions

•   Circumstantiaverborumevidens   (456)
	  This phrase means "evident from the surrounding words"
	  It is used to describe a meaning that is clear from the context of the surrounding words

•   Absurditas rei manifestae   (457)
	  This phrase means "the absurdity of the thing is manifest"
	  It is used to describe a situation that is clearly absurd or ridiculous

•   Quam grammaticae. habet   (458)
	  This phrase means "how much the use of speech is derived from man"
	  It is used to describe the origin of language and its relationship to human beings

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Introduction to Jerome  
	  Jerome was a renowned monk from Stridon in Pannonia
	  He was a contemporary of Augustine and an opponent of his
	  Jerome was known for his controversies and was considered peevish and vain

•   Jerome's Theology  
	  Jerome was allegorical in his interpretations, often without accuracy or precision
	  He lowered the doctrine of illumination in 1 Corinthians 2 to moral and practical things
	  He hinted at a first and second justification before God, but retracted it
	  He asserted predestination, but then retracted it as well

•   Jerome's Reputation  
	  His reputation for knowledge and abilities has been overrated
	  He was known for his profusion of learning, but it was often colored by a lively imagination
	  His ignorance of the truth was often used to give celebrity to superstition in the Christian world

•   Luther's Criticism of Jerome  
	  Luther criticized Jerome for his lack of discernment in the truth
	  Jerome was considered a man of prodigious learning, but his theology was flawed

•   Luther's Consubstantiation  
	  Luther's doctrine of consubstantiation was criticized for avoiding a trope, but still falling into it
	  The same portion of matter cannot be extended in two places at the same moment

•   The Sacramentists  
	  The Sacramentists were criticized for their views on the Lord's Supper
	  They were accused of being "new prophets" and were not considered to be in line with the traditional views of the church

•   Distenta  
	  Distenta refers to someone who is distracted or divided in their thoughts
	  It is often used to describe someone who is occupied with multiple things at once

•   Nulli Grammatico Ferendas  
	  This term refers to someone who is skilled in grammar, but especially in the classical sense
	  It was often used to describe teachers of grammar, but not necessarily those who taught the basics of language

•   Affectatas  
	  Affectatas refers to something that is factitious or artificial
	  It is often used to describe something that is the result of labor and search, rather than being natural or spontaneous

•   Animulae  
	  Animulae refers to the soul or spirit
	  It is often used to describe the inner self or the spiritual aspect of a person

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   472: Industry and Scholasticism  
	  Luther criticizes the scholastic art of his time, which was characterized by cloistered industry.
	  He argues that such industry must be accompanied by the Holy Ghost's testimony in the word or a palpable miracle.

•   473: Anaxagoras' Paradox  
	  Anaxagoras, a philosopher from Clazomenae, is said to have insisted that snow is black because it is made of water.
	  Luther uses this example to illustrate the absurdity of unsupported dogmatic assertions.

•   474: Whimsy and Scripture  
	  Luther argues that if a person's whimsies are taken as doctrines and interpretations of Scripture, then anyone can claim to be a divine.
	  He emphasizes the importance of searching and proof in understanding Scripture.

•   475: Diluere Crimen  
	  Luther explains the Latin phrase "diluere crimen" as "to purify" or "to wash away" a charge.
	  He notes that Cicero used this phrase in a forensic sense to mean the removal of filth from a substance.

•   476: Unsupported Dogmatic Assertion  
	  Luther criticizes the idea of making unsupported dogmatic assertions, which he sees as a form of intellectual laziness.

•   477: Isaiah 63:17  
	  Luther quotes Isaiah 63:17, which asks why God has made humanity err.
	  He notes that the authorized version renders this as a question, but Luther prefers to see it as a statement.

•   478: Perdidit  
	  Luther discusses the Latin word "perdidit," which can mean "to perish" or "to be destroyed."
	  He notes that Israel's case is unique in that it can perish and yet still be preserved.

•   479: Induration  
	  Luther explains the Latin word "induratio" as a pathological hardening or thickening of tissue.
	  He applies this concept to the hardening of the heart.

•   480: Benefacit and Tolerat  
	  Luther explains the Latin phrases "benefacit" and "tolerat," which mean "to heap benefits" and "to endure with much long-suffering."
	  He notes that God can harden people through benefits as well as judgments.

•   482-483: God's Dispensations  
	  Luther argues that God's dispensations of judgment and mercy can have different effects on different characters.
	  He notes that God can harden people through mercy as well as judgment, and that the result is a variety in the spirit that meets with these dispensations.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Aristotle's Theology  
	  Aristotle believed in a God who is the great mover of all things
	  He maintained the existence of a God and the eternity of matter
	  Aristotle's God is immutable, intelligent, indivisible, and without extension

•   Characteristics of Aristotle's God  
	  Keeps order in the heavens but interferes with earth in a limited degree
	  Appears to be the cause and principle of everything
	  Takes care of human affairs but is not concerned with individual crimes or disorders

•   Aristotle's God vs. Human Affairs  
	  His superintendence is like that of a master of a household
	  He establishes order and takes care that his end is accomplished, but shuts his eyes to human divisions and vices

•   Aristotle's View of the Universe  
	  The perpetuation of various species of beings is his grand object
	  He secured this by his one first impulse, which is sustained by general, not minute, superintendence

•   Luther's Criticism of Aristotle  
	  Luther described Aristotle as painting God, implying that his theology is ornate but lacking in substance
	  Luther's criticism is that Aristotle's God is not concerned with human affairs or individual crimes

•   Aristotle's Influence on the Church  
	  Despite Luther's criticism, Aristotle's philosophy was widely accepted and influential in the Christian church
	  His philosophy was used as a model for instruction in the church for many ages

•   The Corruption of Aristotle's Philosophy  
	  The predominance of Aristotle's philosophy led to corruption and the fruit of other corruptions
	  The Reformation of the 16th century exposed and suppressed these corruptions

•   The Word "Corruption"  
	  The word "corruption" has been used to describe the corruption of Aristotle's philosophy
	  It properly denotes a swift snatching up of a substance, and is sometimes applied to the seizure of the body by disease

•   The Manichees  
	  The Manichees were a group of heretics who arose in the reign of Emperor Probus (A.D. 277)
	  They believed in two independent principles, a good and an evil one, to solve the question of the origin of evil

•   Characteristics of the Manichees  
	  They supposed the Supreme Being to be material and to penetrate all nature
	  They made a great parade of seeking truth with liberal and impartiality, but were thus qualified to deceive unwary spirits

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Socinians' heresy  
	  They rejected the Old Testament as belonging to the malignant principle
	  They pretended the New Testament was adulterated
	  They claimed that genuine Christian principles would bear the test of scrutiny

•   Augustine's experience  
	  He was seduced by Socinian ideas for about 12 years
	  He was influenced by their subtle and captious questions about the origin of evil
	  He was also influenced by their blasphemies against the Old Testament saints

•   The Socinians' view of Christ  
	  They held that Jesus Christ had no proper humanity
	  They believed that Jesus was a mere phantasm of a man
	  They thought that Jesus' human body was a mere illusion

•   Augustine's confession  
	  He admitted that his ideas were materialistic and gross
	  He believed that God could not be flesh
	  He thought that Jesus' spiritual nature could not be born of the Virgin Mary without partaking of human flesh

•   Luther's criticism  
	  He criticized the Socinians for their views on God's creation of humans
	  He said that God does not make sinners, but rather humans are made in God's image
	  He believed that Adam was created with the Spirit, which he lost through sin

•   The nature of human creation  
	  Luther said that God created every human being in his own image
	  He believed that the substance of every individual human being exists from the beginning of time
	  He said that no new matter of human kind has been brought into existence since Adam

•   The creation of humans  
	  Luther believed that humans were created with distinctness and individuality
	  He said that humans were not created as a mass of substance to be molded into distinct vessels
	  He believed that God gave each human being a unique existence from the beginning

•   Scriptural evidence  
	  Luther cited Scripture to support his views on human creation
	  He referred to Genesis 1:26-28 and Matthew 19:4-5 to show that God created humans male and female

•   The Socinians' rejection of Scripture  
	  They rejected the authority of Scripture
	  They pretended that the New Testament was adulterated
	  They claimed that genuine Christian principles would bear the test of scrutiny

•   The Socinians' heresy and its consequences  
	  Their heresy was similar to that of the Gnostics and Docetae
	  They believed that Jesus was a mere phantasm of a man
	  Their views were destructive of all piety and led to fantastic ideas about Jesus.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's choice of people in Christ  
	  God chose his people to be in Christ before the foundation of the world
	  This implies that the whole human race was contemplated as personally and individually subsistent before the exercise of this choice

•   Adam's distinct personal subsistence  
	  When God added the procreative power to Adam, he essentially made every individual
	  The substance about to come forth in the Lord's time was already formed, with the power and authority for production superadded

•   God's role in the propagation of wickedness  
	  The only consideration that could involve God in the propagation of wickedness is that he did not immediately destroy the offender and those who had offended in him
	  However, the living substances were formed, and the power and authority for production had been given, with a curse upon them

•   The concept of original sin  
	  Original sin refers to the reality of being fallen from one's original uprightness in Adam
	  This constitutes every son and daughter of fallen Adam as a fallen creature, not merely a child of the fallen

•   The role of Adam as the sole personal agent  
	  Adam was the sole personal agent who committed an act of disobedience
	  Every individual of the race was enclosed in and part of his substance, so that they could not do anything without being with him in the offense

•   The concept of original guilt and depravation of nature  
	  Original guilt refers to the guilt of Adam's sin, which is imputed to every human being
	  Depravation of nature refers to the corruption of human nature as a result of Adam's sin

•   The church's recognition of original sin  
	  The Church of England recognizes the concept of original sin in her baptismal service
	  The service prays that the infant may receive remission of his sins by spiritual regeneration

•   The nature of human beings before the fall  
	  Human beings were created as a compound of soul and body, with the soul being an image of God
	  This continuity of soul and body was an essential part of human nature

•   The concept of death  
	  Death is the solution of the continuity of soul and body
	  This continuity was an essential part of human nature, and its solution constitutes death

•   The purpose of creation  
	  Creation was the stepping stone for further and more complete development of God
	  This development could not be made by simple creation, but required the assumption of new relations.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Adam's relationship with God  
	  Adam conversed with God face-to-face
	  He dwelt among the teaching creatures of God's hand
	  He was the most teaching of all creatures

•   The absence of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit was not present with Adam
	  This was not essential to Adam's condition as a moral creature of God
	  If the Holy Spirit was present, it would have been either conquered in the temptation or withdrawn prior to it

•   Luther's misapprehension  
	  Luther thought the Holy Spirit might be a sort of fast and loose playing of the Spirit
	  The Spirit, when given, acts earnestly and efficaciously
	  The cases are not parallel between Adam and the Lord's called people

•   The Spirit's action in Christ  
	  The Spirit is not our own, but in Christ
	  When we fall, it is not the Spirit that is conquered, but the Spirit not energizing

•   Accountability and trial  
	  Accountability implies account to be rendered, trial, and the presence of evil
	  Adam's state and constitution made him liable to fall by temptation

•   The nature of temptation  
	  Temptation implies the suggestion of evil
	  Adam's appetites and passions, which were pure in creation, were liable to be turned to evil

•   God's debt to Adam  
	  God was debt to Adam to withhold temptation from him
	  Or to minister super-creation aid to keep him from falling
	  Or to heal his wounds and restore soundness and peace to him

•   Dispensation and faith  
	  Dispensation refers to the act of disputing or debating
	  Faith receives implicitly what God explicitly declares

•   Self-idolatry  
	  Self is the idol to the dethronement of God
	  People seek their own interests and gratification, not God's

•   God's agency in the wicked  
	  Luther's explanation amounts to: 1. They are still real existences
		- 2. They are still God's creatures
		- 3. God works all things in them, even as he does in all his creatures
		- 4. He works in them according to their nature, hence he does all their evil in them, but does no evil himself

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Man's Constitution and State  
	  Man had a constitution imparted by God, with a state assigned to him
	  This state implied trial and the possibility of overcoming temptation
	  There was no withdrawal of God's engagement or possession, nor any gainsaying of previous announcements

•   God's Agency in the Wicked  
	  Luther compares God's agency in the wicked to a driver driving a lame horse
	  This excites the idea of physical rather than moral influence, but the truth is that God acts in the wicked by setting moral considerations before their will
	  This is moral necessity, and such a will cannot choose differently

•   God's Design to Make Himself Known  
	  God's design to make himself known to and enjoyed by certain creatures of his hands is the reason for the complex system of operation by which he has dealt with man from creation to the present
	  This design is manifest in the blessed human person of the Lord Jesus Christ and in every individual substance of the human race

•   The Nature of Evil  
	  Evil is not a thing to be created, but rather a privative concept that arises from the lack of agreement or order in some parts of creation
	  Evil is not a substance, but rather a perversion of the will that declines from God and casts away its internal excellencies

•   Augustine's View of Evil  
	  According to Augustine, evil is not a substance, but rather a privative concept that arises from the perversion of the will
	  Evil is a lack of agreement or order in some parts of creation, and it produces positive misery when good things forsake their just place, office, and order

•   The Origin of Evil  
	  The origin of evil is not a positive existence, but rather a lack of agreement or order in some parts of creation
	  The two independent principles that were previously thought to account for the origin of evil were without foundation

•   The Nature of God's Dealings with Man  
	  God's dealings with man are consistent with his own nature and character
	  God's design to make himself known to and enjoyed by man is the reason for the complex system of operation by which he has dealt with man from creation to the present

•   The Union and Concentration of All Moral Excellency  
	  The union and concentration of all moral excellency, truth, love, power, wisdom, and goodness is the truth, and it is the reason for the existence of evil in God's world
	  Evil is not destroyed, but rather continues to exist in God's world, and it will continue to exist until God's glory is fully manifest

•   The Manifestation of God's Glory  
	  The manifestation of God's glory is the reason for the existence of evil in God's world
	  Evil is not destroyed, but rather continues to exist in God's world, and it will continue to exist until God's glory is fully manifest

•   The Extension of These Considerations  
	  These considerations apply not only to God's dealings with the wicked, but also to his dealings with all creatures
	  Evil has been introduced into the creation of God, and it continues to exist in God's world, and it will continue to exist until God's glory is fully manifest

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Nature of the Human Will  
	  Is mutable and can be changed
	  Applies to all moral creatures, not just humans
	  Can be influenced by various factors, including good and evil

•   The Corruption of the Human Will  
	  Has been corrupted by sin and evil
	  Can be compared to a discordant note in a musical instrument
	  Can be influenced by Satan, but not independently of God's will

•   Satan's Role  
	  Is an agent and minister of God
	  Does not operate independently of God's will
	  Is used by God to test and harden the hearts of individuals

•   God's Omnipotent Providence  
	  Carries both good and evil in its workings
	  Is unto God's glory, but not necessarily unto salvation
	  Is a complex and multifaceted concept

•   Luther's View of Hardening  
	  God actsuate the wicked and others according to their nature
	  Satan is unresisted and undisturbed in the wicked
	  God thwarts the wicked by word, deed, or both
	  Is a partial view that does not consider the full scope of God's workings

•   The Natural Man  
	  Has been brought into a state of being that is earthly, sensual, and devilish
	  Is possessed by the devil and given up to him as a prey
	  Is characterized by a lack of humility, submission, and gratitude

•   God's Word and Work  
	  Is a fine image of God giving motion to all creatures
	  Is a way of describing God's moving and driving of all creatures
	  Is a partial view that does not consider the full scope of God's workings

•   Satan's Agency  
	  Is an agency with which God does not interfere
	  Is not an agent or minister of God's
	  Is used by God to test and harden the hearts of individuals

•   The Root of the Matter  
	  Satan is an agent and minister of God
	  Is used by God to carry out His will and purposes
	  Is not independent of God's will or workings

•   God's Sovereignty  
	  Is the ultimate authority and power in the universe
	  Is the one who sets and causes things to be set before the minds of individuals
	  Is the one who hardens the hearts of individuals, but also gives them the spirit of slumber.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    God's Hardening  
	  God's hardening is a special operation on the reprobate soul
	  It involves shutting and sealing the soul in its own native blindness, aversion, and enmity towards God
	  This operation is carried out through the agency of Satan, who is God's rider and lord
    Pharaoh as an Example  
	  Pharaoh is a notable example of God's hardening
	  The whole history of the Exodus is a display of God's design and accomplishment in dealing with creatures
    God's Will  
	  God's will is not caused by anything external
	  It is self-manifestation and has its own reason for all that it does
	  God's will is not changed by human actions, but rather, it is the human will that is thwarted
    Satan's Role  
	  Satan is the agent of God's hardening
	  He is used by God to hinder and blind the reprobate soul
    Luther's Theology  
	  Luther's theology is criticized for not providing a satisfactory answer to the question of God's hardening
	  He sees God's hardening as a negative act, except as it relates to God's general and particular operations in providence
    God's Glory  
	  God continues to move the wicked because it is for his glory that they should act as they are
	  This is the same reason why God ordained and brought about Adam's fall
    God's Generation  
	  God does not create wicked men
	  Instead, he generates them through his own internal principle
    God's Reason  
	  God has a reason for all that he does
	  This reason is resolvable into self-manifestation
    The Word of God  
	  The word of God furnishes an answer to questions about God's hardening and other mysteries
	  We are bound to entertain these questions and supply the true answer
    The Nature of God  
	  A known God is better than an unknown God
	  A God who is revered, admired, and delighted in is better than one who is only trembled at and shuddered before.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Circumscription of God's Design  
	  God's ultimate object is self-manifestation
	  This is achieved by confounding His enemies and emboldening His beloved ones
	  God shows what He is by His dealings with human nature, both in the elect and the reprobate

•   The Principle of God's Manifestation  
	  God's dealings with humanity are a manifestation of what He is
	  This principle is essential to understanding the Bible and its history
	  Without it, the Bible would be a mass of inconsistency

•   Key Terms  
	    Vertible  : able to turn or be turned; changeable
	    Omit  : to fail to do something; to leave out
	    Occursu objecto  : an act of God that comes into contact with the edge of human will

•   God's Actuation and Human Will  
	  God's actuation is the means by which He interacts with human will
	  Human will is often evil and resistant to God's actuation
	  God always acts and therefore always actuates the wicked

•   Ordinatam voluntatem signi  
	  The distinction between a regulated and absolute will
	  A regulated will is limited and restrained by ordinance or outward signs
	  An absolute will is pure and uncontrolled, and cannot be resisted

•   Luther's Argument  
	  Luther's argument is not dependent on the order of the verses
	  The more explicit testimony of verse 18 is implied in verse 15

•   Prosopopoeia  
	  A figure of rhetoric in which imaginary persons are introduced
	  Paul uses prosopopoeia to bring in a supposed objection to his argument

•   Errat.fallitur  
	  A mistake in one's own apprehensions
	  A mistake that arises from appearances that beguile one

•   Majestatem  
	  A form of expression common among men, used to refer to earthly potentates
	  Used to personify God's power and will

•   Fatumin-eluctabile  
	  A form of fate that is inexorable, infallible, and invincible
	  Even those who made the Fates superior to Jupiter still recognized their power

•   Praetexi  
	  A fine web of art spread before a substance to cover or disguise it
	  Used to describe the natural judgment that opposes the spiritual

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Abyss of Despair  
	  Luther felt the very depths of despair
	  He begged God not to command him to go out into the abyss
	  He felt a sense of hopelessness and desperation

•   Excuses and God's Will  
	  Luther distinguishes between God's will and human will
	  He argues that God's will is absolute and unchanging
	  He contrasts this with human will, which is subject to change and error

•   Opposition to Truth  
	  Luther defines opposition as setting out to canvass doctrines with a display of school-learning
	  He argues that this type of opposition is not the same as opposing the truth
	  He suggests that this type of opposition is actually a form of deception

•   The Law of Necessity  
	  Luther argues that the law of necessity lies at the bottom of our hearts
	  He suggests that this law is not outwardly taught or professed
	  He argues that this law can be made illegible and effaced by false teaching and prejudice

•   God's Purpose  
	  Luther quotes Romans 9:30, arguing that God's purpose is to call a body of Gentiles into his church
	  He suggests that this purpose is not limited to a remnant of Israel
	  He argues that God's purpose is to save all people, not just a select few

•   Cutting Out Words  
	  Luther argues that words can be "cut out" from the text, losing their original meaning
	  He suggests that this can lead to a distortion and pollution of the original meaning
	  He argues that this type of cutting out can be used to support false teachings

•   Concussing and Exploring  
	  Luther defines "concussing" as a violent separation of words from their original context
	  He argues that this can lead to a distortion of the original meaning
	  He suggests that this type of concussing can be used to support false teachings

•   Liberty of Judgment  
	  Luther argues that people should be free to judge for themselves
	  He suggests that this freedom is being denied by those who claim to have the truth
	  He argues that this denial of freedom is a form of deception

•   Superficial Arguments  
	  Luther argues that some people make superficial arguments, using empty words and phrases
	  He suggests that these arguments are not based on substance or truth
	  He argues that these types of arguments are a form of deception

•   Treachery and Necessity  
	  Luther argues that some people would say that Judas' treachery was necessary
	  He suggests that this is a form of deception, as Judas was not a necessary traitor
	  He argues that this type of thinking is a form of sophistry, using words to deceive rather than to reveal the truth.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Erasmus' Response to Luther  
	  Erasmus tries to evade Luther's argument using a trope
	  He argues that Luther's interpretation is absurd and doesn't remove the difficulty

•   Tropical Interpretations  
	  Erasmus claims that tropical interpretations are generally inadmissible
	  He objects to Luther's use of illustrations and examines the causes for introducing them

•   God's Sovereign Will  
	  Erasmus argues that God differentiates by his sovereign will
	  He uses the example of national and personal election to illustrate this point

•   The Doctrine of Freewill  
	  Erasmus maintains that the doctrine of freewill is not affected by Luther's argument
	  He notes that Jerome and others taught like him on this point

•   Distinction in Jerome's Writings  
	  Erasmus advises being judicious in discerning what may not be doctrinally correct in Jerome's writings
	  He notes that the principle is the same: God differentiates by his sovereign will

•   Original Sin  
	  Erasmus notes that the question of original sin is not affected by Luther's argument
	  He references a previous note on the subject (Sect. 10, note z)

•   The Difference between Jacob and Esau  
	  Erasmus argues that the question of the difference between Jacob and Esau is not about their manifest existence or personal agency
	  He notes that neither Jacob nor Esau had done good or evil when the words were spoken to Rebekah

•   Rebekah's Prophecy  
	  Erasmus notes that the prophecy was delivered to Rebekah, not Sarah
	  He references Genesis 25:22-23 to support this point

•   The Visible Church  
	  Erasmus notes that Isaac's descendants in the line of Jacob were not only the typical family of the community but also the visible church for a season
	  He notes that the spiritual blessings of God were comprehended in this superiority

•   Sacrilege and Jerome's Teaching  
	  Erasmus defines sacrilege as the crime of robbing heaven
	  He notes that Jerome and those who followed him were guilty of this crime

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Erasmus' Interpretation  
	  Erasmus interprets the text as implying that God's will is not love and hate, but rather the effect of these emotions.
	  Luther responds that if it's an effect, it's God's will that effects it, and the effect is what He approves.

•   Key Terms  
	    Excogitation  : the creation of something in the mind, a fancy or imagination.
	    Pertinacity  : persistent determination.

•   Malachi's Prophecy  
	  Judah reproaches God for being freely and distinguishingly loved, yet being treacherous.
	  The essence of the reproach is the freeness of the love.

•   Textual Analysis  
	  The text is clear and requires no interpretation to make its meaning clear (Textus ipsum Prophetae).
	  Erasmus inserts the word "afterwards" to give clarity, but Luther argues that this is not necessary.

•   Scriptural Interpretation  
	  Erasmus argues that the text refers to the eleventh chapter of Malachi, but Luther disputes this.
	  Luther argues that Erasmus is dragging in words that are not relevant to the text.

•   Paul's Distinction  
	  According to Paul's distinction of offices in Romans 12:6-8, the text refers to different types of gifts or offices.
	  Erasmus argues that the prophets speak only of temporal afflictions, but Luther disputes this.

•   Bond-Will  
	  Erasmus argues that the text does not prove the existence of bond-will, but Luther argues that it does.
	  Luther argues that the afflictions come against our will, but this does not show a lack of freedom.

•   Simile of the Potter  
	  Erasmus argues against the conclusion drawn from the simile of the potter, citing 2 Timothy 2:20-21.
	  Luther responds that the simile is not efficacious in this context, and that Erasmus must prove the similitude he assumes.

•   God's Will  
	  Luther argues that God's will is not contradicted by evil, but rather fulfilled.
	  The text refers to the eternal separation of God's people, which is manifested and realized through their voluntary separation.

•   The Elect Church  
	  The text refers to the whole elect church of God as a sort of huge foundation-stone with inscriptions (Qemeliov).
	  See Zechariah 3:9 for further reference.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's criticism of Erasmus' gloss  
	  Luther rejects Erasmus' interpretation of a passage
	  He argues that Erasmus' gloss is not a judgment of equity, but rather an interested judgment

•   The sovereignty of God  
	  Luther acknowledges the sovereignty of God as a key concept
	  He notes that this sovereignty is often misunderstood and can be bitter to the carnal mind

•   The mystery of creation and fall  
	  Luther did not understand the mystery of creation and fall in Adam
	  He did not grasp the idea that every individual became a hell-deserving sinner in Adam

•   Predestination  
	  Luther did not understand the mystery of predestinative counsel
	  He did not see that predestination is the fulfillment of a forearranged design

•   God's relations with humanity  
	  Luther did not understand that God assumes new relations with humanity in Christ
	  He did not grasp the idea that Christ renders humanity worthy to be taken up from ruin

•   The nature of salvation  
	  Luther did not see that salvation is not a matter of merit, but rather a matter of God's sovereignty
	  He did not understand that God's sovereignty is exercised for the end of manifesting His glory

•   The problem of universal redemption  
	  Luther's view of universal redemption leaves out the concept of merit and partiality in God
	  He does not see that a universal redemption must either leave partiality in God or merit in man

•   Luther's view of sovereignty  
	  Luther puts sovereignty in the wrong place and omits what should be added to it
	  He does not see that sovereignty is exercised for the end of manifesting God's glory

•   The need for an adequate end  
	  Luther did not see the end for which God's sovereignty is exercised
	  He did not understand that this end is the manifestation of God's glory

•   The nature of interpretation  
	  Luther accuses Erasmus of using a "qualified interpretation" that is not faithful to the text
	  He argues that Erasmus is giving a sense that is not present in the words themselves

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The figure of the drowned man  
	  The figure is used to describe the state of humanity after being under water for a long time
	  It is similar to Virgil's phrase "corruptam corruptam indis"

•   Land caprind  
	  See above, Part II, Sect. 3, note 1
	  The author is discussing the declaration of Luther

•   The declaration of Luther  
	  Luther wants to show that the words are of anger, not pity and palliation
	  The author suggests that the sentiment could be "My Spirit shall not always be reproving that man is flesh"

•   The reason for continuing humanity's existence  
	  The great reason is that humanity might show itself what it is, as it has made itself different from what God made it
	  The Lord says he will carry on this work of manifestation for no longer than 120 years

•   The manifestation of the divine Spirit  
	  The divine Spirit acts both medially and immediately upon the spirits of humanity
	  Luther confines it to the effect of their intercourse with others, such as Noah and the Lord's people

•   The Spirit's action on disobedient ones  
	  The Spirit also acts upon the disobedient ones without their intervention
	  The Spirit moves and drives all God's creatures

•   The meaning of "ruach"  
	  "Ruach" means "spirit" or "breath"
	  It can also mean "to strive" or "to judge"

•   The controversy or judgment  
	  The controversy or judgment is a trial or proof of humanity's nature
	  It is carried out by the divine Spirit

•   The hypocritical remnant  
	  The great offense and provocation seems to have been given by the hypocritical remnant
	  They are those who practiced and made profession of the worship of God, in opposition to those who had thrown it off

•   The importance of simplicity in scripture  
	  The author suggests that simplicity should be maintained in scripture where possible
	  This is in opposition to a figurative sense

•   The office of the word  
	  The word is not just mere preaching, but also life
	  The author has previously said that the word is administered by mouth and life

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Interpretation of "flesh"  
	  The author disagrees with Luther's interpretation of "flesh" as "evil affection"
	  The author argues that "flesh" refers to the whole human substance, including body and soul
	  The author cites various biblical passages to support this interpretation (Psalm 145:21, Luke 3:6, Isaiah 40:5-6, John 17:2, 1 Corinthians 1:29)

•   Christ's assumption of humanity  
	  The author argues that when Christ became flesh, he assumed the whole human person, not just the body
	  This assumption is a union with Christ, making believers one flesh with him
	  This union is not limited to the body, but includes the soul as well

•   The church as one flesh with Christ  
	  The author argues that the church is made one flesh with Christ, not just the body
	  This union is not limited to the physical body, but includes the spiritual body as well
	  This union is a result of the Spirit's agency, not just physical presence

•   Luther's interpretation and its limitations  
	  The author argues that Luther's interpretation of "flesh" is limited and does not account for the whole human substance
	  Luther's interpretation focuses on the body, but neglects the soul and the Spirit's agency
	  The author suggests that Luther's interpretation is not sufficient to understand the full meaning of the text

•   The preaching of Christ  
	  The author argues that the preaching of Christ has a twofold effect: comfort and condemnation
	  The people who are comforted are not objects of vengeance, but of favor
	  The author cites various biblical passages to support this interpretation (2 Corinthians 2:15-16, John 15:22)

•   The manifestation of Satan's enmity  
	  The author argues that the manifestation of Satan's enmity is peculiarly affected by the preaching of Christ
	  The preaching of Christ reveals the true nature of human hearts and their enmity against God
	  This revelation is not intended to condemn, but to reveal the true state of humanity

•   Grammatistis and the Jewish Rabbins  
	  The author uses the term "Grammatistis" to refer to the Jewish Rabbins who were self-proclaimed experts in literature
	  The author argues that these Rabbins took great liberties with the sacred text
	  The author cites various biblical passages to support this criticism (2 Timothy 2:3, 1 Timothy 6:12, 1 Corinthians 9:24-27, 2 Timothy 2:5, Ephesians 6, 1 Thessalonians 5, 2 Timothy 4:7)

•   The concept of "double"  
	  The author argues that the concept of "double" is used figuratively to refer to great and manifold sins
	  The author suggests that the phrase "double for all her sins" means that the person was receiving double divine favor
	  The author cites various biblical passages to support this interpretation (Isaiah 61:7, Jeremiah 16:18, 17:18, Zechariah 9:12, Revelation 18:6)

•   Jerusalem's warfare  
	  The author argues that Jerusalem's warfare is not yet accomplished
	  The author suggests that the prophecy of Jerusalem's warfare is still future, although it has already received a partial fulfillment
	  The author argues that the whole space from the Lord's first coming in the flesh to his coming in glory is comprehended in this prophecy

•   The visible church and the covenant of righteousness  
	  The author argues that the visible church received the double at the coming or rather at the ascension of the Lord Jesus
	  The author suggests that the covenant of condemnation was exchanged for a covenant of righteousness at this time
	  The author argues that the prophecy looks farther than this, to the ultimate fulfillment of Jerusalem's warfare.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Interpretation  
	  The author disagrees with Luther's strict interpretation of certain passages
	  Luther's interpretation is seen as far-fetched and not supported by the text

•   The Church and Israel  
	  The true church, the church of the first-born, will receive its consummation and bliss
	  National Israel will receive and enjoy its true possession: Canaan and the Temple

•   Reigning Grace  
	  The text is a broad and palpable testimony to reigning grace
	  Sin is requited with superabounding, free favor

•   Faith and Works  
	  Faith is the only means of pleasing God
	  Works prior to conversion do not prepare us for grace, but rather make us worthy of receiving it

•   Cornelius' Conversion  
	  Cornelius was declared clean by God through faith, not by works
	  He was an elect of God, drawn to Christ for salvation

•   Quickening and Conversion  
	  Cornelius was a quickened man, but not yet a converted man
	  He was brought by the Spirit of Christ into a state to receive Him when He was manifested by preaching

•   The Gospel and Faith  
	  The Gospel is the means of salvation, not works
	  Faith is the only means of pleasing God

•   Luther's Argument  
	  Luther argues that Freewill is called 'flesh' in certain passages
	  The author disagrees with Luther's reasoning, arguing that 'flesh' refers to all human beings, not just sinful nature

•   The Nature of Flesh  
	  The author argues that 'flesh' refers to mortality, not sin
	  The context of the passage emphasizes the glory of God, not human sin

•   The Glory of God  
	  The great subject of the prophecy is the glory of Jehovah, which will be revealed
	  God, who is not like man, has spoken it in the word 'grass'

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Interpretation of Scripture  
	  Luther interprets "flesh" in John 3:6 as sinful or wicked
	  The author disagrees with Luther's interpretation, suggesting a more nuanced understanding of "flesh"

•   The Nature of Flesh  
	  The author suggests that "flesh" refers to human nature, including its properties and qualities
	  The Bible provides a history of human creation and depravity, highlighting the nature of flesh

•   Scriptural Testimony  
	  The author cites several passages to demonstrate the nature of flesh, including:
		- Job 15:14-16
		- Psalm 51:5
		- Jeremiah 17:9
		- Mark 7:21-23

•   The Heart as a Source of Evil  
	  The author notes that the Bible teaches that the heart is a source of evil thoughts and actions
	  This is demonstrated in Mark 7:21-23

•   The Concept of Affection  
	  The author defines "affectus" as all that is liable to be moved and affected in man
	  This includes human emotions, appetites, and passions

•   The Concept of Honesty  
	  The author defines "honestum" as something that is honorable and worthy of praise
	  This is opposed to "turpe", which refers to something dishonorable

•   Examples of Honorable Actions  
	  The author cites examples of people who have risked their lives for their country, such as Scaevola and Regulus

•   Luther's Use of the Word "Justify"  
	  Luther uses the word "justify" to mean making someone righteous
	  The author notes that this is not the same meaning as in Romans 4:5, where it refers to being justified by faith

•   Luther's Argument about Flesh  
	  Luther argues that "flesh" is equivalent to being wicked or a member of the devil's kingdom
	  The author disagrees with this interpretation, suggesting that it is a new and distinct argument

•   Further Discussion of the Terms "Flesh" and "Spirit"  
	  The author suggests that a fuller consideration of these terms can be found in Vaughan's "Clergyman's Appeal"

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    Luther's Argument  
	  Luther argues that the praise of the heathen is false
	  Man is "flesh" and "wickedness"
	  Luther's objections are solid and unanswerable
    Regeneration  
	  Regeneration is the fruit and effect of a divine act by the Spirit
	  It makes the soul new and capable of spiritual perceptions, actions, and sufferings
	  Regeneration can be understood in two senses: as a character or as a state (new birth)
    Baptism  
	  Baptism is the sign and seal of the regenerate state
	  It is the sacrament of the Resurrection, signifying our union with Christ in his death and resurrection
	  Baptism is administered to God's elect, not to the non-elect
    Infant Baptism  
	  Infant baptism must stand on its own grounds of vindication
	  Luther is content with God's command to circumcise every male Israelite on the eighth day, even if it is administered to non-elect
    The Meaning of the Ordinance  
	  The meaning of the ordinance is not impaired by mysterious arrangements
	  It is a shame, grief, and weakness to the spiritual man if he does not use and enjoy the pregnant sign
    The Relationship between Regeneration and Baptism  
	  Regeneration and baptism are closely related, but not identical
	  Baptism is the sacramental introduction of the Lord's people into the resurrection state
    The Sacraments  
	  Baptism and the communion of the body and blood are the Lord's sacraments
	  They are the sacramental introduction and continuance of the Lord's people in the resurrection state
    The Phrase "Renatus per fidem"  
	  The phrase "renatus per fidem" is allowable as expressive of the state into which the eternally foreknown of God are brought
	  It refers to the regeneration of the elect in state, after they have already been regenerated in Spirit by faith and calling upon God
    Objections to Luther's Testimony  
	  Many objections will rise up against Luther's testimony on baptism
	  Why are infants baptized? Why is baptism administered to the non-elect?
    Luther's Confidence in God's Arrangements  
	  Luther is confident in God's arrangements, even if they are mysterious
	  He is content to use and enjoy the pregnant sign of baptism, even if it is administered to non-elect.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's View of Man's Nature  
	  Luther believes that man is born of the Spirit and has the Spirit dwelling in him.
	  This means that man serves God through the Spirit.

•   The Remainder (Secundum Reliquias)  
	  Luther refers to the "remainder" as the unrenewed part of man's nature.
	  This part is not renewed until the resurrection, but the Spirit within man is energized and varies in its effects.

•   Luther's Defense of His Interpretation  
	  Luther defends his interpretation of Isaiah 40:6-7 against Jerome and Erasmus.
	  He argues that his interpretation is consistent with the Bible and is not ridiculous.

•   The Concept of Plenipotentiary  
	  A plenipotentiary is someone who has full power to represent a government or God's word.
	  Luther uses this concept to describe the relationship between God and man.

•   The Distinction between Divine and Created Events  
	  Luther distinguishes between events that are conversant with creatures and events that are conversant with God.
	  He argues that spiritual things are higher than temporal things, but both are under God's dominion.

•   Luther's Order of Argumentation  
	  Luther's order of argumentation is to:
		- Repel Diatribe's gloss
		- Show the folly and inconsistency of it
		- Confound Diatribe's confusion

•   The Concept of Coram Deo  
	  Coram Deo refers to the distinction between divine and created events.
	  Luther argues that God leaves us at liberty in some acts.

•   The Concept of Sui Juris  
	  Sui juris refers to the law or rule that a person prescribes to themselves for the regulation of their conduct.
	  Luther uses this concept to describe the relationship between God and man.

•   Luther's Defense of His Quotations  
	  Luther defends his quotations from Proverbs against Erasmus' objections.
	  He argues that necessity does not preclude human agency, but quickens it.

•   Luther's Style and Language  
	  Luther is accused of being magniloquent (pompous or bombastic) in his language.
	  He is compared to Thersites, a common soldier in Homer's Iliad.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Interpretation of Scripture  
	  Luther believes Scripture is authority, but it will not use it.
	  He uses the Fathers as authority, despite not considering them authoritative.

•   Corruptis and Falacibus  
	  The text describes the state of the receiver as corrupt and the false prophets as wilful.
	  The devil is compared to earthly princes, with a hint of ambiguity.

•   Unoverbulo  
	  Luther's force is in his interpretation of the word "nothing".
	  He believes that the devil's existence is a real and positive one.

•   Longepotentiss et callidiss mundi  
	  Luther compares the devil to earthly princes, emphasizing his power and cunning.
	  The devil's existence is seen as a real and positive one.

•   Fortiter contemnit  
	  The text describes Diatribe as having a good deal of discretion.
	  Luther believes that Diatribe's argument is weak and lacks substance.

•   John 15:5  
	  Luther references John 15:5, where Jesus says "I am the vine, you are the branches".
	  He believes that the parable illustrates the importance of union with Christ.

•   Interpreting Parables  
	  Luther believes that parables should be interpreted broadly, rather than focusing on specific details.
	  He considers the parable of the vine and branches to represent the visible church.

•   Nothingness  
	  Luther believes that the "nothingness" spoken of in the parable refers to the absence of union with Christ.
	  He argues that without this union, fruitfulness would cease entirely.

•   Peromnia et omnibus modis  
	  Diatribe's argument is seen as weak and lacking substance.
	  Her arguments would not prove her point even if they were sound.

•   Coram Deo  
	  Luther believes that the phrase "coram Deo" refers to God's presence as an agent.
	  He argues that the effect of an action is nothing in the sight of God, but the act itself is not.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   God's Creation  
	  According to an eternal draught or model in God's mind, the world was created in his appointed time.
	  Leucippus of Abdera (428 BC) is credited with inventing the system of atoms and void, later explained by Democritus and Epicurus.

•   Pre-Existing State of the World  
	  Plato's chaos, Leucippus' vacuum, and Aristotle's infinite are similar concepts for a pre-existing state of the world.
	  These concepts are discussed in more detail in Part II, Sect. 5, note u, and Part IV, Sect. 8, note r.

•   Detriverunt  
	  A figure taken from threshing or treading out grain with the feet.
	  Possibly a swipe at the name of Diatribe in Luther's use of the term.

•   Luther's Argument  
	  Luther maintains his Achillean lance by:
		- Exposing the staleness, unaptness, and unauthorizedness of Diatribe's evasion.
		- Highlighting the dangerous conclusions that may be extorted from her concessions.
		- Arguing that the concept of Freewill is impossible to realize.

•   Enumerat  
	  Implies "thenumber in full tale" or an ostentatious display of numbers.

•   Comparison  
	  There is a double failure in the comparison: the works are two, and the agent in each is one.

•   Classical Allusions  
	  Luther does not find any classical allusions for the gourd.

•   Renovata Creatura  
	  Sometimes called "thenew creation"; but with less propriety.
	  The new creation is all made out of the old, which "new" does not imply, but "renewed" does.

•   Cooperare Mur  
	  The cooperation in both cases consists in our acting concurrently with God, according to our nature.
	  God calls out our faculties, whether natural or renewed, into act and exercise.

•   Publicet Traducere  
	  A peculiar use of "traduc", meaning to expose or ridicule or dishonor.
	  Examples of this usage include Juvenal and Martial.
