Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's interpretation of Romans 1:20  
	  Luther believes that the Gentiles are without excuse for their sins because they have knowledge of God's power and Godhead.
	  He assumes that the Gentiles have a similar understanding of God as the Jews, but this is not necessarily the case.

•   The conduct of the Gentiles  
	  Luther argues that the Gentiles have sinned without excuse, and that their idolatry and sin are a result of their rejection of God.
	  He believes that the Gentiles have a similar understanding of God as the Jews, but this is not necessarily the case.

•   The testimony against Freewill  
	  Luther argues that the Gentiles do not have salvation by Freewill, but rather by faith.
	  He believes that righteousness is not known without faith, and that faith is not of Freewill but opposed to it.

•   The wrath of God  
	  Luther believes that the wrath of God is revealed against all men in their nature state.
	  He argues that men manifest themselves to be children of wrath and curse through original sin and guilt.

•   Luther's quotation of Romans 1:21-23  
	  Luther does not quote the words in the order they appear in the original text.
	  He appears to have misunderstood the distinction between the wise and the rest of the nation.

•   The form of the text  
	  The form of the text is ambiguous, and could be interpreted in different ways.
	  Luther's interpretation is not necessarily the most grammatically correct or contextually supported.

•   The Jews and their observance of the law  
	  Luther believes that the Jews only yielded an outward observance to the law, and did not really fulfill its requirements.
	  He argues that the real Jew, the spiritual Israelite, was enlightened by the Holy Ghost to see and understand Christ by faith.

•   The law and faith  
	  Luther confounds the law and gospel, and believes that the law is not of faith but of morality.
	  He argues that the law is not a means of salvation, but rather a means of making men superabounding transgressors.

•   The Gentiles and the law  
	  Luther believes that the Gentiles are transgressors of the law, not because they lack faith in Christ, but because they lack spiritual obedience to its requirements.
	  He argues that the Gentiles are no better than the uncovenanted Gentile, and that they are both under the wrath of God.

•   Luther's view of the Apostle's argument  
	  Luther believes that the Apostle is showing that the law-having Jew is no better than the uncovenanted Gentile.
	  He argues that the Apostle is demonstrating that the Gentiles are under the wrath of God, and that they need salvation by faith.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Romans 2:25  
	  The verse states that if you break the law, your circumcision is made uncircumcision.
	  This is a reference to the Jewish practice of circumcision.

•   Etymology of "proait"  
	  The word "proait" is often translated as "before" or "in advance".
	  However, Luther argues that it means "on account of" or "because of".
	  The correct interpretation is supported by the etymology of the word and the surrounding discourse.

•   Paul's argument  
	  Paul argues that the abandonment of God by the Jews proves what they are with respect to God.
	  This is a reference to the Jewish people's rejection of God and their subsequent punishment.

•   The universality of the charge  
	  Paul's argument is not limited to the Jews, but is meant for all people.
	  The charge of guilt is addressed to those who are under the law, regardless of their nationality.

•   The law was given to the Jews  
	  The law was given to the Jewish people, not to the Gentiles (non-Jews).
	  This is a key point in Paul's argument, as he distinguishes between the two groups.

•   The elect and covenanted nation of Israel  
	  The Jewish people were the elect and covenanted nation of Israel.
	  They were the visible church during a certain period of time, and were the type of the true church.

•   The Apostle's distinction  
	  Paul distinguishes between the two parts of mankind: Jews and Gentiles.
	  He uses the law as a badge to distinguish between the two groups.

•   The objection to Luther's interpretation  
	  The objection is that Luther's interpretation is too narrow, and only applies to the Jews.
	  However, Paul's argument is meant for all people, not just the Jews.

•   The universality of the charge (continued)  
	  Paul's argument is not affected by the distinction between Jews and Gentiles.
	  The whole world is declared guilty, which is all Paul wants to establish.

•   The meaning of "obnoxius"  
	  The word "obnoxius" means "liable to charge" or "one actually arraigned".
	  It is used to describe those who are guilty and liable to punishment.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Misunderstanding  
	  Luther misinterprets Paul's condemnation of the law
	  He thinks the works of the law are evil, but Paul says they are not evil in themselves
	  Paul's condemnation is based on the fact that people do not truly perform the works of the law

•   The Meaning of "Flesh"  
	  Luther incorrectly thinks "flesh" refers to sinful affection
	  In reality, "flesh" is a term for the human species, meaning "no human being"

•   The Argument Against Luther  
	  If the deeds of the law are good, but man and free will are condemned by them, what is man and what is free will?
	  Luther's argument is not shaken by this counterpoint

•   The Distinction Between Jews and Gentiles  
	  Luther tries to apply his argument to both Jews and Gentiles, but they did not have the law in the same form
	  The Jew had the ceremonial law, which the Gentile did not have

•   Paul's Argument  
	  Paul speaks of ceremonial works exclusively, but Luther argues that this is not the case
	  Luther's counterpoints are addressed by Paul's argument that the whole law, including the moral law, is one institution

•   Luther's Errors  
	  Luther goes wide and says many exceptionable things
	  He misapprehends Paul's meaning and says unwise and untrue things

•   The Law and Its Requirements  
	  The law has not been abrogated, but its requirements are still in force
	  The ceremonial law was a part of the law's requirements, and it has not been abrogated

•   Paul's Interpretation of Moses  
	  Paul's interpretation of Moses' curse is different from Luther's
	  Paul's curse is not on those who fulfill the law, but on those who attempt to fulfill it without the Spirit

•   The Spirit and the Law  
	  The Spirit is not the lawful fulfiller, but rather the one who enables us to fulfill the law
	  The Spirit is not required for the law to be in force, but rather for us to fulfill it

•   The Aim of the Law  
	  The aim of the law is not to justify us, but to condemn us if we do not fulfill it
	  The Spirit's aim is to do the whole will of God, not to continue in all things

•   The Relationship Between the Law and the Spirit  
	  The Spirit is not the law's fulfiller, but rather the one who enables us to fulfill the law
	  The Spirit's aim is to do the whole will of God, not to continue in all things.

•   Luther's interpretation of Galatians 3:21

	  The Spirit justifies the Lord's called people, but this is distinct from any act of obedience that removes the curse or earns acceptance

	  However, the author argues that the deeds of the law are damnable regardless of the presence or absence of the Spirit

	  The Apostle is arguing against justification by the law, not against man's natural impotency and imbecility
	  The argument is preparatory to the opening of justification by the Gospel

•   The meaning of "justified"  
	  Luther reads the word "justified" in the present tense, but the author argues that the future tense is more likely
	  The word "justified" implies increased or perfected knowledge, not acknowledgment

•   The law and sin  
	  The law shows what is sinful to a greater extent, but also exacerbates and excites sin by forbidding and requiring
	  The law reveals the malignity or "exceeding sinfulness" of man's nature

	  Freewill is condemned, even with the help of the law, because it cannot justify
	  Freewill is impotent and ignorant of what is sinful and what is not

	  The law continues and manifests the damnation that had already been incurred before the law came

	  The argument is that Freewill is condemned, even with the help of the law, because it cannot justify
	  The argument is that Freewill is impotent and ignorant of what is sinful and what is not

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Nature of Sin  
	  Sin was present in the world from the beginning
	  Death passed on to all through Adam, the first man
	  Sin was not imputed when there was no law

•   The Law and Sin  
	  The law was introduced to multiply transgressions
	  The law gave the opportunity for more offenses to be added to the first
	  The law had no effect in multiplying transgressions, but rather in revealing sin

•   The Purpose of the Law  
	  The law was not to communicate the knowledge of sin, but to multiply transgressions
	  The law was given to show God's love and mercy to His people
	  The law was not to condemn, but to reveal the need for grace and mercy

•   The Imputation of Sin  
	  Sin has never been imputed by God to man, except through express and absolute enactment
	  The command in the garden was an example of this
	  The law given to the seed of Abraham was also an example of this

•   The Role of Faith  
	  Faith implies repentance and is necessary for salvation
	  The one who believes shall be saved, and the one who does not believe shall perish
	  Faith is not a work of Freewill, but a gift of God

•   The Nature of Freewill  
	  Freewill is not the source of faith, but rather a power exercised distinct from faith
	  The Freewill man is not righteous before God, but rather a sinner
	  The act of Freewill is an act of sin, not righteousness

•   The Manifestation of Righteousness  
	  The righteous are manifested to be such by the Spirit's acting upon and within them
	  They are enabled and constrained to believe, and thus conform to God's will
	  Their personal actings are accounted as righteous acts, not because of Freewill, but because of faith

•   The Glory of God  
	  Luther's account of the glory of God is excessive and erroneous
	  The glory of God is revealed in the manifestation of righteousness in believers

•   The Covenant and Dispensation  
	  Luther did not thoroughly apprehend the nature and design of the interposed covenant and dispensation
	  The covenant and dispensation were given to teach sin and the need for grace and mercy

•   The Justification of Believers  
	  The believer alone is righteous before God
	  The believer's righteousness is not based on Freewill, but on faith in Jesus Christ

    The design of the law covenant

	  He argues that the law was not given to make men righteous, but rather to condemn them and drive them to the Gospel

•   Hypocrisy

	  He compares it to the histrionic art, where an actor puts on a mask to personate a character

	  To criticize severely or explode against something
	  Luther uses this term to describe his criticism of the Pelagians

•   Contention  
	  A figure of speech used by Paul to compare and contrast different concepts
	  Examples include law and faith, God's righteousness and human righteousness, life and death

•   Solam gratiam  
	  Justification by faith alone, without any personal worth or merit
	  Luther argues that this is the correct understanding of justification

	  Luther argues that Abraham's justification is not based on his works, but on faith

•   Reputation gratiae

	  Luther uses this term interchangeably with "imputo" to describe how God imputes righteousness to believers

•   Renovata creatura per fidem

	  Luther argues that this is a mistaken understanding, as faith is not the source of renewal

	  Luther argues that salvation is based on God's purpose and grace, not human choice

•   Adam's sin  
	  Luther argues that Adam's sin is imputed to humanity through birth
	  He argues that this makes humans voluntary agents in sin, and that God is the propagator of sin

•   Luther's interpretation  
	  Luther's interpretation of Paul's argument in Romans 5:12-19
	  He argues that humans are born guilty and children of wrath, and that they cannot do anything good on their own.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's interpretation of Paul's writings  
	  Luther concludes that Paul condemns free will
	  However, the author argues that Luther misuses his premises to arrive at this conclusion

•   The role of faith in salvation  
	  Faith in Christ is necessary for salvation
	  This faith must be received or obeyed, not through human power, but through the super-natural power of God's Spirit

•   Scriptural evidence  
	  Scripture explicitly testifies to the importance of faith and the power of God's Spirit
	  Examples include passages from Romans and John

•   The opposition between flesh and Spirit  
	  Paul divides people into two classes: those who walk after the flesh and those who walk after the Spirit
	  The Lord in John 3 shows the necessity of a new and spiritual birth, not a parallel between the two classes

•   The concept of "sensus carnis"  
	  "Sensus carnis" refers to the mind in action or the result of that action
	  It is not the mind or desire that is not subject, but the flesh, which is the unrenewed body itself

•   The flesh and the Spirit  
	  The flesh is the substance that forms desires, but it is not subject to God
	  The Spirit, on the other hand, is the power of God that can renew and transform the flesh

•   The text "legi impossibile"  
	  Luther does not adequately explain this difficult text
	  The author provides context and interpretation, highlighting the struggle between the law of sin and the law of the mind

•   The temptation of Paul  
	  Paul was tempted to think he must still obey the law
	  He discovered that he had a law in his members that warred against the law of his mind

•   The role of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit delivers believers from the thraldom and bondage of the law
	  The Holy Spirit is given to believers in Christ, who has executed the sentence of death upon sin

•   The new state of being  
	  Believers are raised up into a new state of being, in which they are subject to spiritual influences
	  This new state is characterized by being renewed and dwelling in the Holy Spirit

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Basis of Reality  
	  The reality of being raised from death is based on being in Christ
	  This is the basis and element of this reality
	  It is the foundation of God's new creation-transactions in Him

•   The Law's Weakness  
	  The law was weak through the flesh's being what it is
	  The law could not fulfill the righteousness of the law
	  The law could not enable people to yield to God as service

•   The Triumph of the Spirit  
	  The body that has death in it and is yet to die shall be quickened by the Spirit
	  This is distinct from the law's obedience
	  The Spirit has already quickened and dwelt in the souls of believers

•   The Victory of Christ  
	  The victory is ascribed to Christ, who gives us the victory
	  This is the basis of the triumph of the Spirit
	  The victory is realized through our Lord Jesus Christ

•   No Condemnation  
	  There is no condemnation to those who are in Christ Jesus
	  This is because the law of sin and death is clearly the law of evil
	  The law of sin and death is in the members, or flesh, or body

•   The People of God  
	  The people of God are said to be in Christ Jesus
	  This refers to two distinct states:
		- In Him by covenant and predestinative union from before the world
		- In Him by realized, conscious, and efficacious union, through the calling of the Holy Ghost

•   Sacramental Union  
	  A third state may be distinguished as that of sacramental union
	  This is distinct and separable from the other two states
	  It is like the entrance of the Lord into His kingdom, by baptism

•   The Blessedness of the Called  
	  The blessedness described belongs to His called
	  It is the ordained, earned, and waiting portion of all His elect
	  Those who are brought by the Holy Ghost into the knowledge of this grace live and walk after that part of their frame which lives

•   The Lord's Called  
	  The Lord bears testimony to His called
	  This testimony is realized for all His elect in His time
	  God has condemned their sin which is in their flesh

•   The Law of Sin and Death  
	  The law of sin and death is clearly the law of evil
	  It is in the members, or flesh, or body
	  The law of sin and death is the impossibility of the law, which the law gave no power to obey

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

    The concept of dikaiosunh (righteousness)  
	  Refers to habitual conduct, aim, and principle
	  Conformity with the law is circuitous, not direct; incidental, not deliberate
    Walking in the Spirit  
	  Means living in accordance with the Holy Spirit
	  Contrasts with living after the flesh (Gal 5:16)
    The relationship between the Spirit and the flesh  
	  The flesh is the natural, unrenewed substance of man
	  The flesh is inimical to God (Rom 8:7)
    Luther's interpretation of Romans 8  
	  Luther believes that those who live after the flesh are the same as the flesh
	  The author disagrees, arguing that the flesh and the man are distinct substances
    The role of the Holy Spirit in the Christian life  
	  The Holy Spirit renews the mind and enables good works
	  The Holy Spirit is the source of all good works
    The relationship between faith and works  
	  Faith is the acceptable principle, without which there is no acceptance
	  Freewill has no faith, and therefore does nothing acceptable
    The concept of sin  
	  Whatever is not of faith is sin (Rom 14:23)
	  Freewill, without faith, does only sin
    The application of Romans 8:8  
	  Luther applies the text to the eternal state of the justified person
	  The author argues that the text actually refers to the state of one who doubts their own righteousness
    The relationship between doubt and sin  
	  Doubt about one's own righteousness is sin
	  Freewill may have no doubts, yet still be damned
    The role of freewill in the Christian life  
	  Freewill has no faith, and therefore does nothing acceptable
	  Freewill is only sin, unless it is renewed by the Holy Spirit

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's interpretation of Paul's writings  
	  Luther's interpretation of Paul's writings is not necessarily correct
	  The texts, when properly understood, do not support Luther's principles

•   Sublapsarianism  
	  Refers to the idea that the fall of man was a consequence of Adam's sin
	  Luther does not explicitly endorse this view

•   Peculiaris  
	  Refers to the idea that Paul's writings are unique and special
	  Luther believes that Paul's writings are not confined to John, but also include other apostles

•   Paul's relationship to the world  
	  Luther believes that Paul was in the world, but not of it
	  The contrast is between the world at large and Paul's peculiarly connected ones (the Jews)

•   Translatum in spiritum  
	  Refers to the idea that we are transferred from the world into the Spirit through Christ
	  Luther opposes Christ to the world, making Christ the Spirit in contrast to Adam, the flesh

•   The will of the flesh and the will of man  
	  Luther believes that these terms exclude everything belonging to the human will from the generative power
	  He understands "bloods" to express natural birth, and "will of the flesh" for personal and individual will

•   Regeneration and faith  
	  Luther believes that we must be begotten again before we can believe
	  Faith is not the cause of regeneration, but rather the manifestation of it

•   Pro gratia Christi  
	  Luther believes that grace is given to us in return for Christ's grace
	  He understands this as parallel to Romans 5, where one degree of grace is given for another

•   Luther's understanding of grace  
	  Luther believes that the grace we have from Christ is actually given to us by the Father
	  This grace comes to us and is conferred upon us as a consequence of Christ's actions

•   Luther's interpretation of Romans 5  
	  Luther believes that the passage in Romans 5 is about the relationship between Christ's grace and our own
	  He understands this as a parallel to the idea of grace being given to us in return for Christ's grace

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Condignity and Congruity  
	  Condignity refers to the quality of deserving something
	  Congruity refers to the quality of being suitable
	  Both terms are related to the concept of inherent worthiness

•   Meritum Condignum  
	  Meritum condignum means "worthy merit" or "merit worthy of the reward"
	  It refers to the idea that some merits are worthy of a reward

•   The Gospel Mystery  
	  The Gospel mystery is a matter of revelation, not discovery by natural reason
	  God has revealed hints and traces of this mystery to all nations from the beginning
	  The kingdom of God was announced immediately after the fall, in the denunciation of the serpent

•   Pronobisexaltatum  
	  Pronobisexaltatum is a word of doubtful meaning, possibly referring to being exalted at the Father's right hand
	  Luther understands it as an allusion to Jesus' words in John 12:32, "And I, if I be lifted up."

•   Necessitas Cere  
	  Necessitas cere is a phrase that means "a sort of oxymoron" or "concordia discors"
	  It refers to the idea that free will should be silent and give glory to God

•   Extra  
	  The word "extra" is used to denote distinctness or separation
	  There are only two sorts of substances: to be without one is to be within the other

•   Luther's Argument  
	  Luther argues that Scripture speaks by way of comparison
	  If Scripture does not speak by comparison, then Scripture is void, Christ is denied, and God is unjust

•   Jam Judicat Ust  
	  Jam judicat ust means "already judged" or "already condemned"
	  It refers to the idea that a person's judgment is already determined by their rejection or acceptance of Christ

•   Persynecdoche  
	  Persynecdoche is a figure of speech where a part is taken for the whole or the whole for a part
	  Luther uses this figure of speech to refer to the whole of man as being put for his grosser part

•   Hic Dicit  
	  Hic dicit means "that is, according to Luther"
	  Luther argues that it is God's will that is done, not man's, and that John is accounting for the superior honor paid to Jesus

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   The Meaning of "From Beneath"  
	  The phrase "from beneath" is used by Jesus to describe the origin of the Jews, who are considered the devil's seed.
	  This is in contrast to Jesus, who is from heaven.

•   John the Baptist's Testimony  
	  John the Baptist testifies that Jesus comes from heaven, unlike himself.
	  John's testimony is not about his own birth or origin, but about Jesus' divine origin.

•   Luther's Misunderstanding  
	  Martin Luther misunderstood the text and did not see its glory.
	  He also did not elicit its testimony against free will correctly.

•   The Meaning of "Earthly" and "Heavenly"  
	  The terms "earthly" and "heavenly" do not refer to Christ's people and the world, but to Christ and John individually.
	  John was a man in his natural frame, just like other men, but Christ's human person was from heaven.

•   The Lord's Meaning  
	  The Lord means more by "from beneath" than John the Baptist did.
	  The Lord is speaking of the Jews as the devil's seed, whose throne and habitation are beneath the earth.

•   The Lord's Testimony  
	  The Lord's testimony is to the best and finer part of the Jews, not to their grosser part.
	  The Lord is accounting for their murmurs and rejection of him.

•   The Power of the Holy Spirit  
	  The Holy Spirit's illumination is not about enlightening a man's own soul, but about throwing light upon Christ.
	  This is a beautiful and accurate description of the Holy Spirit's violence with which the soul is converted.

•   Luther's Testimonies  
	  Luther's testimonies are clear and strong, but not as effective as this single text.
	  The question is whether they bear on the subject, how they exactly bear on it, and with what degree of effect.

•   The Importance of This Text  
	  This text is a single testimony that is worth a hundred abstruse and obscure ones.
	  It is a question of whether they bear at all on the subject, how they exactly bear on it, and with what degree of effect.

•   The Impression of Luther's Testimonies  
	  The impression of Luther's testimonies is weakened by being extended.
	  Many small blows, of which one or two beat the air, render the victory doubtful in the sight of the bystanders.

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's View of Free Will  
	  Luther believed that free will has no role in salvation
	  He argued that Scripture preaches Christ by antithesis, excluding free will
	  Christ is preached everywhere, and therefore free will is opposed everywhere

•   Scripture and History  
	  Luther's view of the kingdom of God is not fully realized or applied
	  This is due to a lack of understanding of the origin, design, and operation of the kingdom
	  The kingdom is not just a creation-state, but a counsel and scheme of God

•   The Flesh and Spirit  
	  Luther's representation of the flesh and spirit is not in line with the author's view
	  The author considers the flesh and spirit to be the unrenewed body and renewed mind of God's people

•   The Conflict of the Renewed Man  
	  The renewed man, who has the Spirit, has a conflict to maintain
	  The author asks what the wholly unrenewed man is like before God and what his endeavor after good is

•   Laborare  
	  The allusion is to 1 Corinthians 9:26, where Paul says "I run"
	  Luther uses the word "laborare" to mean "to strive" or "to run"

•   Justitiariorum  
	  The word "justitiariorum" is not found in Latin, but is used as a bad Latin for "justice"
	  The author interprets it to mean "persons who are going about to establish their own righteousness"

•   Oudeiv  
	  The word "oudeiv" implies no person, whether man or devil
	  It is used to emphasize that the conflict is not just between humans, but also between humans and God

•   Defects in Luther's Theology  
	  Luther's theology is defective in this paragraph
	  He gives quietness, but not triumph; quietness too, without reason
	  He did not see Christ's peculiar and peculiarizing headship

•   Christ's Headship  
	  Christ's headship is not just a general concept, but a specific and peculiar one
	  He is the one who enables God to raise up the cursed from their curse after suffering a part of it
	  He lives in them, even now, as though they had risen with him

•   The Merit of Acceptance  
	  The merit of acceptance has been wrought already, to the uttermost, by Christ alone
	  The people of God have only to enter into and enjoy their portion, which is a mixed one here, but an unmixed one hereafter

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Arrogate  : 
  • To demand something as one's due or property
  • To assert one's right or title to something

•   Adconsolandum  : 
  • An odd expression meaning to console the spirit tempted to see with an evil eye
  • An evil eye is one that is unsound or infected with envy, malice, and blasphemy

•   The Ungodly Prosper  : 
  • The Bible says "the tabernacles of robbers prosper" (Job 12:6, Psalm 73:12)
  • This refers to the ungodly who prosper in the world and increase in riches

•   Luther's Difficulty  : 
  • Luther struggles to reconcile the condemnation of the reprobate with God's justice
  • He acknowledges he cannot reconcile it and makes unwarrantable concessions

•   Creation and Fall of Man  : 
  • Luther's imperfect conception of creation and fall of man leads to his difficulty
  • He thinks every individual has a distinct personal subsistence given to them in creation

•   God's Justice  : 
  • Luther asks where the injustice is in God bringing out each individual into manifest existence
  • He argues that God has given them the opportunity to show what they are, according to their own making

•   Original Sin and Guilt  : 
  • The union and unity of each individual with Adam constitutes their original sin and guilt
  • This is the source of their loss of creation state and character

•   God's Loving Kindness  : 
  • The only question that can be asked in this mystery is about God's loving kindness
  • Paul puts the difficulty and calls for submission, assigning the principle of the procedure

•   God's Right as Potter  : 
  • God has exercised his right as potter and has done it for a great and wise reason
  • The man whose eyes the Lord has opened will see and search into these things and will justify

•   Justice and Fulfillment of Relations  : 
  • Justice is the fulfillment of relations; God had fulfilled all His relations when man incurred his fore-announced curse
  • What justice requires is that it be exacted, and God has a right to institute relations as He did between Himself and the human race in Adam

Here are 10 bullets with sub-bullets summarizing the text:

•   Luther's Criticism of God's Ways  
	  Luther uses God's actions to condemn him, but God's ways are not unjust.
	  The elect are not crowned sinners, and the union of the elect with Christ explains both dooms.

•   Pliny's Views on Nature  
	  Pliny the Elder and his nephew saw nature as matter, not as having a spiritual significance.
	  They were amiable men, but their views on nature were limited.

•   Demosthenes and Cicero  
	  Demosthenes was abandoned by his countrymen and died in exile.
	  Cicero was delivered up to his enemies and died in exile.

•   Luther's Illustration  
	  Luther's illustration of God's justice is unnecessary and untrue.
	  The spiritual man can see God's justice without Luther's illustration.

•   Pertinacity  
	  Pertinacity refers to persistent determination to hear the truth.
	  It is a quality that is necessary for spiritual growth.

•   Original Sin  
	  Luther views original sin as a vitiation of nature, not as the first sin.
	  The argument against free will is not affected by this view.

•   Additional Considerations  
	  God's foreknowledge and predestination are relevant to the discussion of original sin.
	  Satan's lordship over the world and the case of the apostate and rejected Jews are also relevant.

•   Satan's Ingratitude  
	  Satan's ingratitude is a result of his instrumentality in the world.
	  God could have cured Satan's ingratitude, but it is necessary for God's glory.

•   The Lord's Dealings with His Church  
	  The Lord's real and designed glory requires that the time of ingratitude should be.
	  This view reconciles us to what might otherwise be a constant burden and distress.

•   Erasmus's Views  
	  Erasmus was merely talking about the origin, nature, and design of evil, rather than asserting they are categorically true.
